Katalog Berita (Halaman 23/57)

#6824. Princess Kate Says Nature Helped Her Heal After Her Cancer Diagnosis

“People have long sought comfort in the natural world”

It’s not very often that Princess Catherine sits down for a traditional magazine interview, but in a new story published today in Country Life, the Princess of Wales got candid about her recovery following her 2024 cancer diagnosis and subsequent chemotherapy treatment (she went into remission early last year).

“I was told in hospital that my recovery would not be linear,” Kate says. “[That] there will be good days and bad days, that it will take time.”

She adds: “What is interesting is to see the science now back up what we instinctively know and feel. Humans have always turned to nature. Whether it is the beach for healing and recovery, the countryside for peace and perspective or the mountains to find solitude, people have long sought comfort in the natural world. It is such a restorative environment and incorporating nature into someone’s recovery pathway is vital.”

This past June, Kate completed the National Three Peaks Challenge, a feat that involves climbing the three highest peaks in the United Kingdom in a matter of only 24 hours. Part of the reason she took on the challenge was to highlight “the deeper impact of serious illness and the importance of holistic healthcare.”

The royal has also been teaming up with organizations that focus on outdoor activity and mental health, like Mind Over Mountains and the Dose of Nature charity.

In the wake of these events, interviewer Robert Macfarlane spoke with Kate about how nature tied to her recovery process. Of her “good days and bad days,” Kate noted that nature demonstrates the same ebb and flow. She said, “It shows us that periods of rest, reflection, and renewal are equally as important as moments of growth.”

Kate also spoke about her longstanding love of the outdoors. “I remember from a very early age spending a lot of time in nature, playing in streams, walking in the countryside, blackberry picking or simply time alone in my own world, enjoying the simplicity of ordinary things, such as digging under the laurel bush for what I thought was treasure,” she said. “There was always a sense of freedom I felt in nature, something about being outdoors that is hard to find elsewhere.”

She went on to note how, as adults, we often lose this sense of wonderment, and she hopes to instill the same fascination with the natural world in her three kids: Prince George, 13, Princess Charlotte, 11, and Prince Louis, 8. “We are often so busy we simply forget to pause, slow down and notice the detail in life,” Kate said. “It is something I remind myself to do with my own children; to give them freedom to explore, rather than constantly telling them what to see or what to think.”

#6823. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

#6819. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. #6816. Everything to Know About Nicholas Hoult’s Wife, Bryana Holly

They just made their red carpet debut at the premiere of Nosferatu.

Nicholas Hoult’s latest flick How to Rob a Bank is about to hit theaters, which means the actor is in the thick of a press tour for the project and making rounds on red carpets.

He made a stop at Jake Shane’s podcast, Therapuss, during his press tour on November 11 and made rare comments about his wife—yes, wife—Bryana Holly.

I know what you’re thinking: How long has it been since Nicholas Hoult and Jennifer Lawrence called it quits, and when did he start dating Bryana? Ahead, find everything we know about Nicholas Hoult’s wife, Bryana Holly.

Bryana is signed to Wilhemina Models and occasionally poses for lingerie, swimsuit, and clothing brands. She also starred in Hoodie Allen’s music video for “Movie” in 2015:

If Bryana looks a little familiar, you may recognize her from her past relationships. The model was romantically linked to Brody Jenner in 2013 before she dated 5 Seconds of Summer drummer Ashton Irwin from 2015 to 2016.

The pair has been together since 2017, and in 2024, Nicholas confirmed they secretly tied the knot.

At the Governors Awards in November 2024, Nicholas called Bryana his “wife” while talking to E! News. “Afterwards, I remember I had my wife’s nails imprinted on my hand,” he said of her reaction to Nosferatu.

A few days later, he confirmed they were married to People, saying, “I feel like it’s fairly clear from my comments.”

Remember that Therapuss interview I mentioned earlier? Well, Nicholas shared the story of how he met his wife in 2016.

“I was shooting a movie,” he said during his convo with Jake. “We were kind of filming and shooting a Halloween party at the same time. And then we had actually a really fun…it was kind of an overcommitment first date.”

He shared that he and Bryana started chatting about their music taste and decided to go to the Desert Trip music festival (which he claimed is “affectionately known” as “Oldchella). They apparently bonded over listening to artists like The Rolling Stones, Paul McCartney, Bob Dylan, and The Who.

“We didn't really know each other, but we both wanted to go,” he explained. “We went to dinner once before just to be like, ‘Let’s make sure neither of us are complete lunatics.’”

Nicholas brought Bryana as his date to the Nosferatu premiere in L.A. back in 2024. And, if you were wondering, this marks their first red carpet appearance after being together for seven years.

Bryana and Nicholas welcomed their first child, Joaquin, in 2018. He’s been seen out and about with the Skins alum and has even made a rare appearance on Bryana’s Instagram (which is now private, womp).

The couple then quietly welcomed their second child in 2022, and according to Nicholas’ How to Rob a Bank co-star, Pete Davidson, they also gave birth to twins at some point.

“He’s a great dad, father of four,” Pete told Entertainment Tonight. “Four under what? 7 [years old] ... which is nuts.”

He also praised Nicholas for how much he catered to his family while filming.

“Elsie was pregnant while we were filming and he just had his twins,” Pete continued. “He would fly back every weekend to go see his family, and that was very inspiring and awesome.”

#6813. What Does the Winner of ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Get, Exactly?

It’s a very different situation for the pro vs. the celeb.

Dancing With the Stars is wrapping up season 35 and someone is about to walk away with a glittery mirrorball trophy and a bunch of money. Actually several someones are going to walk away with a bunch of money, because Dancing With the Stars shares the love between multiple contestants when it comes to winnings. If you have questions, we have answers, so let’s get to it.

Nope. The winner of Dancing With the Stars doesn’t get extra money for winning—they just get the Mirrorball Trophy. However, they do get financially compensated for their time on the show—within an established payment structure.

The cast makes money for each week they remain on the show. According to Variety, everyone takes home $125,000 for the rehearsal period, as well as the first two weeks DWTS airs—meaning, even if you’re voted off you still get paid. The remaining celebs then get bonuses each week they continue.

A while back, there were reports (via Variety) that contestants got an additional $10,000 for weeks three and four, an additional $20,000 for weeks five and six, an additional $30,000 for weeks seven and eight, and then another $50,000 for weeks nine and ten.

This means that all the final celebs who make it to the Dancing With the Stars finale earn $295,000, regardless of whether or not they win.

Pros earn a per-episode salary. We have a full breakdown this week, but according to Us Weekly the highest-paid dancers (aka the pros who have been around for the longest) make around $5,000 an episode.

But there’s no extra baked-in “prize” for the pros if they secure a win, though there is a bonus. Jenna Johnson explained how it works to The Morning After podcast, saying “Whoever makes it to the finale, you all get paid up until then. And you do get a nice bonus. If you win, it’s not like you get a million dollars. You’re just getting a cute trophy together.”

Jenna also confirmed that pros who are voted out earlier are “guaranteed until a certain amount of weeks” pay. Which is something Brandon Armstrong’s wife, Brylee, confirmed on TikTok last year, saying “Brandon is still very much a pro on the show. Just because his partner was eliminated doesn’t mean that he just gets the boot and he’s gotta pack his bags and go all the way back to Utah.” Brylee added that “Dancing with the Stars takes care of their people, so no need to worry.”

#6811. Survey Identifies Data Governance Enforcement Gap for Agentic AI Tools

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time. Perhaps more worrying is that only 19% said their organization could detect in real time when an agent last accessed data outside its intended scope.

That disconnect is at the center of Delinea's fall 2026 Identity Security Report, "The AI Enforcement Gap," which suggests companies have made quick progress establishing AI governance but have not necessarily built the technical controls needed to enforce it.

The findings come from a pair of surveys covering 2,254 IT and security leaders and 2,250 non-IT employees at organizations with at least 500 employees across the U.S, U.K, Germany, Australia, Singapore, UAE, France, and India.

All the organizations were using or piloting AI, while the employees surveyed used at least one AI tool for work.

The problem becomes more consequential as companies move from AI tools that primarily respond to users toward agents capable of taking actions on their behalf.

Eighty-seven percent of IT and security respondents said their organization had experienced or suspected of an incident during the past year in which an AI tool or agent accessed sensitive information beyond what was required for its task.

Delinea's research also suggests companies are giving agents access that can persist longer than necessary. Only 58% of IT and security leaders said they have mechanisms that automatically revoke or expire AI access when a session ends, and those controls do not necessarily cover every agent or tool. Forty-two percent said many agent credentials remain active until an audit.

Agents also are not always receiving their own narrowly defined permissions. Half of IT leaders said their organizations use the employee's existing permissions as a boundary for what an agent can access. That can effectively hand an agent years of accumulated access belonging to the person who launched it.

That is particularly significant because an AI agent does not necessarily behave like a traditional service account. A service account typically performs a predefined process, while an agent can choose different tools, actions and sequences at runtime depending on the situation.

"Service accounts run fixed scripts," said Mike Albrecht, associate director in the Risk Advisory Practice at Cross Country Consulting. "An AI agent decides what actions it's going to take at runtime."

The enforcement problem is not limited to autonomous agents.

The researchers found that 76% of employees surveyed had bypassed formal approval at some point to use AI tools with company data, applications or systems. And 48% said they always or extensively use AI tools without going through formal approval.

Meanwhile, 60% said they had felt pressure to use AI with sensitive or confidential information even when they were not sure doing so was permitted.

Executives were particularly likely to circumvent the process. Eighty-one percent of C-level respondents said they always or regularly bypass AI access approval, compared with 33% of intermediate-level employees.

The result is a widening gap between written AI governance and what companies can see and control. Delinea found that only 41% of IT leaders said all AI tools and agents accessing company data are actively monitored. When an AI tool or agent went outside its intended scope, 55% said it took at least a day to detect the incident, while 30% said detection took four days or longer.

That suggests the next phase of enterprise AI governance may depend less on writing additional rules and more on whether organizations can enforce existing ones while an agent is working.

"Most organizations cannot confidently answer three questions: What agents are running in our environment? What can they access? What have they actually done?" said Delinea CEO Art Gilliland. "An agent can begin a session with legitimate access and drift into something it was never meant to do."

The full report is available for download here.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

A new report from Caylent, an AI-focused Amazon Web Services Premier Tier Services Partner, found that enterprises are already moving agentic AI beyond pilots and into production environments. At the same time, organizations are putting strict conditions around autonomy, making governance and control the next major challenge for enterprise AI adoption.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research from Digital Realty.

#6808. ‘Rivals’ Season 2’s Shocking Tony Baddingham Scene, Explained—And What Happens Next

David Tennant’s ruthless TV boss has officially reached breaking point.

Warning: Major spoilers for Rivals season 2, part 2 ahead.

Just when we thought things couldn't get any more dramatic in Rutshire, Rivals has delivered one of its most shocking moments yet. The second half of season 2 has finally landed on Disney+ and Hulu, picking up in the aftermath of Lady Monica Baddingham's (Claire Rushbrook) devastating death. And while her husband Tony (David Tennant) is struggling to come to terms with losing his wife, his grief is taking a seriously disturbing turn.

Because, in a scene that nobody saw coming, the ruthless Corinium Television boss takes a rifle to Monica's beloved peacocks. Yes, you read that correctly.

It's a particularly brutal moment, even by Tony's standards, and one that reveals just how far he's spiraling following his wife's death.

But what exactly pushed him to breaking point? And what does it mean for the rest of the season?

When we last visited Rutshire, Monica had been killed in a car accident after discovering that Tony was having an affair with Maud O'Hara (Victoria Smurfit).

The latest episodes pick up in the aftermath of her death, with Tony struggling to process his grief while facing the consequences of his infidelity.

While the TV boss has never exactly been known for his kindness, the scene takes his cruelty to another level.

The peacocks are a reminder of Monica and the life they shared, making his decision to kill them feel particularly vicious.

Rather than dealing with the pain of losing his wife, Tony appears to direct his anger toward something she loved.

Speaking to Deadline about the latest episodes, David and executive producer Dominic Treadwell-Collins discussed Tony's increasingly destructive behavior following Monica's death.

He told the outlet: "To lose your life partner is devastating. Even for someone who’s not always the most morally engaged human, I think it’s bewildering and it’s distressing for him. He’s rudderless without her, so I think it’s quite a plausible reaction.

"He’s a man who doesn’t have a particularly well-oiled path to his own emotions, so I think the fact that such a traumatic moment should end with some kind of violent exorcism makes perfect sense. I think that’s who Tony is, unfortunately."

For someone who has spent much of the series manipulating those around him, Tony suddenly finds himself unable to control the one thing that matters most: his own life.

Of course, there's also the small matter of his affair with Maud, which continues to complicate an already messy situation.

If you were hoping Monica's death might inspire Tony to turn over a new leaf, we wouldn't hold our breath.

Despite everything that's happened, Tony remains determined to maintain his grip on Corinium Television, with his bitter rivalry against Declan O'Hara (Aidan Turner) showing no signs of slowing down.

And things are about to get even more complicated now that Declan has discovered the truth about Tony's affair with Maud.

The revelation leads to an explosive confrontation during the Bonfire Night celebrations, leaving Declan and Maud's marriage hanging in the balance.

Meanwhile, Tony's relationship with his son Archie remains fractured, and his increasingly unpredictable behavior raises questions about just how far he's prepared to go to protect his position.

With the battle for the television franchise reaching a critical point, Tony could find himself facing consequences both professionally and personally.

And given what we've just witnessed, we're slightly terrified to see what he does next.

The first three episodes of Rivals season 2, part 2 are available to stream on Disney+ and Hulu, with the remaining episodes arriving weekly.

#6805. Zoë Kravitz Keeps Turning to One Collection for Her Red-Carpet Looks

Zoë Kravitz has been going for gold with her recent string of red-carpet looks, all thanks to Anthony Vaccarello’s final collection for Saint Laurent.

On Tuesday evening, the actor and director attended the New York City premiere of her new heist comedy, How to Rob a Bank, where she turned heads in a lustrous, ab-baring set from the Saint Laurent Spring/Summer 2027 ready-to-wear collection.

The ensemble consisted of a cropped bolero jacket and a pair of straight-leg slacks, both of which were made from a gold, foil-like material with paisley designs embroidered all over.

Kravitz’s midriff-baring jacket was trimmed with chocolate fur at the wrists, while the neckline was decorated with a wreath of golden florals. To mirror the neck, her slightly-darker pants were cinched with a textured, studded belt.

To complete her monochromatic fit, Kravitz slipped into some golden sandals with curling heels and had her hair pulled into a messy top bun with baby strands dangling in front.

It was just last week that Kravitz pulled from this same collection, when she and fellow Saint Laurent muses Hailey Bieber and Rosé took the stage at the 2026 CFDA Fashion Awards to present Vaccarello with the International Designer of the Year award.

The prize served, in part, as a recognition of Vaccarello’s 10-year tenure at the French fashion house (he announced his impending departure early last month). And to commemorate the special moment, Kravitz and her co-presenters all dressed in looks from his final YSL offering.

The unforgettable collection was comprised of over 60 outfits, almost all in shimmering gold, that acted as the creative director’s glorious, gilded, swan song. It was presented in Paris, with a sparkling Eiffel Tower as its backdrop. No wonder Kravitz doesn’t want the moment to end.

#6804. Claude Intros Sonnet 5.5 with Model-Switching Cybersecurity Safeguards

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

According to Anthropic's explanation of model switching, automatic fallback is enabled by default in Claude. Users receive a notice, and the answer is labeled with the model that provided it. The chat then stays on Sonnet 5 unless the user changes the selection.

The company identifies penetration testing and exploit generation among activities that may trigger cybersecurity fallbacks. Source-code vulnerability scanning remains supported. Biology and reasoning-extraction safeguards can block requests outright, rather than route them elsewhere.

The distinction matters operationally. A block stops the request; a fallback allows an attempt to continue with a different model. Neither outcome should be treated as interchangeable with completion by the model the user originally chose.

Anthropic says Sonnet 5.5 generates output more than 30% faster than Sonnet 5 and can reduce cost per task by up to 30%, based on its testing. Token prices remain unchanged.

Those claims describe performance under the company's test conditions. An organization evaluating the service also needs to examine what happens when its own assignments encounter safeguards.

An internal pilot could record how often work is interrupted, whether fallback answers are usable, and when users need help. Such measurements would give buyers a more relevant basis for deployment than assuming every task receives the advertised model's capabilities.

Anthropic has announced plans to expand its Cyber Verification Program with tiered access for Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, and Mythos models. Its current program guidance explicitly excludes the two new 5.5 models and says the expansion is forthcoming.

Organizations should distinguish those planned arrangements from access available today.

The broader enterprise issue is accountability. When an AI service changes models during an assignment, someone still needs to determine whether the resulting work is suitable. A visible handoff helps, but organizations must decide how employees should respond to it.

For more information on Sonnet 5.5, go to the Anthropic site.

#6799. Convergence of AI, Quantum Risks Is Impacting Cybersecurity

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures. The 2026 Thales Data Threat Report: Quantum & AI Trends, based on responses from 3,120 security and IT management professionals across 20 countries, found that 98% are considering how the two technologies affect each other. Thirty percent reported a significant increase in security budgets specifically for AI.

The research, conducted by 451 Research by S&P Global, examines both the risks and potential benefits of the technologies. Fifty-seven percent believe quantum computing will enhance machine learning, while 54% expect it to enable advanced simulations of complex systems. The security findings span data governance, cloud infrastructure, and preparations for quantum-resistant encryption.

Cloud-based storage, cloud-delivered applications, and cloud management infrastructure were the three most frequently cited asset types targeted by attackers, at 35%, 34%, and 32%, respectively. The report connects that exposure to the large datasets required for AI training and the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure.

Organizations are also looking to cloud providers for protection: 67% reported investing in their cloud provider's AI-specific security tools, compared with 63% using an established security provider. Respondents could identify multiple sources of protection, including startups, large language model providers, and internally developed controls.

Encryption coverage remains incomplete. Only 7% of organizations reported encrypting more than 80% of their sensitive cloud data, while 29% reported encrypting more than 60%. The report's chart puts the average share encrypted at 47% in 2026, down from 51% in 2025.

The findings extend concerns raised in coverage of Thales' 2025 cloud security research, when AI security already ranked second to cloud security in spending priorities and 52% of respondents reported that AI security spending was displacing existing security budgets. The latest report again places AI security second, while its historical comparison shows average sensitive cloud data encryption coverage falling from 51% in 2025 to 47% in 2026.

AI security was the second-highest prioritized security spending category overall, behind cloud security. But protecting data ranked much lower among the measures organizations use to judge AI projects: Reducing or managing risks of data loss or noncompliance placed sixth among seven success criteria, at 35%.

Improving customer experience led those measures at 68%, followed by reducing employee toil through greater task automation at 63%. Meanwhile, poor data quality or initial data governance was the leading inhibitor to AI adoption, cited by 65%; security risks from exposed data followed at 61%. Another 51% cited AI initiatives moving too quickly to be properly secured.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

A new report from Caylent, an AI-focused Amazon Web Services Premier Tier Services Partner, found that enterprises are already moving agentic AI beyond pilots and into production environments. At the same time, organizations are putting strict conditions around autonomy, making governance and control the next major challenge for enterprise AI adoption.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research from Digital Realty.

#6795. Meet the Cast and Characters of ‘The Body’

The Body, the latest Netflix series set in the naughty aughties, as they say, has already created some buzz. A spooky, queer coming-of-age story with lots and lots of dancing?! What more could you want in that eerie time between Halloween and the winter holidays? Since several of the cast members are relatively unknown, here’s what you need to know about the cast and characters of The Body.

The series follows a dance team at a Catholic school in the early 00s who are struck by visions, mass hysteria, and possible other supernatural phenomena. We’ve got a sneaking suspicion that it will satisfy your Yellowjackets withdrawals cravings in more ways than one. The Body was created by Quinn Shephard, who previously adapted the series Under The Bridge for Hulu and made the movies Not Okay and Blame, which she also starred in alongside Nadia Alexander and Chris Messina.

To promote the series, Netflix gave each of the main girls saintly taglines. You decide how tongue-in-cheek they are meant to be in context when you watch. Anya’s is “Our Lady of Perpetual Confliction.”

Not to start this off with a tongue-twister, but Kristina Bogic played Gracie Darling in the Australian series Playing Gracie Darling. You can watch the similarly eerie series on Netflix. Her only other credit is a short film called Howl.

Lisbeth’s tagline is “The Snake in the Garden.” Oooh!

Geena Meszaros played a character called “Oatmeal Beth” on the Netflix series Wayward and a character called Bonnie Sue West in the 2024 film The Order. Her other credits include Soul’s Road, In Cold Light, Under the Banner of Heaven, the Freevee original High School, and the limited series A Teacher.

Elise’s tagline is “She Whose Heart Does Not Waver.” Aww?

Sara Boustany’s first acting job on screen was two episodes of Pen15... no big deal!! She also played a teenage version of Sarah Shahi’s character in Dolly Parton’s Heartstrings and “Isabel” in the 2025 movie Hello Beautiful.

Leo is one of the few male characters from the adjoining boy’s school.

Jackson Kelly played David Saunders, the teenage patient/suspect on the first season of The Pitt. He also played Marcus on the limited series Imperfect Women. Jackson has also popped up on shows like The Good Doctor, Station 19, The Righteous Gemstones, and George and Mandy’s First Marriage.

Grace’s tagline is “The Patron Saint of Sacrifice.” Roger that.

Sofia Wylie arguably became the female lead of High School Musical: The Musical: The Series after Olivia Rodrigo left to become, well, Olivia Rodrigo. So you know she’s a triple threat. Prior to HSMTMTS, she competed on So You Think You Can Dance and played Buffy on the Disney Channel series Andi Mack. More recently, Sofia starred in the Netflix fantasy film The School for Good and Evil and played a supporting role in the Prime Video romance film The Map That Leads To You.

Maddie’s tagline is “The Disciple of Sacred Truth.” Well well well!

Shirley Chen is an indie darling in the making you’ve seen in films like Slanted, Didi, Quiz Lady, and Beast Beast. Her television credits also include two episodes of Parenthood, two episodes of Speechless, and one episode of The Resident.

Catherine is another notable adult character as she is Lisbeth and Leo’s mom.

Louisa Krause is a stage and screen actress you know best from her recurring roles on Billions and Ray Donovan and her lead role as “Anna” on the second season of The Girlfriend Experience. Her big screen credits include Superman, Dark Waters, Woman Walks Ahead, and Martha Marcy May Marlene. In the theatre world, she’s best known for playing Rose in several premiere productions of Annie Baker’s play The Flick.

Father Franklin is, naturally, a member of the administration as the high school’s religious leader and is also Grace’s uncle.

Nnamdi Asomugha is a former NFL player who played for the Eagles, the 49ers, and the Raiders. He’s also an Independent Spirit Award nominated actor for Crown Heights. His film and television credits include The Good Nurse, Sylvie’s Love, and a guest star appearance on Friday Night Lights, among others. He’s set to star alongside Jessica Chastain in The Savant. Nnamdi is also an accomplished stage actor who made his off-Broadway debut in Good Grief in 2018 and his Broadway debut two years later in A Soldier’s Play.

Gabby Windey is a legendary member of both Bachelor Nation (and so far the only member of the runner-up to co-lead to married-to-a-woman pipeline) and The Traitors castle. She’s also a former ICU nurse, a Dancing With The Stars runner-up, and hosts a hilarious podcast called Long Winded. This is her acting debut, but next, you’ll see her in Molly Gordon’s upcoming film Peaked alongside Owen Thiele, Emma Mackey, Simone Ashley, and Connor Storrie.

Tyler is a younger, male student in the series.

Marcel Ruiz played Alex Alvarez on the popular reboot of One Day At A Time for al four seasons. He actually won an Imagen Award for best young actor in 2017, NBD. Since then he starred in the movie Breakthrough and will be featured in the upcoming historical drama Porto Rico.

#6792. Every Outfit TV Is the Digital-Age Fashion Police We Never Knew We Needed

The founders of @everyoutfitonSATC spill the tea on their latest venture into video podcasting

“There are some things that need to be seen to be believed,” says Lauren Garroni, the brunette half of @EveryOutfitonSATC—a Sex and the City fashion archive Instagram account turned Fashion Police-style podcast that she runs with her blonde counterpart, Chelsea Fairless. She’s talking about a stretched canvas image of Dave Navarro walking in the Anna Sui Spring-Summer 1997 runway show, which is currently being sold online by a well-known publishing company. But she might as well have been speaking about the pair’s latest venture: Every Outfit TV.

“Every Outfit is a weekly rundown of the comings and goings in pop culture and fashion,” explains Garroni. She and Fairless launched their podcast in 2021, around the time that the SATC spinoff And Just Like That was announced. Devotees will recall that it was actually phase two, phase one being an Instagram account born in 2016 from Fairless’s frustration with the lack of variety when it came to photos of SATC outfits on the internet. Ten years later, the podcast is viral, the Instagram has 709k followers, and the overall project has morphed into Every Outfit TV, a video component available exclusively on Patreon.

The video format offers Fairless and Garroni the chance to do things that wouldn’t work with solely audio. (Note: not every new podcast episode will have video.) “We grew up being obsessed with the VH1 Vogue Fashion Awards and stuff like House of Style,” says Fairless. The brilliantly lo-fi, green-screen-style opening sequence posted to their Instagram account last week distills the energy they’re going for with its nods to The Nanny, Clueless, and Access Hollywood.

Podcast listeners, TV viewers, and Instagram followers can expect new franchises like “It’s Always Fashion Week Somewhere” and “The Runway Rewind,” which will showcase the pair’s thoughts on runway shows while tapping into the tabloid entertainment vibe that they both admire. There will also be Oprah Winfrey-style book club episodes and “Top 10” lists fiercely curated by Garroni and Fairless themselves.

Ask the duo why now was the ideal time to pivot, and Fairless answers bluntly. “Lauren forced me to,” she says. Garroni sees it differently. “Chelsea finally succumbed to being on camera,” she refutes. Really, the two are responding to a general podcast-world shift to video, a medium that enables them to “fulfill an itch,” says Fairless. She didn’t have cable in her house as a child, prompting her to develop an obsession with fashion magazines and websites like First View (RIP). Garroni’s upbringing makes up for it. “My parents just gave me unrestricted access to television, cable, and the internet from a young age,” she says. Now, both of them are self-diagnosed as chronically online and cite hours on TikTok and Instagram as the reason why they collectively possess the knowledge of someone who has a PhD in fashion history.

While this wealth of niche information could potentially alienate an audience, Garroni and Fairless’s playful banter (think Siskel and Ebert, or Waldorf and Statler) makes up for it. They don’t shy away from cross-generational references, either. “We have laundered our older gay men references that we learned from watching E! News and E! True Hollywood Story,” says Garroni.

In the end, the duo intends to be authentically themselves. “I think Lauren and I are examples of millennials who are very culturally Gen X,” says Fairless. “We were always more interested in Gen X things, Gen X people. I didn’t give a shit about the Backstreet Boys when I was a teenager. We were into Hole and Pavement.”

It would be remiss not to talk about the hit television series that started it all. At this point, they have documented every outfit on Sex and the City more than once in the past ten years, but developments in the show’s universe kept them going. “Something would always happen to prevent us from abandoning this,” says Fairless. Garroni agrees, recalling when Cynthia Nixon ran for governor of New York in 2018. (They released a satirical book We Should All Be Mirandas a year later. Garroni claims to be a Miranda with a Carrie rising, while Fairless is a Miranda with a Samantha rising.) “It always sustained us,” says Garroni. “Every moment we thought, ‘Okay, how long can this joke go on for?’”

If Fairless has anything to do with it, the answer to that question is forever. “Sex and the City is never done. If there is one thing I know to be true in this life, it will never go away,” she says. “It will always change forms and there will always be something newsworthy to talk about.”

Asked what else is coming down the pipeline, Garroni is quick to mention field pieces that will take them out of their impeccably designed Los Angeles set, complete with vintage gray furniture designed in the ’90s by architect David Chipperfield and a pink carpet. (It was modeled after a mashup of Halston’s New York City apartment—an Every Outfit hyperfixation—and the Chicago department store Carson Pirie Scott.) Fairless, however, has other goals in mind. She’d like to add more single-breasted blazers to her wardrobe rotation, she says. Also: “I want hair and makeup.”

#6790. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

#6789. Faux Fur, Tuxedo Dressing, and Other Coat Trends Taking Over This Winter

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

These are the styles to know as the weather gets colder

A great winter outfit deserves an equally great coat, especially when it’s an essential layer for the next several months. Beyond the practical need for warmth, outerwear often sets the tone for an entire look as the first layer you see. A sharply tailored wool coat can make jeans and a sweater feel more polished, while an oversize silhouette or rich texture brings dimension and new proportions to otherwise clean-lined looks.

For fall/winter 2026, designers are rethinking classic outerwear through distinctive silhouettes, textures, and details. At Ferragamo, Tory Burch, and Fendi, precise tailoring took a dressier turn, with slim silhouettes, tuxedo-inspired designs, and satin lapels bringing eveningwear polish to wool coats. Meanwhile, Ralph Lauren, Mugler, and Burberry embraced leather for a sleeker city-ready take on winter dressing. Faux fur took on a distinctly vintage feel at Stella McCartney, Bottega Veneta, and Khaite, while plush collars added texture to everything from elegant overcoats to more everyday designs.

Whether you’re looking to invest in a forever piece or simply want something fresher than the coat you’ve worn for the past few winters, these six trends offer plenty of inspiration.

Faux fur is taking a distinctly retro turn this winter, with plush textures and elegant silhouettes reminiscent of vintage finds. Stella McCartney’s floor-grazing belted coat and Bottega Veneta’s voluminous fur both recall the glamour of the ’30s and ’40s, while Khaite’s shaggy cream jacket has more of a ’70s feel, particularly when dressed down with jeans. Of course, we love a faux-fur coat as the finishing touch to a dressy outfit, but there’s something even chicer about pairing one with everyday denim and boots.

Arma Verena Reversible Shearling and Leather Coat

For fall/winter 2026, the black evening coat is having a moment. Designers showcased styles with luxe updates to keep things more interesting. Think sharp, menswear-inspired tailoring with a more formal finish, as seen at Celine, to elegant satin coats at Dries Van Noten. It’s the go-to coat to wear to upcoming holiday parties and formal dinners, or if you just want to upgrade your traditional winter coat for something a bit more luxurious this season.

Polo Ralph Lauren Wool and Silk Crepe Tuxedo Duster Coat

The Row Erdene Single-Breasted Wool Long Coat

There’s a certain refinement to a beautifully tailored coat, especially when the details borrow from formalwear. Think precise cuts, elongated silhouettes, and tuxedo-inspired touches that feel polished enough for an evening out. There’s a more dressed-up sensibility to these classic designs—as seen at Ferragamo, Tory Burch, and Fendi—from the precise fit to the smallest finishing details. This attention to detail brings a beautiful sense of sophistication to workwear and when paired with simple everyday separates.

Gap x Victoria Beckham Wool-Blend Chesterfield Coat

Mackage Elodie-R Double-Breasted Wool-Blend Coat

When a puffer or classic wool coat feels too predictable, a leather coat offers a much bolder alternative. #6786. Friday the 13th Just Got a Little Luckier This Year

And you can thank Mercury Retrograde and Venus Retrograde finally coming to an end for that!

Friday the 13th may be tomorrow, but that doesn’t mean that luck isn’t in the air! This year on the longtime “unlucky” day, both Mercury Retrograde and Venus Retrograde (finally!) come to an end within a few hours of each other, bringing relief and clarity to your close relationships and ability to express yourself.

One retrograde is painful and frustrating enough, but over the past few weeks, you’ve navigated two at the same time, which can feel immobilizing at best and chaotic at worst. Fortunately, you’re reaching the finish line! Beyond the retrograde relief, Friday the 13th is actually considered an incredibly magical day, brimming with intuitive breakthroughs, despite its reputation.

For millennia, the number 13 was actually revered by many cultures and traditions all across the globe because of its connection to the average number of lunations we experience every year and also the average number of menstrual cycles a person experiences in a year. It was celebrated as a day to connect with and honor the Goddess. People who have periods are said to experience heightened intuition during their cycle and a strong connection to the other Realms. It’s through these associations that the number 13 carries deep and profound ties to magic, creation, manifestation, and birthing both new life and new desires and visions for your future.

Over centuries, the original meaning was distorted by patriarchal leaders and religions out of fear of collective feminine energy. By concocting false narratives and creating fearful myths about Friday the 13th, the patriarchal forces were able to exercise more control all while divorcing and dispelling many feminine people from reclaiming their own power and agency. Which is actually why many people consider this an “unlucky” day—but it’s really not at all! Once again, the patriarchy was out here ruining everything.

So clear your schedule because here come the divine downloads and revelations you’ve been waiting for. With Mercury Retrograde and Venus Retrograde ending, you’re ready to decide who and what from your past is worth clinging onto and which coffins you’re ready to put a final nail into. #6785. SailPoint Intros New Capabilities to Address Agentic AI Identity Security Challenge

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint. In its latest Horizons of Identity Security report, SailPoint found that 79% of enterprises already have AI agents running in production, while just 2% have deployed identity security tools specifically designed to govern them.

That gap could become harder to ignore as organizations give agents more autonomy. Agents can increasingly access applications, sensitive data, and credentials, and in some cases take actions on a user's behalf, effectively creating a new class of identity that IT and security teams need to manage.

SailPoint, which develops identity security and access governance technology for enterprises, is responding with a collection of new identity security capabilities announced at its Navigate 2026 conference. The company is expanding its Agentic Fabric to discover "shadow AI" agents and provide runtime authorization and controls capable of immediately disabling an agent.

The company also introduced what it calls SailPoint Autonomous Agents, specialized AI agents designed to govern other agents. The security agents can monitor runtime activity, intercept threats, and adjust permissions while allowing legitimate agents to continue operating, SailPoint said.

The idea reflects one of the challenges created by increasingly autonomous AI systems: Traditional human approval processes may not be fast enough to govern software capable of taking large numbers of actions on its own.

"The idea that a security admin is going to review and approve access for a group of autonomous agents making 10,000 tool calls a second isn't just outdated — it's a fantasy," SailPoint CTO Chandra Gnanasambandam said.

Another part of SailPoint's approach focuses on visibility.

New endpoint and browser sensors, along with SIEM and XDR telemetry and non-human identity discovery capabilities, are designed to uncover agents, MCP servers, credentials, tools, and data stores that haven't been brought under an organization's identity program.

The company is also introducing Autonomous Identity Security Posture Management, or A-ISPM, which uses identity information from its Atlas platform to identify dormant accounts, orphaned access, shadow administrators, and other potentially risky access paths. Organizations can then revoke access, disable an identity or AI agent, assign an owner or automate enforcement.

Just-in-Time provisioning is another part of the strategy. Rather than giving people, service accounts, and AI agents permanent access, permissions can be granted when needed and subjected to conditions such as reauthentication or a business justification.

Capabilities across SailPoint's Agentic Fabric, Human Fabric, and Atlas platforms are beginning to roll out globally. A-ISPM and several other announced features are expected in the company's fourth quarter of fiscal 2027.

"Autonomous agents are no longer just experimental copilots; they are becoming the primary execution layer of modern enterprises," SailPoint President Matt Mills said.

For more information, go to the SailPoint site.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

A new report from Caylent, an AI-focused Amazon Web Services Premier Tier Services Partner, found that enterprises are already moving agentic AI beyond pilots and into production environments. At the same time, organizations are putting strict conditions around autonomy, making governance and control the next major challenge for enterprise AI adoption.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research from Digital Realty.

#6782. How Olivia Munn Takes the Edge Off

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The actress shares the beauty and self-care essentials she swears by, including a $75 volumizing cream

Welcome to Take the Edge Off, a series that breaks down the beauty and self-care routines of influencers, CEOs, experts, and celebrities. Find out how they unwind and decompress, and take a closer look at the holy-grail essentials they’re raving about.

Life can throw unexpected surprises—good and bad—your way, and many lean on wellness practices to stay grounded in chaos. Olivia Munn knows this firsthand.

The actress was diagnosed with early-stage Luminal B breast cancer in early 2023, thanks to what she refers to as the “life-saving” risk assessment test with renowned obstetric and gynecologic surgeon Thaïs Aliabadi at the helm. With a toddler and fresh off a double mastectomy, Munn turned to meditation to find her footing through it all.

“I always associated meditation with having to quiet your mind, and I couldn’t imagine how anyone actually did that, but over time, I’ve come to understand that it isn’t about stopping the noise in your head; it’s about learning not to be carried away by it,” Munn tells Bazaar. “I don’t feel the need to chase every thought or immediately make sense of everything. I can just let things settle without feeling responsible for figuring them all out.”

That same strength and balance are exactly what fashion label Anne Klein wanted to embody when the brand enlisted Munn as ambassador for its fall/winter 2026 collection and to continue spreading her message.

Ahead, we caught up with Munn about what else keeps her present during times of stress, from the manifestation candle she loves to light to words she lives by.

“The future you is depending on the you today to do the right thing.” This means the me today depended on the me three years ago to go to the doctor, get the MRI, and face cancer head-on. I was depending on the me last week, month, or year to start working out more, learn that new language, or organize my closet. When I think about how fast time goes by, and how tomorrow, next month, and next year come in the blink of an eye, it motivates me to take the first step toward whatever I want to do.

A couple of months ago, I began using prescription-strength azelaic acid and tretinoin. I start with azelaic acid, then tretinoin, and finish with the Volume Cream by Adipeau, my holy-grail moisturizer.

My favorite cream right now is Adipeau’s Volume Cream. I’ve reached the point in my life where my hands and neck are showing signs of being crepey. A friend told me about this cream and promised me that if I used it every day for a month, I’d see a difference, and she was right. The science behind it is incredible, so incredible that I often worry about whether the company is doing well because I need them to stay in business forever.

My makeup goal has always been no-makeup makeup. But it is incredibly difficult to master, especially on yourself. My hat’s off to all the influencers who do it so beautifully; I see you and wish I were you.

Shige brand cuticle oil. The amazing manicurist Shige [Taylor] created her own nail line, beginning with an incredible cuticle oil made in Japan. [My] nail bed has never looked or felt so hydrated.

Yes, I light candles from Aloha Elixir. They’re made in Hawaii and are really special, intentional candles.

When I start getting anxiety, which I often do, or have difficulty falling asleep, I do this breathing technique that activates the parasympathetic nervous system, which controls our “fight-or-flight” response: Breathe in through your nose for four beats, hold for seven, breathe out through your nose for eight beats. It calms my system down, and the anxiety goes away, or I drift off to sleep. I do it three to six times, sometimes a little more. But it always works for me.

#6781. Meet the Cast and Characters of ‘The Good Daughter’

Excited to watch The Good Daughter on Peacock, but didn’t read the book by Karin Slaughter that it’s based on? Here’s everything you need to know about the cast and characters of The Good Daughter, without giving anything away. Well, maybe one thing. There’s a character who only shows up in one timeline for a reason. You might guess why, but I’m going to spoil it later just so we’re all on the same page.

First of all, no... this series is not connected to The Good Wife, The Good Fight, The Good Doctor, or The Good Place. As mentioned above, The Good Daughter is an adaptation of a bestselling novel that was published in 2017. The story is about two adult sisters who are reunited after a violent attack in their Georgia hometown brings up memories of the murder that tore them apart.

Slaughter herself adapted her book for the screen, penning the teleplays for all six episodes. Steph Green stepped in to direct and executive produce the series. As for the cast, here’s who you’ll see on The Good Daughter and who they play.

Sam, quintessential eldest daughter, left town and became a patent lawyer.

Rose Byrne’s breakout role in Hollywood was as a titular bridesmaid in Bridesmaids, though she had already appeared in films like Sunshine, Troy and Marie Antoinette at that time. That same year, 2011, she played Moira MacTaggert in X-Men: First Class and would reprise that role in a later film. She’s still best known for comedies, however, like Spy, Neighbors, and Neighbors 2 as well as horror movies like 28 Weeks Later and the Insidious films. Television-wise, Byrne played Gloria Steinem in Mrs. America and starred in both Physical and Platonic. She was nominated for an Academy Award for If I Had Legs I’d Kick You and a Tony Award for Fallen Angels.

Meanwhile, younger sister Charlie stayed in town and became a defense attorney.

Meghann Fahy was one of the breakout stars of The White Lotus’ second season, though she also hadn’t come out of nowhere. Prior to the HBO seires, Meghann made her Broadway debut in the Pulitzer Prize winning musical Next to Normal as an understudy and replacement for the character Natalie, then played Sutton on The Bold Type, and also acted in 83 episodes of One Life To Live. Since the HBO series, she has starred in the movie Drop and the Netflix series Sirens as well as The Perfect Couple and Your Monster. She is also a two time Emmy nominee, for both Sirens and The White Lotus.

Audrey Grace Marshall and Drew Cheek play Young Sam and Charlie

Flashbacks show who the sisters were before the trauma that shaped them.

Before The Good Daughter, Audrey Grace Marshall played Young Cassie on The Flight Attendent and was supposed to be in the Buffy the Vampire Slayer reboot. She did play Viv in the Fairly Oddparents live action reboot thing in 2022. Drew Cheek, on the other hand, has only one previous credit: a movie called F*ck Valentine’s Day.

Rusty is the sisters’ father and a former defense attorney with a reputation.

Brendan Gleeson is an Academy Award-nominated and Emmy Award-winning actor. He won the latter for playing Winston Churchill in the miniseries Into the Storm. You’ve seen him in the Harry Potter films, Braveheart, 28 Days Later, Gangs of New York, Troy, In Bruges, Edge of Tomorrow, Paddington 2, and The Banshees of Inisherin. Brendan has not done much television, but did lead Mr. Mercedes from 2017 to 2019, appear in the 2022 series State of the Union, and play Silvermane in Spider-Noir.

Lenore is Rusty’s secretary and the girls’ surrogate mother figure.

This is Harper Steele’s first ever professional acting credit. She is an Emmy winning comedy writer who worked at Saturday Night Live for 13 years and was a head writer for four of those years. Her other screenwriting credits include Eurovision Song Contest: The Story of Fire Saga and The Spoils of Babylon. Harper recently appeared with Will Ferrell in the road trip buddy documentary Will & Harper about their friendship before and after she came out as transgender.

Kelly is a suspect in a school shooting who Charlie chooses to represent.

Shayna Bernardo’s previous credits before The Good Daughter include the Lifetime movie Don’t Sweat The Small Stuff, an episode of Doom Patrol, and the horror movie Sorority Secrets.

Judith is an English teacher at a local middle school.

Paula Malcomson is probably best known for playing Katniss’ understandably checked-out mother Asterid Everdeen in The Hunger Games movies. She was also a series regular on the Battlestar Galactica prequel series Caprica, as well as Deadwood and Ray Donovan. She also recurred on Sons of Anarchy.

Huck is a teacher at the local middle school with a grudge against Rusty.

Jake McDorman is best known for leading roles in shows like Quintuplets, Greek, Manhattan Love Story, Limitless, Murphy Brown, The Right Stuff, Mrs. Davis and Dopesick. He also had a recurring role on Shameless. Movie-wise, you’ve seen him in Happiest Season, Lady Bird, American Sniper, Live Free Or Die Hard, Bring It On: All Or Nothing, and, of course, Aquamarine.

Olivia Williams plays Harriet ‘Gamma’ Quinn

First of all, no, this Harriet was the sisters’ mother and a genius physicist. Spoiler alert: she was violently murdered when Sam and Charlie were young girls.

You may know Olivia Williams from films like Rushmore, the 2003 Peter Pan, Anna Karenina, and An Education. Fun fact: Williams played Moira MacTaggert in X-Men: The Last Stand. She played Camilla Parker-Bowles on two seasons of The Crown and her other television credits include Dollhouse, Manhattan, and Dune: Prophecy. She’s also a prolific stage actress and starred as Nurse Ratched in One Flew Over The Cuckoo’s Nest at London’s Old Vic Theatre this year.

Ben is Charlie’s estranged husband, and also a lawyer. So many lawyers!

Michael Dorman is an Australian actor with many starring roles on television to his name, including shows like Wonderland, For All Mankind, Patriot, Territory, and Joe Pickett. His film credits include Leigh Whannell’s The Invisible Man and Pirates of the Caribbean: Dead Men Tell No Tales.

#6778. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

#6777. 6 Antique Trends Designers Are Watching for 2027

Above: The kitchen in designer Phillip Thomas’s Bellport retreat.

Do the words “antique” and “trend” really belong in the same sentence? In many ways, a great antique shop feels like the antidote to trend fatigue: a space where the true must-haves are hidden in dusty corners, with part of the fun in the thrill of the hunt. Yet, as we round out 2026, there’s no denying the current design cycle is casting off perfection for patina. Antiques are en vogue, and top designers are keeping a close eye on the periods, pieces, and yes, trends, that are poised to find new life in 2027.

“The return of interest in antiques reflects a trend towards more personal, unique interiors, and away from strict modern rooms which can often be cool and generic,” says ELLE Decor A-List designer Jamie Drake. This shift paves the way for more playful interactions between pieces, unexpected layering, and unabashed personality.

Other experts second the motion for more character. “I’m seeing clients really drawn to antiques with more to them than meets the eye: pieces that have a bit of humor or whimsy, or pieces with great provenance,” says ELLE Decor Rising Star William Cullum. (He lists a “ridiculously giant butterfly light fixture” from New Orleans, rattan chairs from Bing Crosby’s estate, and a disco ball from Cyndi Lauper’s Bonham’s sale in his list of recent acquisitions—professional and personal.)

Luckily for the amateur antiquer, not all trending vintage styles require a substantial space commitment or glamorous provenance—although an interesting backstory helps. “Antiques are always about a story: where you found them, who owned them, and how that story will continue on after us,” Cullum says.

Ahead, we asked top designers and experts to share the most inspiring antique trends of the moment. From neoclassic mirrors to hand-painted chests paired with modern art, their takes are sure to inform your next estate sale rummage, or inspire you to restyle an old heirloom.

We called mirrored surfaces a major trend to watch for fall 2026, and antique trends have followed suit. Unexpected (or better yet, artfully manipulated) reflections can add rich dimension and movement to any space, but in this category, antiques create a window to the past that’s tough to beat.

“One of the easiest ways to add a sense of history to a room is to include an antique mirror,” says Drake. “Whether you choose a sober and strict neo-classic piece or a more flamboyant baroque one, reflections of the past add depth and sparkle.”

“One trend I’m observing is an uptick in antique dowry chests,” antiques dealer Ali Mahon says. From Pennsylvania Dutch to Swedish Folk and Spanish carved, she’s noticed clients gravitating toward more delicately adorned storage over spare. With thoughtfully mixed furniture (from style to period) becoming more desirable than matching sets, Mahon sees more hand-painted antique surfaces in our future: “With their decorative tulips, leaves, birds, and hearts, dowry chests create a colorful splash in any room.”

ELLE Decor Rising Star Reagan Gail Struble agrees—although delicate designs are not a requirement of the trend, in her book: “Something about a playful wash of color on an awesome old piece of furniture is very alluring, especially when you can see the age and texture of the wood peeking through,” she says. “Hand-painted case pieces add a little punch of charm to any room.”

Luckily, these antiques can be just as functional as they are fashionable. Mahon recommends using a painted chest as a coffee table, at the foot of the bed, or in a space that could benefit from a bit more romance (and a few extra drawers). When styling your find in a modern space, Struble says that contrast is key: try a “chippy Swedish chest of drawers” with an unexpected photograph or painting above it.

According to ELLE Decor A-List designer Nick Olsen, Art Deco pieces are setting the tone for 2027 antique trends—although, if you ask him, they were never out. “I'm particularly inspired by the [art deco influence in the] Gujarat palace in India,” he says. “I'm fascinated by how every element seems to have a wide border: marble floors, carpets, walls of veneer and mohair velvet upholstery.”

According to Olsen, using Art Deco design compositions can do as much for a room as actual pieces from the period. “I like the idea of contrasting borders: solid textures punctuated by smaller graphic prints on furniture and more delicate lighting,” he explains. And while borders are easy to create with paint, paneling, and carpet seaming, Olsen challenges clients to push the Art Deco agenda further by bordering with luxe materials.

Whether you’re new to antiquing or simply trying to add more character to a space, lighting is an excellent place to start. “Lighting is the jewelry of the home, so it’s one of my favorite places to bring in something vintage,” says A-Lister Corey Damen Jenkins. “An Art Deco sconce or an antique brass lantern has a warmth to it that’s hard to find in something brand new.”

Adding to an existing space? Jenkins advises starting small: “Swap one overhead fixture or put a pair of vintage lamps on a console, and you’ll be surprised how much the whole room shifts,” he says. “It’s an easy way to add history without starting over.”

“In light of America’s 250th birthday, Americana in general is in vogue,” says Mahon. Among her clients, the trend has manifested in quilts and textiles, portraits of sea captains, trade signs, and ironstone—culminating in an aesthetic she describes as “elevated country—not corny.”

If you’ve noticed that no particular design period seems to reign supreme among designers’ top antique trend picks, it’s not an accident. Heading into 2027, there’s a strong argument to be made that mixed-period design is more influential than any singular reference.

“I love a room that doesn’t belong to any one decade,” Jenkins says. “A nineteenth-century chest with a 1970s lamp on top, or a Lucite chair pulled up to an antique desk. Those are the pairings that make people stop and look twice.” Drake adds that seemingly out-of-place seating is an easy way to add intrigue. “I love including antique chairs as sculptural notes, contrasted against modern upholstery and tables,” he says.

But when it comes to antiquing, there’s really only so much one can plan for. Letting a great find lead the way often turns out to be a better strategy than arriving to an estate sale with a rigid checklist. The best way to keep up with top designers when it comes to antiquing this year? Find a piece that has something to say, place it where it seems to belong, and then try it where it doesn’t.

Grace McCarty is a freelance Associate Digital Editor at ELLE Decor, where she covers design trends, shopping, and culture. She previously held a staff position at SELF Magazine, where she focused on beauty, style, and wellness. Her work has also appeared in Glamour and Parade.
