#6742. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

#6737. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

#6732. Kim Kardashian’s Beverly Hills Marital Villa Lists for $8.5 Million—Plus 4 More Luxury Homes for Sale

This week’s standout luxury listings include the Notting Hill blue-door townhouse, Diana Jenkins’s Hidden Hills mansion, and a Connecticut estate by a Frank Lloyd Wright protégé.

Even if you keep up with the Kardashians, you might not recognize this Beverly Hills house, which was one of Kim Kardashian’s first real estate purchases. The reality star bought the gated home 16 years ago for $3.4 million, and it served as the setting when basketball player Kris Humphries proposed to her in 2011. But the residence has gotten a major overhaul in the years since Kardashian sold it following the short-lived marriage, and now the Tuscan-style villa is on the market for nearly $8.5 million.

Other listings on our radar this week include another reality TV–linked estate: A former Real Housewife of Beverly Hills is parting with her 13,200-square-foot Hidden Hills house. On the East Coast, a striking Connecticut home by a Frank Lloyd Wright protégé is also on the market. And across the pond, the memorable blue-door townhouse from ‘90s rom-com Notting Hill in London is also for sale. Check them all out below.

One of Kim Kardashian’s earliest real estate purchases was this gated property in Beverly Hills, which she acquired in 2010 for $3.4 million. The Tuscan-style villa quickly became a familiar backdrop on her family’s E! reality show, Keeping Up with the Kardashians, and was even the site of Kris Humphries’s (televised) 2011 proposal to her. In the aftermath of their 72-day marriage, Kardashian sold the half-acre estate, and its new owners tapped fast-rising designer Sebastian Zuchowicki to oversee a complete overhaul. Now on the market for just under $8.5 million with Jack Harris and Michael Fahimian of Beverly Hills Estates, the five-bedroom home has a double-height entry foyer, custom kitchen cabinetry, and a primary suite with a fireplace, two walk-in closets, and a soaking tub.

Now, four years since leaving the Bravo reality series, the native Bosnian is returning to her home country, per Realtor.com—and listing the 13,200-square-foot mansion for $23 million with Sandeep Dhariwal of Sotheby’s International Realty—Beverly Hills Brokerage. Set on 4.4 acres, the seven-bedroom house was built in 2019 and comes with a plethora of perks, among them an infinity-edge pool, barbecue, fitness studio, and home cinema.

It’s perhaps the most famous house in one of London’s most charming neighborhoods, so beloved that crowds are known to gather around it like the English version of Carrie Bradshaw’s West Village apartment. The blue-doored townhouse where Hugh Grant’s fictional bookseller lived and wooed Julia Roberts’s paparazzi-plagued Hollywood actress in 1999’s Notting Hill is now on the market. And the 2,750-square-foot house can be yours for roughly $10 million.

With its organic silhouette, cantilevered roofline, and clever use of space, Leuthold House by Allan Gelbin bears the hallmarks of his mentor, legendary architect Frank Lloyd Wright. Completed in 1966 on five rolling acres in New Canaan, the five-bedroom house spans roughly 5,700 square feet and underwent a restoration by Hobbs Inc. and JMK Architects in 2014. The interiors feature long expanses of glass and mahogany, while the grounds come with a spring-fed pond, a pool, a one-bedroom guest house, and 150 specimen trees. Listed for $6.2 million with John Engel and Melissa Engel of Douglas Elliman, the two-story house is on the market for the first time in 29 years.

One of the art world’s most famous couples once lived and worked in a gated townhouse a block from Washington Square Park. Jackson Pollock and Lee Krasner didn’t own the MacDougal Alley home—a brick-faced carriage house built in the 1870s—but rather stayed there in the winter of 1950 due to the generosity of its owner, a fellow celebrated artist and their friend Alfonso Ossorio. Now, Ossorio’s 1,500-square-foot residence is on the market for the first time since 1988, per the New York Post. The asking price for the two-bedroom, two-bath home is nearly $8 million.

Bolder hues are getting the starring role as winter sets in

While soap manicures, subtle petal nails, and longevity techniques once dominated the summer months, a darker color palette is quickly taking over now that the clocks have gone back and evenings are early. Yet if you're hesitant to venture into "too bold" territory or fear a pop of color on your fingertips, a dark manicure doesn't have to feel overly attention-grabbing.

In fact, as professional nail technician Samantha Rudge shares, dark manicures with a more minimalist finish are proving to be winter's most popular manicure trend. "For this season, I think we'll be seeing darker classic manicures with minimalist touches," she shares, adding that she has already seen an uptick in shade requests for Bio Sculpture's rich, chocolate-toned Decadent Cocoa and the ever-popular Garnet Noir, a moody red-black hue. "Nail designs like chrome, cat eye, micro French tips, and tortoiseshell will also dominate this season," she adds.

"Short nails are also very popular right now, no matter the nail art or shades," she adds. Regardless of shape or color, Rudge stresses the importance of prioritizing nail health at this time of year. To protect nail longevity and prevent chipping, Rudge recommends assessing your natural nail condition, choosing the right base (BIAB or gel), and using a nourishing cuticle oil to repair and strengthen brittle nails.

Ahead, discover 10 new dark manicures for those who love a more minimalist finish, from “Garnet Noir” nails to subtle lines.

Somewhere between a square and an oval, these squoval nails look sophisticated and smart—plus, paired with the deep red “garnet noir” shade, they make a strong case for opting for a shorter length.

The epitome of minimalist nail art, add subtle intrigue to your manicure this winter via a curved line created on the cuticles. Choose complementary shades to recreate it: think cherry red with hot pink; navy blue and khaki green.

Taking over the SS27 runways this season, purple is finally having a moment in the spotlight. When it comes to nail colors, look for richer hues with a jewel-tone finish to keep the manicure elevated and modern. Rudge recommends Bio Sculpture's Erica shade.

All shades of wine are a staple as the festive season approaches, but why not add a chrome effect for extra sparkle?

Coffee tones are becoming one of the most popular color stories in nail salons this winter. These teeny-tiny dots make for a unique switch-up and can be created using a micro dotting tool.

A subtle marble effect elevates an otherwise classic burgundy manicure.

While almond and square are among the most popular of nail shapes, don't underestimate a soft oval. Paired with a classic cabernet tone, this dark manicure strikes the balance of looking impactful yet soft.

If you're taking a break from acrylics or longer lengths, a super-short licorice manicure is the way to do it. Add a glossy top coat and keep the nails short and square.

In the mood to experiment? Simply switch up each nail with chocolate swirls, block colors, or a French tip to recreate this look.

This ultra-glossy midnight blue manicure is all about a high-shine, mirrorlike finish that could be created with a chrome powder or top coat.

#6730. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

Casting Robert Pattinson definitely challenges that approach, but we’ll see!

#6723. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6721. The 10 Best Hair Wavers Bazaar Editors Are Actually Using

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Get beach hair all year round with these top-rated tools

As someone with pin-straight Asian hair, I have always admired waves and curls. I have slick, straight, stubborn hair, and—most frustratingly—it resists holding a curl. I’ve tried every trick in the book: extra-hold mousses, setting my curls with pins, and the best strong-hold hairsprays. Nothing seemed to do the trick—until I discovered hair wavers, specialized curling irons that create bends instead of twists.

In recent years, hair wavers have grown in popularity because they're easy to use and deliver effortless-looking results. “The effect [of a waver] is less curled and more textured. It’s great for that effortless, tousled vibe or adding body to finger hair without that much bounce,” says celebrity hairstylist Danielle Priano. Ahead, Priano shares her picks for the best hair wavers, along with some of the Bazaar beauty team’s favorites.

Every time my hairstylist uses this waver at the salon, I notice that my waves can stay for up to three to four days, which is considered a long time for my hair type. The waves also look so natural that they give me that “just permed my hair” effect. Priano is a fan of the tool too, saying, “It heats evenly and creates long-lasting waves with a sleek finish.” Plus, it has tons of features like a two-hour auto shutoff and a clear digital display.

You may have seen this on social media, and for good reason: At under $50, it is a great option for budget-conscious users. It’s also highly rated for both long and short hair and is easy (and safe!) for beginners to use because it has no exposed barrel. We also love that it comes in 1- and 1.25-inch wave sizes, so you can choose the wand that best fits your length and style preference.

Priano also swears by Trademark Beauty’s double-barrel wave iron. “This one’s loved for its wider barrels and even heat distribution,” she says. “It’s great for longer hair or when you want that soft glam wave.” As a bonus, the tool also works well for shorter hair as it creates two waves rather than the usual three.

This is my go-to hair waver at the moment because it’s so versatile. You can adjust the barrel size to get the exact wave you want, from tight to soft and beachy. (And you also have full control over the temperature.) I’m super lazy, so I like to opt for something that has full capabilities in one.

You can’t beat this price, which makes Bed Head’s tool a great entry-level styler. That, along with its tourmaline ceramic barrels, 10 heat settings, and easy digital controls, makes it super straightforward to use. The barrels themselves are also on the large side, which creates a great beachy effect in the final look. And if you’re accident-prone, take comfort in the fact that it comes with a protective glove, too.

“This waver is pricey, at nearly $200, but it’s also the most versatile. It comes with three barrels—a waver, a rod, and a clamp—so you can create any type of wave or curl your heart desires,” says Bazaar beauty director Jenna Rosenstein. “I have thick hair, and it works just as well on my hair as it does on those with fine, thin hair, since the heat is customizable up to 450 degrees. It’s also great to travel with!”

This one is another Bazaar editor favorite: “While I do own two different hair wavers, they both serve a unique vibe. I love this INH Triple Barrel when I’m going for a looser, more lived-in wave,” says Sarah Olivieri, Bazaar senior designer. “It really gives the illusion of salty beach hair freshly dried from the sun.”

“I love the look of effortless waves, but creating seamless waves without creases or harsh demarcations between crimps can be tricky,” says Bazaar beauty market and commerce editor Katie Intner. “I love the jumbo Mane waver because the large barrel size gives a more natural wave and crimps hair in under a few minutes. Plus, it’s dual voltage, which is great for travel, and gives a surprising amount of wave longevity.” Even better, the base is detachable and can be used with any of the brand’s other hair tool attachments.

This waver is on the pricey side, but that’s because it has the brand’s next-gen features. Think: even heating and a curved—not cylindrical—shape on the barrels that gives that ideal “effortless” look. They’re also finished with an anodized coating that adds shine and reduces frizz. Who doesn’t want that?

If you aren’t a bona fide hairstyling expert and want a hair waver that doesn’t require much technique, this option from WavyTalk might be a good fit. It has a built-in timer you can set from four to 10 seconds that beeps when it’s time to move on to the next section of hair. Its deep V-shaped plates and dual-barrel design sculpt hair for a more pronounced wave.

Luckily, it’s “fairly straightforward,” Priano says. “You start by sectioning your hair; the smaller the section, the more defined the wave. Then, working from the roots down, clamp the waver onto the hair and hold for a few seconds, usually 5 to 10, depending on your hair type and heat setting. Move down the same strand, overlapping slightly to continue the wave pattern all the way to the ends. Repeat throughout the entire head, and once cooled, you can brush it out for a softer, more lived-in texture or leave it defined for more drama.”

What’s the Best Way to Prep Your Hair Before Using a Waver?

To minimize potential damage, you’ll want to do a few things first: “Always start with clean, dry hair; wavers are not meant for wet styling. I recommend applying a heat protectant from mid-lengths to ends to shield the hair from damage,” Priano adds. “If you’re looking to boost volume and hold, add a lightweight texturizing spray or a mousse before blow-drying. And don’t forget to detangle. Smooth, tangle-free strands help the waver glide better and create more uniform results.”

Is There a Difference Between a Waver and a Curling Iron?

“The main difference between a hair waver and a curling iron lies in the shape and result. A curling iron wraps hair around a single barrel, creating more classic, rounded curls or ringlets, depending on the size of the barrel and the technique,” Priano says. “A waver, on the other hand, presses the hair between barrels to form deep, wave-like ridges.”

#6717. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

#6716. The 12 Best Round Brushes for a Salon-Level Blowout

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Perfect your next hair look with these expert-loved picks

Perfecting an at-home blowout once required contortionist-level abilities combined with a little patience and a lot of skill, especially when styling the back of your head. But nowadays, thanks to the advancement in hair tools—from high-powered blow-dryers to one of the best round brushes—it's easier than ever to craft sleek styles that rival your local salon without dealing with arm fatigue.

Unlike regular boar-bristle or vented brushes, round brushes feature a cylindrical shape in which hair wraps around for a seamless dry. Though many of these tools are crafted from similar materials, they're also available in a range of sizes to suit your hair texture and unique beauty needs. But regardless of the brush you choose, experts say a polished dry isn't achieved with dripping wet hair. "The key is to get all the moisture out," celebrity hairstylist Priscilla Valles tells Bazaar. "To achieve the smooth look, you need to keep drying until the hair is completely dry."

New York City-based hairstylist Jackson Simmonds recommends having two to three round brushes in your collection—just in case you change your hairstyle. “To keep it simple, think, smaller brushes for shorter lengths, bigger brushes for longer lengths,” he explains.

Here, we've narrowed down some of the best round brushes from Oribe, Sisley Paris, and more, all highly recommended by experts. Whether your hair is short, extra long, curly, or super fine, these top picks can be game-changing to help deliver a bouncy, full-bodied blowout from the comfort of your bathroom.

The bristles also aren’t overly stiff, so they won’t scratch your scalp as you’re styling. And we’re always here for a no-slip grip!

These U.K.-made brushes are celebrity stylist Benjamin Mohapi’s personal favorites. “They are the oldest brush company in the world, and I've used them for the entire 40 years of my career,” he explains, adding that the brand has options in boar bristle, vegan bristle, and nylon ceramic. “As far as I’m concerned, there is nothing better.”

Bristle type: nylonBarrel material: ceramicSize options: 45mm and 60mm

Here's a brush from the experts at Sisley Paris that's literally made for your next blowout. Its boar and nylon bristles are gentle yet effective, and compactly housed in a wooden barrel. The soft bristles provide hold for easy drying and also keep the hair super soft. We also appreciate that it has scalp-stimulating bristles, so it feels especially nice when you use it at the root.

Bristle type: boar, nylonBarrel material: woodSize options: medium

This popular round brush proves that getting a polished, salon-level finish doesn't have to cost a ton. Ideal for reducing frizz, the staple uses a ceramic-coated barrel for even heat distribution. Plus, it features an ergonomic handle for extra comfort, which you’ll love whether you’re new to blowing out your hair at home or just need the extra grip. Once you get the hang of things, you may decide to upgrade, but it’s a great starter brush.

The three sturdy knobs on the handle of this round brush mean you don't have to worry about the tool accidentally slipping out of your hands mid-blow-dry. Plus, the placement of the bristles in Philip B.'s tool makes it easy to give your roots an extra lift. With a mix of boar bristles and nylon for extra reinforcement, it’s a sturdy brush that’s earned stellar reviews for good reason.

Simmonds recommends the Japanese-made brand, saying that it offers “quality brushes that deliver results, look great coming out of my kit, and last for ages with proper care.” The mix of boar bristle and heat-resistant nylon pins is also ideal for optimal hold and shine.

Bristle type: syntheticBarrel material: ceramic coatingSize options: 25mm–53mm

Like anything worth learning, styling your hair with one of the best round brushes takes practice. "Ask your stylist if they’ll do a lesson," offers Gamez. It also might be helpful to reference a YouTube or TikTok tutorial. The general guidance is: start fresh out of the shower, detangle your hair, and then begin blow-drying and brushing small sections of hair, working your way around your head. You can start in the front or back sections. Professional hair colorist Tylor Johnson recommends starting with the front sections around your hairline, as your hairline pieces are the most important and require the most precision, and you don't want to be fatigued when styling them.

What Are the Benefits of Using a Round Brush and Blow-Dryer?

It’s all about the shape. “The round brush allows the hair to wrap around it, creating a bend in the hair as it dries and cools,” explains Mohapi, who explains that a regular hairbrush should not be used for heat-styling. “Expensive brushes like Masons are not designed for direct heat, and you will ruin a perfectly beautiful brush,” says the stylist.

Several top stylists prefer to use round brushes and blow-dryers separately rather than an all-in-one device, as it enhances control while minimizing hair damage. "Blow-dry brushes tend to be high heat, so if you’re putting that tool on soaking wet hair, you’re going to overdry and damage it," explains Gamez. "A blow-dryer allows you to create space between the heat and the hair shaft as needed. You can control the air direction of a dryer with your nozzle, pointing downward and sealing the cuticle, resulting in shinier, smoother hair, as opposed to a hot brush that blows air outward in all directions."

On the other hand, blow-dry round brushes definitely have their benefits. According to Valles, one key factor is their overall ease of use to craft salon-worthy styles at home. "A blow-dry brush makes the process so much easier," Valles says. "Some people aren't as coordinated handling a brush and dryer simultaneously, but the blow-dry brush gives the same results and is easier to manage." At the end of the day, the best round brush for you is one you actually enjoy using regularly.

“Many people wouldn’t want to use a boar bristle brush for ethical reasons, and there are lots of good alternatives, so boar bristle isn’t the only option,” explains Mohapi. “It is, however, a highly effective material and is used extensively in hairbrush manufacturing globally.” Here’s why: “Boar bristles are highly tolerant of heat and won’t melt or buckle under the intense extremes of a packed Friday at the salon. The fibers are both strong and flexible and have the unique quality of being able to almost grab the hair and hold it in place as you pass it through the brush.”

That said, the stylist adds that some synthetics are nearly as good. They just may not last as long. “Nylon is a different thing altogether,” he notes. “Nylon will close down the cuticle and help create a shiny, slick finish to the hair. This is great for straight hair, but nylon brushes take all the body out of the hair, so when you use a nylon round brush, it has a different type of finish.”

#6713. This Charming 1960’s Alabama Cottage Unites Classic Southern Style With Family Heirlooms

There’s room for everyone, grandchildren included.

The best houses keep family close, whether that means an expansive living room that fits all the siblings and their spouses, room for another generation under the same roof, or grandparents just a few minutes down the road. For Leigh and Kevin Misso, founders of River Brook Design & Construction, that sentiment became the starting point for a deeply personal project: transforming a dated 1960s house in Mountain Brook, Alabama, into a new home for Leigh's mother and stepfather, who moved to be closer to their grandchildren.

At 2,600 square feet, the three-bedroom house wasn’t particularly grand, but that was precisely its appeal. “We wanted to embrace its modest scale rather than fight against it,” says Leigh. The couple undertook a complete renovation, adding square footage, vaulting ceilings, and reimagining the once-cramped kitchen as an airy gathering place. Throughout, the goal was to preserve the home’s cottage-like charm while giving it the breathing room to accommodate a growing family. “Every detail was thoughtfully considered to make this a place where children and grandchildren feel at home, generations can gather, and meaningful family traditions and memories can be made for years to come,” adds Leigh.

The resulting interiors are crisp and refined, while still holding decades of family history. Leigh’s grandmother's antique sideboard anchors the dining room, while her wicker sofa finds new life on the sunroom porch, dressed in blue-and-white Les Touches fabric. A four-poster bed passed down through her stepfather’s family takes pride of place in the primary bedroom. Elsewhere, mustard velvet, richly colored built-ins, and playful painted stripes add a fresh perspective to traditional Southern decorating.

Of course, the house’s greatest luxury might be its expansive rear porch, where a custom hanging daybed beckons, and where Leigh’s mother spends much of her time. It was one of the renovation’s biggest investments, but for a family whose entire project was predicated on spending more time together, the payoff was worth it. “Ultimately, our goal was to create a home that feels just as beautiful and comfortable for quiet everyday moments as it does when the entire family comes together,” says Leah.

FAST FACTSDesigner: River Brook Design & ConstructionLocation: Mountain Brook, AlabamaThe Space: A three-bedroom, two-and-a-half-bathroom home across 2,600 square feet.

“This is one of my favorite little rooms in the house,” says Leigh of the family room, which doubles as a play space. “We wanted to create a cozy, intimate retreat that felt full of personality, warmth, and charm. I love that we embraced the smaller scale of the space rather than trying to make it feel larger. The result is a room that feels incredibly inviting, layered, and collected.”

Vaulted ceilings and an exposed wood beam give this modestly sized living room an impressive sense of scale. Below, Leigh and Kevin balanced a tailored Coley Home sofa with dark upholstered seating, antique armchairs, and a Louis Philippe mirror.

“Ultimately, we wanted this room to embody the charm of the entire home—small in scale but never small in feeling,” says Leigh. “It’s a space that feels elegant enough for entertaining yet relaxed enough for grandchildren to curl up on the sofa. That balance of beauty, comfort, and livability is always at the heart of our designs.”

Proof that the smallest corners can have the biggest personalities, this charming game nook was designed with the grandchildren in mind. “I especially love the juxtaposition of the modern white pedestal table with the rich mustard velvet chairs,” adds Leigh. “The sculptural shapes and playful color bring a fresh energy to the space, while the gallery wall of collected artwork adds history, personality, and a sense of nostalgia.”

The sunroom acts a bit like a family scrapbook, compiling a slew of heirloom and inherited pieces into a comfortable place to unwind. “We wanted this space to feel as though it had been collected over generations rather than decorated all at once. It's comfortable, timeless, and a little whimsical—the perfect place for grandchildren to gather, family to visit, and everyday memories to be made.”

“I love how this dining room feels intimate, layered, and full of personality. We wanted to create a space that felt a little more formal and collected, while still being warm and inviting for everyday family gatherings,” says Leigh of the gathering space, which includes an antique sideboard that used to belong to her grandmother. “The mix of family heirlooms, traditional patterns, and unexpected pops of color gives the room a sense of history without feeling overly serious.”

A far cry from the home’s original 1960s kitchen, this reimagined space pairs vaulted ceilings with traditional, furniture-inspired details. A built-in china cabinet lends a sense of old-world formality, while marble countertops, warm wood floors, and oversized pendants keep the room feeling polished. Behind the scenes, a concealed coffee bar and appliance cabinet ensure everyday necessities don't interrupt the view.

Sleepovers get their own stylish headquarters in the grandchildren's bunk room, where the team channeled a playful and imaginative attitude. “One of my favorite details is the painted stripe treatment that wraps the walls and ceiling,” says Leigh. “Rather than using wallpaper, we chose to paint the stripes, creating a custom look that adds depth, dimension, and a wonderful sense of enclosure.”

The heart of the primary bedroom is an eye-catching antique four-poster bed, which has been pass down through several generations of Leigh’s family. “It’s such a special piece, and I love that it brings a genuine sense of history and heritage to the space,” she adds. “Rather than designing the room around something new, we embraced this treasured family heirloom and allowed it to become the focal point.”

Leigh and Kevin Misso are the husband-and-wife founders of River Brook, a Birmingham, Alabama–based design-build studio specializing in thoughtfully crafted homes that balance timeless architecture with modern livability. Together, the duo brings a refined yet approachable perspective to residential design, emphasizing traditional details, layered interiors, and spaces that feel collected rather than decorated.

#6711. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

#6710. Mary Lovelace O’Neal Broke the Rules of Black Art

Marianne Boesky Gallery in New York mounts the late abstract artist’s first posthumous show, “Requiem”

Mary Lovelace O’Neal moved to New York in the late 1960s at the height of the Black Arts movement. The Mississippi-born artist enrolled at Columbia’s School of the Arts in 1968, where she honed her distinct take on abstract-expressionist painting. Blazing a trail as one of the few Black women in this field, she faced criticism from her peers, who felt her work should engage more directly with the political issues of the time, that it was not “Black enough.” She responded with her so-called “lampblack” paintings: radical pitch-black abstractions made with carbon soot, allowing her to retain her expressionistic sensibility while answering the social question of Black art.

But this response to socially conscious art through minimalism never fully defined her career. Lovelace O’Neal relocated to San Francisco after graduating from Columbia, where she reignited her exploration of color and abstraction. Her black color fields “unzipped,” as she called it, to reveal new, kaleidoscopic abstractions. By the 1980s and through the ’90s, Lovelace O’Neal developed her idiosyncratic expressionistic style, often meditating on tangible subjects through obscure, gestural paintings. Requiem for a Daffodil (1997), a six-by-11-foot painting, embodies this bold, high-energy approach. It features floral forms, from yellow bouquets to a field of delicate white flowers running across the top border, gathered together for what can only be understood as a funeral. Lovelace O’Neal’s vigorous brushstrokes streak across the canvas, yet the work retains a sensitivity, a delicate and caring touch for the subjects that populated her abstract works. It is perhaps the best representation of the middle part of her six-decade career. These two decades are the subject of the appropriately titled “Requiem” at New York’s Marianne Boesky Gallery, her first posthumous show on view through December 19.

“The painting Requiem for a Daffodil, to me, is an expression of the depth to which Mary could feel for her subjects,” gallerist Marianne Boesky tells Bazaar. “A funeral dedicated to a flower, attended by flowers, delivered with such solemnity, is a reflection of Mary’s acceptance and understanding of the loss of something beautiful, innocent, fleeting.”

Lovelace O’Neal died on May 10 at her home in Mérida, Mexico, at 84. Born in Mississippi and raised in Arkansas, she left home in 1960 to study under David Driskell at Howard University in Washington, D.C., before briefly attending Maine’s Skowhegan School of Painting and Sculpture in 1963. At this school, she devoted herself to abstract expressionism, studying the work of Franz Kline and Willem de Kooning.

Lovelace O’Neal lived what can only be called an exciting life, documented through archival material in the back room of Boesky Gallery. While living in California, she also traveled the world, collecting memories and experiences on jaunts from South America to Africa that informed her paintings. Panthers with Socks (1985–90) epitomized this process; the frenetic abstraction of a leaping white cat draws on both her 20s in New York and her 1984 trip to Morocco.

“In Panthers with Socks, the brushy figure of this leaping animal makes for a clear allusion to the Black Panther Party and the artist’s involvement in the Civil Rights Movement,” Boesky explains, “while the sights, sounds, and memories of the artist’s time in Morocco inspire the bold colors and theatrical architecture of the composition.”

Although Lovelace O’Neal leaned away from overt political work during the 1960s, her abstract painting never abandoned concerns about race and notions of Blackness. One example in the show, Juke Joint (1997), referenced the Jim Crow–era establishments where Black communities gathered, places Mary witnessed firsthand growing up in the southern United States. The gestural work conveys the movement of dancing legs, evoking the free spirit of these establishments.

The ’80s and ’90s were largely defined by her life as a teacher. The artist joined the Department of Art Practice at the University of California, Berkeley, as a professor in the ’70s and received tenure in 1985. “I wanted to pay tribute to her as both an epic painter and as a lifelong teacher,” Boesky says. To do so, the gallerist intentionally paired Lovelace O’Neal’s show with Michaela Yearwood-Dan, a 32-year-old Black abstract painter, who opened her solo show, “A Woman of Will,” the same night.

“[When I first saw Mary’s paintings,] it was really the first time I felt like I had really come across a female Black abstract painter who felt uncontained,” Yearwood-Dan tells Bazaar. “There was a real sense of freedom and lack of control, but in an intentional, free-spirited way.”

By juxtaposing these two shows, Boesky facilitates a cross-generational dialogue between the two artists, who, despite admiring each other’s work, never met in person. “Mary admired the energy and color in Michaela’s abstract paintings and quickly grasped Michaela’s commitment to activism, which also underpinned her paintings,” Boesky says. “Michaela had known about and followed Mary’s work, unbeknownst to me, at the time I began working with both of them. Completely different generations and geographies, but kindred spirits nonetheless. It felt like kismet to present their shows side by side.”

That the work still resonates across generations underscores Lovelace O’Neal’s lasting power. The paintings on view at Boesky reveal how the artist used abstraction to layer unmitigated expression and feeling into her subjects, from people and animals to place and memory. “She understood early that ideas and emotions had the potential to be conveyed more powerfully through abstraction than through identifiable narration,” Boesky says. Viewers can catch glimpses of a pig in What’s time to a pig (c. 1990s) or potted plants in A Tree Branch (c. 1990-91) amid the frenzied mark-making. Boesky says these images “stampede across a canvas or hover like a dream.”

Lovelace O’Neal offers a sneak peek into her dreams—her mind—through these paintings. The beauty of “Requiem” is that it compiles works from the undeniable turning point of her career. The works still feature the lampblack foundation while also capturing how color furiously reemerged in her practice. Seen for the first time since her passing, the work remains as potent as ever, a product of a life devoted to her idiosyncratic vision.

“She referred to the act of painting as labor, her life’s labor,” Boesky says. “And she referred to the paintings as her children, the product of that labor, put out into the world to make their own mischief.”

#6705. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

#6703. Even a Gay Assassin Can’t Escape the Damsel-in-Distress Trope on Black Doves

It’s not *better* but at least it’s different?

In many ways, Black Doves is a platonic love story between a spy, Helen (Keira Knightley), and an assassin, Sam (Ben Whishaw). But the Netflix series has plenty of room for sex and romance. You’d expect that, right? It wouldn’t be a good spy story without some kind of spice. But while Black Doves plays along with a lot of familiar spy romance tropes, it does one thing differently: it does them with men. Spoilers for Black Doves season 2 ahead.

When you watch a lot of movies and television shows within the same genre, whether for work, continuing education, or just pure love of the game, you tend to pick up on some common tropes. Sometimes, like becoming aware of a friend’s bad habits for the first time, it’s annoyingly difficult to un-notice them.

One of my least favorite tropes in the action genre, or set of tropes really, has to do with how the spy and/or assassin’s love interest invariably gets treated. These women, because they’re practically always women, are kept in the dark and therefore lack agency. The romances are inherently toxic because they lack trust! Then, the second the bad guys learn about them, these women get kidnapped or killed or otherwise put in danger to motivate and/or devastate the male hero. Boo, I say! Boring! Sexist, even! These women are not characters. They are plot devices.

In Black Doves season 1, we learn that Sam was once in a relationship with a man named Michael (Omari Douglas) that he maintained by lying about what he did for a living. When Sam’s real job follows him home, so to speak, Michael is put in a traumatic and distressing situation. Someone is shooting at him, his boyfriend is a stone-cold killer... and his boyfriend lied to him. When Michael returns, briefly, in season 2, it’s the last thing that seems to have sucked the most.

Then, in season 2, Sam tries something safer. He engages in a sexual situationship with a hotel concierge named Jerry (Samuel Barnett). On paper, it’s perfect for someone like him. Jerry is into him, but not too much. There’s no chance of them being seen in public together, because he works at the same place they hook up. It’s genuinely sweet to see Jerry try to get Sam to open up. But the second that Sam does, and invites him on a proper date, the bad guys swoop in and use Jerry as leverage. Not only does the poor concierge die for the plot, but Sam is forced to fridge his own love interest.

Is this now homophobic, instead of sexist? I would tentatively say no, for a few reasons. I covered the “Bury Your Gays” moment circa 2016, and I don’t think this qualifies there either. You can’t really say that the show thinks gay men don’t have agency, and are only fit to be plot devices when Sam, a gay man, is co-lead. And if Sam was the only queer character in the series, his inability to find happiness might be a problem; but two other assassins, Williams (Ella Lily Hyland) and Eleanor (Gabrielle Creevy), are both women and gleefully infatuated with each other. The “murder lesbians,” as another character on the show decided to label them, are in the least toxic romantic relationship on Black Doves. So, that’s something!

For what it’s worth, Helen’s husband in the series, Wallace (Andrew Buchan), is also kept in the dark for his own protection and then put in danger to motivate Helen at the end of season 2. Plus, her extramarital love interest in season 1, Jason (Andrew Koji), was quite literally killed for the plot. This really is a dangerous show for men. It’s about damn time? Forget I said that.

It is darkly refreshing (now that we’re in Woke 3, I’ve decided we can call things refreshing again) to see these tropes done on Black Doves in a way that balances the scales. I found the dozens of articles explaining why women feel safe watching “M/M romance” like Heated Rivalry to be condescending at times, and therefore I’m hesitant to admit that it’s nice for me as a woman to watch a story with these tropes that so many men seem to enjoy. I still don’t see the appeal. Nobody should be a plot device, regardless of gender. But I’m slightly less bored, and against all odds, rooting for Sam to find some peace and a lasting romance of his own.

#6702. These Are the Home Trends Gaining Ground for 2027, According to Zillow

A new analysis of millions of for-sale listings reveals the features sellers are increasingly highlighting.

Above: A color-drenched den on Long Island, New York, designed by General Assembly.

Homes are getting smarter, but sellers are also advertising places to unplug. Zillow’s 2027 Home Trends Report finds growing mentions of both AI and tech-free spaces, along with color-drenched rooms, home offices, and “catios.”

The report tracks year-over-year changes in mentions of hundreds of features across millions of for-sale listings. The results reflect how sellers and agents are marketing homes—not a survey of buyers or evidence that a particular feature will help a property sell. Still, they offer a look at the amenities and design details increasingly making their way into listing descriptions. Here are the main takeaways.

Mentions of AI are up 119 percent, the largest increase in this year’s report. Zillow points to AI-powered appliances among the features sellers are advertising, while mentions of app-controlled home systems rose 34 percent.

Spaces to unplug are also appearing 34 percent more often. The report describes an interest in both technology that simplifies daily tasks and designated places to take a break from it.

“Digital detox is the new luxury,” says Amanda Pendleton, Zillow’s home trends expert, in a statement. “Homes are getting smarter, but buyers also want tech-free zones where nothing needs charging. Sellers and their real estate agents are now highlighting spaces to unplug.”

Color-drenched spaces—rooms where the same color extends across the walls, ceiling, and trim—are up 84 percent in listing mentions, making them one of the report’s biggest increases. Zillow sees the approach as part of a move toward bolder, more polished interiors. Exterior finishes are gaining ground, too: painted brick is up 27 percent, and board-and-batten siding rose 24 percent.

Color drenching takes a shade beyond the walls and onto the ceiling, trim, and other architectural details. The approach has even spawned a bathroom variation: tile drenching, which extends the same tile across surfaces for a continuous look.

The language used to describe these homes is changing alongside the features. Mentions of “elevated” rose 71 percent, while “cozy” fell 22 percent, “eclectic” dropped 19 percent, and “boho” declined 13 percent. Cozy remains one of the most common words in listings, but it’s appearing less frequently than it did a year ago.

Kitchen language is moving in the other direction. Mentions of “chef’s kitchens” fell 21 percent, and “gourmet kitchens” declined 15 percent.

Mentions of multigenerational living jumped 58 percent. Zillow connects the increase to homes accommodating the “sandwich generation,” or people caring for children and older family members.

Features that can serve different ages and needs are also appearing more frequently. Grab bars are up 18 percent, wider doorways are up 13 percent, and nurseries rose 9 percent. The report places these changes alongside the growing emphasis on flexible rooms and spaces with more than one use.

The home spa is changing. Mentions of spa-inspired bathrooms rose 60 percent, and cold plunge pools increased 44 percent. Jacuzzis and jetted tubs, meanwhile, each fell about 9 percent.

Spaces for exercise are also gaining ground. Home gyms are up 18 percent, while golf simulators rose 29 percent. Together, the findings cover both workout spaces and amenities marketed around rest and recovery, rather than a single version of the home wellness room.

Cats get a category of their own this year. “Catios”—screened-in outdoor spaces that let indoor cats spend time outside—are appearing in 32 percent more listings. A place to get some air, watch the birds, and keep an eye on the neighbors—the cat has a wish list, too.

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#6699. Looking for a New Winter Perfume? These 4 Zara Vanilla Scents Smell So Expensive

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

There’s a reason vanilla perfumes are so popular. They’re no longer the sickly-sweet, sugar-loaded compositions you might remember. Today they’re much more complex—the creaminess of the note is balanced with more sophisticated whiffs of florals, woods, and amber. This new generation of vanilla-forward fragrances feels rounded, full-bodied, and, perhaps most importantly, expensive.

Sure, many of these complex scents come with a hefty price tag. But luckily, Zara has an entire collection dedicated to these deep vanilla perfumes, and there is an array of options that are all ideal for a comforting winter scent. With every spritz, you’ll smell how distinct each one is. In some, vanilla sits quietly at the base, gently rounding off sweeter or floral notes. In others, it takes center stage, creating a creamy, decadent throw.

Whether you like your vanilla subtle and soft for an everyday spritz, or bold and woody for after-hours, there’s a budget-friendly Zara perfume calling your name.

Notes: Chantilly cream, jasmine, sweet tonkaMood: A gourmand scent that’s indulgent and warm but never sickly

The truest vanilla of them all, Dark Romance is the place to start if you’re after a traditionally sweet, creamy—but not sickly—vanilla fragrance. The base note is sweet tonka, which creates that toasty, caramel-y base that sits on your skin, while notes of jasmine and Chantilly cream linger with a velvety, milky sillage. It’s the perfect everyday fragrance; one we envision misting on a cashmere sweater on a chilly night.

Notes: Apricot blossom, jasmine sambac, vanillaMood: A floral vanilla that’s creamy and comforting

Zara’s Hypnotic Vanilla smells exactly as it sounds. Decadent from the first spritz, it instantly envelops you in this golden, buttery cloud of vanilla. Initially, it’s sweet, but then the apricot blossom and jasmine sambac come through, giving it a floral and slightly powdery edge. If Dark Romance is the one you’d wear for a cozy night in, Hypnotic Vanilla is its older sister you’ll reach for for a night out. It’s a great option if you can never choose between gourmands and florals.

Notes: Salty coconut, jasmine, vanilla bloom, amberMood: A full-bodied gourmand that’s creamy, sweet, a little spicy, and seductive

With Immortal Vanilla, you’ll be surprised it only costs $39. While Zara’s perfumes have impressive sillage, this one truly does linger, so you’re bound to receive many compliments. On paper, it has notes of coconut, jasmine, amber, and vanilla. But IRL, they come together to create something that’s comforting, sensual, and ever so slightly creamy and salty. After one spritz, you’ll want to douse yourself in it again, and again, and again.

Notes: Wild orchid, black amber, vanilla Mood: A deep, sophisticated scent that balances vanilla with a woody base

If you want your vanilla perfume to smell like an old-fashioned, New York-style jazz bar, Daring Vanilla is for you. Of the four scents, this is the one that comes closest to a traditional oud fragrance, with naturally smoky, woody notes balanced by wild orchid and vanilla, which lend a warm, subtly sweet throw. It’s the kind of fragrance you’d slip over an LBD, slide into some killer high heels and head out for the night.

#6698. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

A new report from Caylent, an AI-focused Amazon Web Services Premier Tier Services Partner, found that enterprises are already moving agentic AI beyond pilots and into production environments. At the same time, organizations are putting strict conditions around autonomy, making governance and control the next major challenge for enterprise AI adoption.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research from Digital Realty.

#6697. Make the Skirt Your Fall Personality

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Thanks to Prada, the skirt has never felt fresher

The collection seen around the world this year was, by all accounts, Prada’s Spring/Summer 2027 show, which served as something of an edict on the skirt. Composed of 63 looks, each featuring a distinct iteration in varying colors and fabrications, the presentation was nothing short of a skirt master class from Miuccia Prada. Following that spectacle, you would have been hard-pressed not to spot a skirt on nearly every runway, be it the gold-leaf pencil skirts at Saint Laurent or the many offerings from Julian Klausner’s Dries Van Noten, which brought to light another unsung hero as of late: the ocelot print!

Though the silhouette felt decidedly new under the glamour of the spring collections, the truth of the matter is that skirts made a similar splash during the fall shows. However, the garment itself wasn’t necessarily the prevailing message. Rather, fall/winter 2026 signaled a return to a more considered approach to dressing, with perhaps no better champion of this sensibility than Matthieu Blazy at Chanel, whose interpretation of the wardrobe staple helped make it feel novel once again.

Of course, as temperatures drop, the natural inclination is to reach for clothing that provides maximum coverage, which often means an onslaught of pants, whether in heavy wool or denim. However, all hope is not lost for the garment of the moment as we inch towards brisker weather. The key to making it work during the colder months comes down to a thoughtful approach to layering, as well as opting for styles in substantial fabrics that can stand up to the chill.

Below, you’ll find eight skirt styles, like those in leather or denim, and how to wear them for everything from weekend adventures to boardroom meetings without feeling too buttoned-up.

Perhaps Matthieu Blazy’s design prowess could be likened to the Midas touch, because just as he has helped bring the skirt back into relevance, so too has he turned to the quarter-zip. Channel a bit of Chanel’s Métiers d’Art 2026 collection by pairing an oatmeal-colored cashmere-blend quarter-zip with a tartan skirt adorned with fringe, and introduce some daytime drama with a pair of shoulder-duster earrings. To finish things off, reach for croc-embossed Mary Jane pumps and a stark white shoulder bag for just the right amount of ladies-who-lunch panache.

Loewe Scarf Mini Knotted Leather Shoulder Bag

Top everything off with a cropped puffer jacket featuring an aviator-inspired shearling collar, a detail echoed in the retro sensibility of a pair of shield sunglasses.

Tom Ford Bronson Acetate Pilot Sunglasses

Polo Ralph Lauren Matte Woven Down Bomber Jacket

Though Western dressing seems to be an ever-present trend, there’s no denying that the aesthetic has become increasingly dominant in recent seasons. To channel it without looking like an extra in a rodeo show, the key is to mix hallmarks of the style, such as suede and turquoise accents, be it in the form of a concho belt or jewelry, with more everyday pieces. Consider a vintage-inspired gray hoodie and croc-embossed high-shaft boots that evoke the old West without feeling overwrought. Finish with a cropped bomber jacket in a similar hue to the skirt to create the impression of a matching set.

However, don’t be afraid to go against the grain and embrace a more saturated palette, particularly with holiday gatherings on the horizon. To temper the abundance of color, introduce touches of white in the form of a topcoat and retro-inspired kitten heels finished with a bow, lending the outfit a welcome sense of balance.

The Frankie Shop Gaia Double-Breasted Coat

Give the traditional prep-school uniform a contemporary twist by pairing an A-line denim skirt with a striped long-sleeve polo shirt and an unconventional gray wool blazer with double lapels. A leather skirt can, at times, feel a bit severe, so consider softening its appearance with your go-to white T-shirt and a V-neck sweater in a complementary shade of gray. For a downtown cool factor, reach for a pair of slip-on loafers in lieu of heels or a boot. Finish with a statement cuff for added polish.

Majestic Filatures Cotton Crewneck T-Shirt

A utilitarian skirt turns traditional workwear on its head, but pair it with familiar staples, such as a striped monk-collar shirt and a knitted vest, both rendered in unexpected color combinations. These patterns are statement makers on their own, which is precisely why they work so well next to understated pieces. Consider styling them with a black short-sleeved cashmere T-shirt and wool cardigan, which allows the print to command attention without overwhelming. #6695. A Magical Room Designed from a Kid’s Point of View

See how IKEA finds can make a child’s space dream a little bigger and work a little harder.

Above, watch as House Beautiful Associate Shopping Editor Janae McKenzie shows how IKEA makes it easy to design a room that works for both kids and grown-ups. The secret? Think at their level. When furniture and storage are scaled to a child’s height, everyday tasks become opportunities for independence.
