And don’t underestimate the power of a small hideaway corner. The KURA reversible bed can be raised to create a nook that becomes a private retreat for reading, playing, or simply letting the imagination run wild. The coordinating screen can be added to create a cozy, secret hideaway (and tuck any mess out of sight) or removed when it’s time to open up the playspace.

The best kids’ rooms create magic without sacrificing practicality. Watch the video to see how to bring this idea to life with IKEA finds that make room for both.

#6694. ‘DTWS’ Fans Are Divided Over Connor Wood and Rylee Arnold’s “Recycled” Dance-Off Choreography

Was it a clever callback or just too familiar?

I never thought I’d say this, but this week’s episode of Dancing With the Stars was a tough watch. Hear me out—it was only because my faves, Maura Higgins and Connor Wood, competed in a bonus dance-off to Sheryl Crow’s “All I Wanna Do.” I just couldn’t choose between these charming celebs. Sue me!

That being said, it was a tight race between the stars and their pro partners, Rylee Arnold and Mark Ballas. Team Tink won in the end thanks to two extra points from the judging panel, but DWTS fans seem divided over who really deserved the win after the scores were given.

“ANYONE who thought connor had a chance i hope you're feeling real embarrassed MAURA ATE HIM UP SOOOO HARD,” one person wrote, while another said, “I don’t even care that they lost (whatever) but I like watching Rylee and Connor WAYYY more.”

After the performance, Carrie Ann Inaba criticized Rylee and Connor for incorporating more country-inspired moves into their cha-cha.

“So much fun, both of you! It’s like the battle of fringe dance,” she said, before telling Connor and Rylee, “You had a lot of country line dancing. You threw that back in, which was fun for the audience, but I don’t know. It’s not a cha-cha.”

Naturally, fans took issue with Carrie Ann’s comments. “What’s the point of picking a country song if you don’t want them to incorporate that into their dance. The feedback made no sense,” another viewer said.

If that wasn’t enough to keep the debate alive, others pointed out how similar Rylee and Connor’s dance was to their week 1 cha-cha to another country song, “I Like It, I Love It” by Tim McGraw.

However, longtime fans know that repurposing choreography is the name of the game when it comes to DWTS.

One commenter on Reddit explained, “Truth be told, all the pros recycle dances for the dance-offs, which is why the concept has never really worked for me or been particularly entertaining. However, those Hee Haw moves definitely threw the entire dance-off off, and I’m glad CAI called it out. You could tell she absolutely hated every second of it.”

Either way, it doesn’t seem like Connor or Maura were phased by the online chatter after their dance-off. They actually dropped a series of comments supporting each other’s performances.

“So much fun with you guys @fibula @ryleearnold1,” Maura wrote, to which Connor responded, “we had too much damn fun I love you all.”

The podcaster even jokingly chimed in when a fan claimed Maura “cleared” him, saying, “agree that’s my queen.”

And that’s why these two are my faves—can’t wait to see them in the semifinals next week!

#6691. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

John K. Waters is the editor in chief of a number of Converge360.com sites, with a focus on high-end development, AI and future tech. He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

A new report from Caylent, an AI-focused Amazon Web Services Premier Tier Services Partner, found that enterprises are already moving agentic AI beyond pilots and into production environments. At the same time, organizations are putting strict conditions around autonomy, making governance and control the next major challenge for enterprise AI adoption.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research from Digital Realty.

#6689. Spice Up Your Mani with Cinnamon Brown Nails This Fall

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

At this point, we all know that a brown manicure is a fall staple. Even as I type this right now, I’m wearing one that’s a week old (in DND Spiced Brown, if you’re wondering). Espresso nails? Love. Chocolate brown nails? Yep. Chai latte nails? A lighter way to do things. But, it turns out we haven’t exhausted every way to wear the shade quite yet. The latest brown look to get on my radar is cinnamon nails, the warm, spicy take on the fall-friendly color. And honestly, with the holidays around the corner, the smell of cinnamon is sure to be wafting through my kitchen for the next couple months, so the timing couldn’t be better.

These warm, spicy nails combine rich brown with subtle red and copper undertones for a cozy, cinnamon-inspired shade that feels tailor-made for autumn. Ahead, with the help of celebrity nail artist Stephanie Stone (she’s Sally Hansen’s nail style director and has worked with lots of your favorite stars—ahem, Charli XCX) I’ll break down everything you need to know about trending cinnamon brown nails, including plenty of inspo pics, tips for getting the look, and polish recommendations.

As the name suggests, cinnamon brown nails are inspired by the warm, reddish-brown hue of the spice itself. It’s warmer than a classic brown and less red than a terracotta, sitting right in between the two. While espresso and chocolate brown nails tend to lean darker and cooler, these bring some toasty and earthy vibes to your fingertips. While a classic creamy polish looks great in cinnamon, the shade also offers plenty of opportunities to play around with the finish, like with a dimensional velvet cat eye manicure or a chrome topper.

The latter is one of Stone’s favorite ways to wear the trend—she reaches for Sally Hansen’s Miracle Gel in Chics in Charge to get the effect. “Think the glazed donut’s cousin coming to visit for winter break,” she says. And especially apt for the holidays, you can add a little dazzle to the shade: “If you want to give them some extra sparkle, add a crystal at the base of your nails for an extra catch of light,” she adds.

Stone puts it perfectly: “Every great manicure starts with immaculate cuticle prep and nail care.” Before reaching for any polish, “trim and file your nails to their desired shape,” she says. “Then use Sally Hansen’s Push & Buff Cuticle Pen to clean your nail plate of an excess dead skin.” This helps clean up your cuticles easily and quickly. Next, “wash your hands with soap and water and wipe your nail beds with alcohol,” she adds, noting how important this final prep step is, especially if you’re doing a metallic finish. “Metallic shimmer and polishes expose any texture or dust you may have on your nails,” she explains.

Then, it’s polish time. This part is pretty straightforward: Paint your nails with two coats of your favorite beige or brown nail polish, she says, recommending a medium brown shade “if you want your nail look to be reminiscent of a cinnamon roll” or a reddish brown “if you want your nails more reminiscent of cinnamon as the spice itself.” After letting dry for a few minutes, add top coat and cuticle oil to finish things off. Need some product recs? Stone’s go-to polish is Sally Hansen’s Miracle Gel Polish in Been There, Dune That, but you can also get the look with Olive & June’s Nail Polish in Sassy Molasses or Essie’s Gel Couture Liquid Diamonds in Marquise Please too.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo. She’s covered and tested all things nails, from the coolest new polish colors to cuticle care and nail art trends.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

#6688. Inside Danielle Lauder’s Barbie Dream Closet

Designed by AMMOR Architecture, the ultra-pink, mega dressing room is a cinematic fantasy come to life.

Cher Horowitz had a touch-screen conveyer belt in her color-coded closet. Carrie Bradshaw kept her Manolos in an unusually spacious high-fashion-filled walk-in. And Margot Robbie’s Barbie displayed her hot-pink wardrobe like doll’s packaging in her wall-free fantasy villa. Danielle Lauder, the great-granddaughter of Estée Lauder, created her own iteration of the dream closet—this time, in real life.

Her own inspiration? “I always thought of Belle in Beauty and the Beast in the library, just rolling around on her ladder,” she says. “I’m not much of a reader, but as a collector, I knew that’s what I wanted.”

Her wish list for the jewel-box space also included curving walls, glossy pink lacquer, illuminated marble, and a capsule-shaped vanity inspired by the makeup collection she designed for Estée Lauder in 2019. The beauty empire was founded by—and named after—her great-grandmother in 1946; and her father, William P. Lauder, serves as the chairman of the board. (Danielle now oversees her own production company, Act 4 Artists.)

To make her dream a reality, Lauder turned to architects Goil Amornvivat and Thomas Morbitzer, founders of New York City–based AMMOR Architecture. The duo had instant chemistry with Lauder, who was especially taken with the meticulous diorama they created for her.

“Tom and I both love Barbie,” says Amornvivat. “So we tweaked our design process to make an architectural model that was more like a dollhouse, where you could switch out surfaces.” Their efforts worked on Lauder, herself a Barbie fan, and the pair walked away with the commission to not just create the dressing room but to overhaul the entire industrial-style apartment in the West Village of Manhattan, which was then being used as an art studio.

“They both really understood and saw my vision,” says Lauder. “Plus, they were the most excited, which made me more excited. Goil is like the Energizer Bunny.”

The 250-square-foot dressing room is, Lauder says, “the biggest room in the apartment besides the living room.” It’s also where Lauder spends the most time. She enters just off the primary bedroom via an arched passageway finished in custom bronze plaster, emerging from the dark portal into a pink fantasia where her expansive fashion collection is arrayed like a personal boutique. “The idea was for it to be part display area but also a functional closet,” she says.

At the center is a custom pink vanity outfitted with fluted drawers, dual-sided mirrors, and a top made of richly veined Midnight Rose marble, sourced from New York Stone. Lauder keeps her collection of vintage and reissued Estée Lauder compacts in the drawers. “I love collecting them and having them out on display or in a nice drawer,” says Lauder. “They’re beautiful little artifacts.”

On the curving walls, rounded niches display shelving and racks against illuminated stone, part of Lauder’s request for layered lighting. “I love the backlit marble,” she says. “It’s so flattering.” She keeps her enviable bag and shoe collection on permanent display, maintained with help from her housekeeper. Lauder draws from the selection on a daily basis, with particular affection for family heirlooms such as her mom’s vintage Dolce & Gabbana handbags.

Overhead, Amornvivat and Morbitzer devised a futuristic lighting installation, stretching mesh taut over an oval-shaped light source to create a seamless surface. “The light in there is controllable by color, and it's dimmable,” notes Morbitzer. “It’s kind of like a skylight, which was important because it’s the only room without windows,” adds Amornvivat.

The bespoke elements continue: heated floors, an ice maker, a perfume bar, plenty of drawers, and a pair of walk-in closets cleverly concealed behind matching lacquered doors. “The whole room is custom millwork,” says Amornvivat.

And Lauder did get the sliding ladder of her dreams. “That was my hill to die on,” she says. “I’ve always struggled to reach things in my closet, so it was definitely practical. And my grandfather had one in his apartment in the library when I was growing up, and I just loved it.”

Now that the closet is complete, Lauder has a hard time leaving. “I spend more time in here than I do probably even sleeping in bed,” she says with a laugh. The space is remarkably faithful to the original idea that Amornvivat and Morbitzer presented in their dollhouse diorama. “I wanted it to be totally mine, feminine and fun, and still very warm and inviting,” she says. “It’s become my ultimate bachelorette hub.”

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6685. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

A vendor coalition built on an Okta-developed blueprint is proposing a shared architecture for discovering, governing, monitoring, and containing enterprise AI agents.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

#6683. Front Office: Radhi Devlukia

The host of the A Really Good Cry podcast turned to designer Jess Cooney to create a home office-meets-studio made for deep conversations.

Radhi Devlukia isn’t afraid of tears. On her podcast, A Really Good Cry, the whole point is to get personal, and to do so with guests as varied as supermodel Miranda Kerr, dermatologists, hospice nurses, personal finance advisors—and Devlukia’s husband, fellow podcaster Jay Shetty.

Now, two years into the show, Devlukia, a best-selling author, cook, and founder of beverage company Juni, is unveiling a new studio and office space located within her and Shetty’s Los Angeles house. Devlukia turned to designer Jess Cooney, who has a namesake Berkshires-based interiors firm, to craft a space designed to support these really good cries. Cooney had never tackled a podcast studio before but was ready for the challenge, which included building a room that could both serve as a set—technical components, artificial light, and all—as well as a welcoming home office.

From the start, Cooney knew what the space would not include. “We wanted to move away from the tropes usually seen in podcast studios, like styled bookshelves or cluttered backgrounds that detract from the people being interviewed, and also anything logo or merch heavy,” she says. “And toward something that felt warmer and more inviting.”

Instead, she took cues from spaces like fashion designer Bella Freud’s London apartment, Jean Cocteau’s Villa Santo Sospir in the Côte d’Azur, and Paloma Elsesser’s home by Gregory Rockwell. The resulting office, says Cooney, “has this incredible womb-like, feminine atmosphere that cocoons and holds you.”

Below, Devlukia takes our Front Office survey, giving a behind-the-scenes look at her new home base.

Radhi Devlukia: The hanging art piece in the middle of the window, Kissed by Katie Burdon.

When you need to lock in and power through work, what’s your setup?

Doors open, facing the window, instrumental music playing. I also move areas a lot during work hours to stay focused, so sometimes I’m at the desk, sometimes on the swivel chairs, sometimes on the floor by the window!

I have articles printed about my grandma and grandad on my canvas memory wall. They taught at schools in Africa in the 1970s and there were articles written about how much they were doing for the community. I also have vintage ceramics—I love the mix of old and new.

It really depends. If there are two of us, we’re on the swivel chairs. If there are a few of us, we’re around the marble table. We love keeping things casual at the office so sometimes people sit on the cozy rug or wherever feels comfortable.

What was the best meeting you’ve had there?

We have had so many creative meetings in this office. It’s such a calming and flowing environment. The whole rebrand of the podcast was ideated in this new office, which I’m obsessed with now.

What would you change about it if you could?

Maybe just having the ability to shoot with the blinds open.

What’s the funniest thing that happened there?

My team loves to prank me, so there’s been a lot of laughter in our office!

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

We’re not showing up empty-handed this season.

It’s officially “what can I bring?” season. The next two months are packed with parties, family dinners, and white elephant gift exchanges. For every potluck, there’s at least one stressed-out host. So when you’re deciding what to bring—because you wouldn’t dare show up empty-handed—don’t forget about the person throwing the party. As the shopping-obsessed design editors we are, we’ve got you covered on the very best hostess gifts.

There's no better time to score the perfect present for your friends and family eager to put on a soirée. The good news is that anyone who likes to host does it often enough (not just around the holidays) to use your gifts at least a few times throughout the year.

The best gifts will do one of two things: make hosting easier, or help them relax when the festivities are over. Below, you’ll find over 30 picks that fall into these two camps, from genius kitchen supplies to a silk eye mask for resting after the party. What's more, if you fall into the hostess camp from time to time, you may even spot a few things your own home could use, like a French-country-inspired utensil crock or a set of pale green Champagne flutes. The humble doorstop. Rarely thought of, but essential for any host when guests are a revolving door. This quirky-yet-sturdy cast aluminum snail will help them leave the door open for guests to mill about with ease.

If the host is a dear friend or family member, remind them how much they mean to you with a thoughtful framed photo. This one is super versatile; it can be displayed horizontally or vertically. The center glass display rotates and gives the illusion of a floating image.

A utensil crock is one of those utilitarian things that everyone needs, but no one wants to buy for themselves. That's where you come in. Give them the item any cook needs: a place to store all their cooking utensils. Because it's out and on display, make sure it's cute. This one checks both boxes: It's attractive and big enough to store at least six or seven utensils.

No at-home fête is complete without an array of cheeses for which a set of knives is a must. This one, which includes five specialized knives, is a steal for $60. "I actually bought this set the first time I hosted my book club, and I have never looked back. I love that each handle is a slightly different shade of blue," says tester Jessica Cherner.

Nothing, and we mean nothing, ruins a beautiful tablescape like paper towels as napkins. It's like getting dressed up and wearing running shoes. If you've noticed your hostess ripping off pieces of paper towel when setting the table, get her a set of cloth dinner napkins that she can throw in the washing machine after the meal.

Calling all hostesses who make a mess in the kitchen: You're not alone. Lots of hostesses tend to cover their countertops in a light dusting of flour and splatter their brand-new white tees with red sauce. It happens to the best of us. Don't let it happen to the generous hostess who's cooking you dinner. Protect her look with this beautiful floral apron.

You may have noticed that most restaurants serve bread shortly after you sit down at the table. That's because offering guests bread is actually considered a sign of good hospitality, so if you're headed to someone's home for a brunch, luncheon, or dinner party, bring a beautiful bread basket with you. (Bonus points if there's bread in it!) The host can pop it in the middle of the table as a welcoming symbol to their guests.

Though no one needs a reason to throw a party, hosting friends and family to celebrate something exciting is always a little more fun than a no-occasion soirée. And no celebration is complete without Champagne. Saber a cork and pour some bubbles into these beautiful flutes, which are crafted from recycled South African glass.

Le Creuset debuted a new color last year, appropriately dubbed Pêche, which means "peach" in French. We don't like to play favorites, but this hue is definitely at the top of our list of Le Creuset launches. If you want to gift it to the hostess in your life, opt for something small, like this mini cocotte.

Mood lighting is key to setting the vibe at home, and nothing beats candlelight. These tapers not only add ambience, but they also enhance the tablescape with their twisty shape.

An ice bucket is an essential when it comes to party-hosting goods. This is one of our favorites because it has that old-school vintage look. In fact, it was designed with an heirloom quality in mind.

Another must-have? A trio of miniature bowls for nuts, candies, or anything else guests can easily pop into their mouth while they sip handcrafted libations or wait for the main course to arrive. We particularly love this set because they're all attached by a tiny handle.

After the hostess is done physically cleaning up after the party, let her clean the energy out too. Palo santo is the perfect way to do that. Simply light one end until the flame is deep within the stick, blow it out, and let the fragrant plumes waft through the air.

This marble wine chiller is another practical gift, but it doesn't skimp on beauty. Perhaps more than any natural stone, marble has an ability to keep liquids at their optimal temperature, which is why, back in the day, many bathtubs in wealthy homes were made entirely of marble. This wine cooler is a lot lighter and easier to maneuver than a tub, but it works the same way: keeping whatever is in it at peak temperature.

Backgammon has allegedly been around for millennia, but our earliest recordings stretch only as far back as 17th-century England. No matter, this clever game has clearly stood the test of time, as it's still a fun way to pass the time today. This set, designed by famed photographer Gray Malin, is one of the prettiest we've seen.

#6681. Of Course Hunter Schafer Pulls Off This Micro Leather Minidress

Hunter Schafer took the mob wife aesthetic and gave it a daring, high-fashion spin. Last night, the model and actress attended the red-carpet premiere of Blade Runner 2099 in Los Angeles while in the teeniest of Prada dresses and the fluffiest of fur jackets.

The minidress was a ruched leather number dyed in splashes of distressed brown for an antique look. The micro strapless piece had a sweetheart neckline and a hemline so high that it didn’t go past Schafer’s hips. Luckily, she wore matching leather panties underneath.

Styled by Dara, the supermodel made the mini work, slipping into sparkly black textured tights that acted as her bottoms and were met by pointy black heels. #6680. ‘DWTS’ Frontrunner Amber Glenn Has Had to Make a Major Decision to Stay in the Competition

Competing for the Mirrorball means making some tough choices off the dance floor.

We all know Amber Glenn is a fierce competitor, thanks to her Olympic gold medals. So, it’s no surprise she’s one of the semifinalists on Dancing With the Stars season 35. But what fans might not know is how much she’s given up to keep pushing toward the Mirrorball trophy.

According to The Sun, Amber has stepped back from figure skating events to focus on the beloved reality competition. She was most recently removed from the entry list for the NHK Trophy in Japan, which kicks off later this month. It’s the sixth and final event in the Grand Prix regular season and the second figure skating event she’s opted out of at the last minute.

Amber also withdrew from this week’s Saatva Skate America competition in Everett, Washington, as she preps for the DWTS semifinals.

“For now, Amber is focused solely on DWTS, which has taken up most of her energy and time with grueling rehearsals and weekly live shows,” the outlet reports.

The Olympian is a frontrunner for this year’s coveted prize, up against Maura Higgins, Connor Wood, Harry Shum Jr., Ezra Frech, and Tatyana Ali.

During a November 12 appearance on The Zach Sang Show, Amber opened up about what it would mean for her to help her pro partner, Pasha Pashkov, win his first-ever Mirrorball trophy.

“That would mean the world because, and I’ve said this before, is that I came into this competition like, of course, who doesn’t want to win?” she said. “But it wasn’t like, ‘I need this because I’ve worked my entire life for it.’ Like, I’ve been dancing for two months. Yes, it has been a very intense, very passionate two months of working, but it has been his whole life that he has been dancing.”

She continued, “Imagine how much work I’m putting into it. He’s doing twice that amount because he has to choreograph. He has to do so many, like, visual selections. Like, there’s so much that the pro does for every single show that people don’t see.So yes, it’s about the celeb and who wins, but it also is about the pro.”

Though Amber has had to give up some skating for ballroom dancing, there’s a chance she’ll be back on the ice at the Prevagen U.S. Championships later this season in January.

In the meantime, we can’t wait to see her crush the DWTS semifinals next week!

#6679. Is This the Ultimate Aspen Anti-Chalet?

Designer Rafael de Cárdenas, known for creating arresting spaces for Skims, Nike, and a host of New York City restaurants, worked with longtime client Rachel Dorrell on a mountain retreat with otherworldly glam.

Above: The primary bathroom of a home in Aspen, Colorado, designed by Rafael de Cárdenas with architecture by SAOTA. Custom tub with Dornbracht fittings; ceramic vase by artist Kazunori Hamana; side table by Rafael Prieto.

“We’re having a crisis of taste,” architect and designer Rafael de Cárdenas is in the midst of saying. “There’s a sameness. It’s all a historical mishmash.” He’s at breakfast in Manhattan drinking a cinnamon latte (the restaurant did not have the turmeric he requested). He has already completed his run in Central Park and his gratitude list in his Day One journal app, and he’s on day 735 following a visit to the Hoffman Institute—the famously mysterious weeklong “personal growth” retreat. “I feel like we’re in The Handmaid’s Tale,” he says. “AI is happening, but we’re going to pretend we’re designing manor houses in the English countryside in the 19th century?” Later he adds, “I have never been interested in recreating the past.”

So when longtime client Rachel Dorrell asked him to create a mountain house in Aspen for her growing family, no one expected a typical ski chalet. From her first visit to the plot of land, she realized the views of the town and the slopes beyond would be unmatched. Today those “crazy views” are framed by windows so large it’s hard to tell where rooms end and the outside begins. De Cárdenas, who collaborated with SAOTA architects to create the modernist structure, set about realizing Dorrell’s vision of “Colorado, but refined,” as she puts it. “Glamorous ’80s apartment” is how he describes the results.

“The mood board was Angelo Donghia,” that great of American design who worked with the likes of Liza Minnelli. “And Beetlejuice, always Beetlejuice! And Mickey Rourke’s apartment in 91/2 Weeks.” A cheetah-print rug sits in the library, while sculpted white custom sofas anchor the immense yet minimalist living room. “So many people have asked, ‘Shouldn’t there be art above the fireplace?’ ” de Cárdenas says. “I’m like, ‘Why?’ ”

Following the rules has never been his style. It has been 20 years since he launched his design firm, after studying at UCLA and assisting architect Greg Lynn at Vienna’s Angewandte institute and MAK museum, then returning to his native New York. The son of an erstwhile fashion PR executive and an actuary, de Cárdenas aspired to work for David Rockwell or for Alex de Betak’s fashion production company, where he once applied for a job. In the end he did a stint at Calvin Klein and worked as a production designer for films and commercials. Then a friend asked for help redoing a kitchen, something he did “kind of at night,” de Cárdenas remembers. Next came his first real client: actress Parker Posey. It was a revelation. “She was the first person I ever had to make window treatments for. I was like, ‘What the f***?’ I remember looking at a window and being like, ‘What is this supposed to look like?’ I didn’t understand that residential interior design is, essentially, fabric.”

Around the same time, he landed his first major corporate client, Nike, and did the design of aNYthing, a Lower East Side boutique that became a nexus for skaters, artists, and musicians in the late 2000s. “No one was doing stores like that at the time,” says creative director Jen Brill, who worked with de Cárdenas on several Nike projects. “There was nothing minimal about it. It was loud, it was fun. It was the first store I saw that was like retail theater.”

Another friend, Felix Burrichter, a curator and the founder of Pin-Up architecture magazine, recalls de Cárdenas’s early fascination with the Memphis Group architects (“when it was still considered bad taste,” Burrichter says) as well as his willingness to experiment with materials, such as applying duct tape patterns to the walls of a Jeffrey Deitch exhibition in Rome, or wrapping an Ikea chair with fluorescent gaffer tape. “For the aNYthing store, I physically built a lot of those things, because there was no one to do it and no budget,” de Cárdenas says. He sometimes laments not being so hands-on anymore, now that he has a team of 18 based in New York and Paris. “There was a lot of energy in those projects.”

“His work is like Limelight–meets–Park Avenue,” says Burrichter, who calls de Cárdenas the heir to ELLE Decor A-List designer Peter Marino, a maestro who works with significant private clients alongside luxury behemoths such as Dior and Chanel. De Cárdenas may have moved from duct tape to gold leaf applied by European artisans, “but there is always that tension in Raf’s work,” Burrichter says, “the binary between decorating and being an architect, between uptown and the club.”

Dorrell, for her part, became aware of de Cárdenas a decade ago, when she and her husband purchased a home in the West Village that he had staged. “We were like, ‘Who did the interiors and who did the art? It’s perfect.’ ” She says they were drawn to the ’70s vibe and the peachy hues, an unusual shade at the time. “We didn’t change a thing.” Since then they have worked together on homes in Austin, New York, and currently their largest yet, in Palm Beach. Dorrell sends “rapid-fire” texts of inspiration ranging from tiled walls at Lake Como’s Villa d’Este to barstools in Paris. “Raf cracks me up—we are brutally honest with each other,” she says, explaining that her taste skews more traditional, while his is often edgier.

“Some of my projects have moments that are ugly, and they excite me,” he says. “Because something truly excellent, something that is pushing boundaries, is not going to land pleasurably at first.” He points to Madonna, a frequent touchstone and source of inspiration for him. “The first time you saw Blond Ambition, the first time you saw Madonna with a high ponytail and with the headset, it was a little jarring. The thick eyebrow and the cone bra.” He often finds himself thinking, Where is that moment now?

Despite his concerns about the flattening effect of the algorithm, he’s still happy he’s in the business of taste. “I can’t believe I was lucky enough to land here, because I didn’t know what interior design even was,” he says. “I like my job. I’m grateful for my job. I want people to say, ‘He’s good at his job.’ ”

The Dorrells might concur: “ ‘We’re not doing another project,’ ” Rachel recalls her husband saying about Aspen. “Now he’s like, ‘Thank god,’ because it’s so awesome.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

#6678. This Puget Sound Home Kept All of Its Original Charm—While Hiding Every Modern Convenience

The design team faithfully restored the details that have the most heart.

When this Puget Sound home was built in 1908, its original owners gave it a name: Heart’s Delight. Years later, after the house was bought by another family, principal designer Lisa Staton, alongside her senior designer Lauren Galeotti, took on the task of restoring the space with that same sentiment in mind.

“My favorite thing about this house is all of the love it holds,” Galeotti says. The new owners became deeply attached to the space, and the neighbors had long adored the house, too. As work progressed, the contractors and tradespeople fell for it as well. “She really charmed us all, from start to finish,” Galeotti says of the home.

That affection guided a renovation rooted in respect for what was already there. “Our passion for heritage homes is fueled by the original craftsmanship, history, and charm the homes hold,” Staton explains.

Every piece of original millwork stayed, and any new elements added had to take their cues from the old—even if that meant undoing some of the design missteps of the past. For example, a custom, made-to-match fireplace now sits over the original brick-and-plaster hearth that had been painted white. Though it was a whole team effort to get right, it now flawlessly complements the living room’s woodwork.

The current homeowners are also avid entertainers, so the kitchen needed a total strategic (and stylistic) rework that wouldn’t expand beyond its footprint. Adding the backsplash tile, created with Dorset Delft artist Aviva Halter, and a textural floor tile were key to giving the room its inimitable character. Other splurges, like all-new lighting throughout and the powder room's striking mural wallpaper, were necessary investments in the home's future.

But the biggest one, Staton says, was restoring all original details that everyone had fallen in love with: "always worth the investment!"

FAST FACTSDesigner: Lisa Staton and Lauren Galeotti of Lisa Staton Interior DesignStylist: Cozbi Jean Location: Puget Sound, WashingtonThe Space: A four-bedroom, four-bathroom Craftsman-style home built in 1908

This living room already “glows with daylight” thanks to its original windows, and the millwork has charm and texture in spades, so the main task in updating this room was to create a cozy, after-hours vibe.

“We wanted to make it warm and inviting, and every surface and corner needed to be enveloping,” Staton says. She achieved this by mixing newly sourced finds with well-loved pieces from the family’s personal collection, and it all blends seamlessly.

After entering the home through the original French doors, guests are greeted by the great room. This entertaining-ready space is faithful to its original style with two very notable additions: the custom fireplace cladding and accordion panels directly above that open to reveal the TV. It’s a room that can easily turn a quick drop-by into an entire evening in. “A pair of matching sofas invite you to sit and chat for a while,” Staton adds.

Keeping the spirit of this kitchen intact while gut-renovating it was a challenge, but the trick was to hide away the modern conveniences wherever possible and draw the eye toward period-appropriate accents instead.

“In heritage homes, it is really helpful to bring in antique elements to the space to add warmth and character in imperfect items,” Galeotti says. In this case, the flush mount ceiling lights, stools, window treatments, and hutch bring in texture and patina that add to the room’s authenticity.

Combining the storage and function of a kitchen island with the rustic character of a cook’s table, the kitchen’s central surface feels more like a piece of furniture than an extension of the cabinetry. “Since we did not need to put any appliances in the island, that really became an opportunity to keep beauty at the forefront,” Galeotti explains.

One of the team’s favorite parts of the kitchen, though, lies in the smaller details: the handmade backsplash tiles, which bring a personal, playful touch. “The client chose the subject matter of each custom-painted tile, including meaningful flowers and even their pets!” Galeotti says. Their Dorset Delft style makes the stoneware feel like it has always belonged in the house.

Though space is certainly tight in this pantry, it’s a workhorse area in its own right. “We designed custom cabinetry to line the walls with plenty of storage, and were even able to squeeze in a wall oven, microwave, and warming drawer,” Galeotti notes. This discreet appliance placement also helps the main cook space retain its ageless atmosphere.

The homeowners asked for this newly added powder room to feel “whimsical,” and Staton’s team granted that wish, selecting a dusky Andrew Martin mural wallcovering that depicts a landscape with traditional folk art motifs. Its towering pear trees, mythical creatures, and rambling vales read like an epic fairy tale across the four walls. “It is a small room, but we wanted to make a big impact,” she says.

According to Galeotti, “the dining room lives in the same footprint and largely looks exactly like the day it was built!” The team restored the original built-in cabinet, whose diamond-patterned panes echo the room’s leaded-glass windows. This clean-line geometry contrasts beautifully with the curves, plumes, and ruffled silhouettes seen in the window treatments and Murano glass chandelier.

Updating the primary bedroom’s layout was another necessary correction to previous design choices—the team took down a wall and turned two rooms into one. “However, given how the windows are on the large wall where the rooms used to be divided, it may have originally been one space,” Galeotti adds. Now, there’s an unobstructed view out to the water and mountains just beyond the property. They also added a his-and-hers closet, new flooring, and a bed Galeotti describes as a “perfect cloud” from which to greet the surrounding scenery each day.

Previously, the primary bathroom was completely open to the bedroom, with carpeting and a step up to access it. “It was a bit of a strange situation,” Staton admits. A serious layout rework was in order so that the space is now separate, level with the bedroom, and equipped with a freestanding tub and beautifully tiled shower. “We also added a feature we do in most bathrooms: use vintage or antique mirrors as doors to create medicine cabinets,” she adds.

“The addition of this bedroom at the back of the house gives guests a really private retreat in a quiet corner,” Galeotti says. With its bobbin bed frame, antique furniture finds, and misty color palette, this sleep space provides a soft place to land.

“This is the kids’ bathroom, but we imagined a bathroom that felt beautiful for children, guests, and grown kids alike,” Galeotti notes. The space originally suffered from the same “watch the step” quirk that the main bathroom did, so Staton installed a similar solution here: She put the plumbing into the floor and incorporated all-new finishes and fixtures to add elegant polish that all ages can appreciate.

Lisa Staton Interior Design is a boutique firm established in 2004 with roots in the East Coast and Seattle, united by a shared love for enhancing older homes and creating new ones inspired by historic craftsmanship. The team brings a unique blend of both modern and traditional elements to a wide array of homes.

#6676. How to Layer Clothes for Fall and Winter Without Sacrificing Style

Fall is officially here and I am doing everything to stay warm. Layering is a very necessary skill come the colder months of the year. Not only is it practical—keeping you warm and such—but it also makes your fall and winter outfits that much more interesting.

As a Florida native now in the Northeast, layering is something I’m just now getting the hang of. And if you’re like me, either new to the cold or been here for years and can’t quite get it, I’ve got you covered. Below, I’ve gathered a few fall layering styling tips so you can lean into the fun of it all.

One of my favorite cozy pieces in my closet is an oversized turtleneck sweater. Not only will it keep you warm, but it is very chic. A fun way to elevate what could be seen as a simple look is tying your sweater around your waist, but specifically off to the side. It adds an undeniable touch of cool.

When first getting into layering, you can always start small. And doubling up on your shirts is the easiest way to achieve warmth. Raise the stakes by showing off those layers, either by wearing a longer shirt underneath or slightly raising the top shirt so the one underneath is visible. Even more points if the shirts are different colors so you get a color-blocking moment. Add in a peekaboo jacket zipped similarly to the look above and you’re set.

Belt layering is definitely more for the styling side of fall/winter layering, but it’s just as important. When trying to bring a little more life to a cold weather look, belt stacking is a fashion-forward option I personally love.

Oversized jackets are the real winners of fall. Especially a leather jacket like the one above. Add in a patterned sweater tied around the waist for a little more fun and you’ve got a perfect look.

Similar to tying around the waist, adding a scarf over top of your coat is another more style-forward layering tip that might not necessarily provide all the warmth, but brings the style.

A true Y2K favorite of mine that works great for the colder months is dresses over jeans. If you’re into nostalgia fashion, this is a more modern way to tap into the trend, while also staying warm.

Aiyana Ishmael is the style editor at Cosmopolitan magazine. In her work, Aiyana focuses on the culture of fashion and how it intertwines and shapes the zeitgeist. She is an award-winning journalist from Miami, Florida, and a graduate of the historically Black university, Florida A&M. She is a 2024 Forbes 30 Under 30: Media honoree, and her debut romance novella PASSING GAME is set to release March of 2027 (831 Stories/Simon & Schuster).

#6675. Jackie Kennedy’s Private Letters to Truman Capote Reveal a Fuller Picture of Their Friendship

Heading to auction on November 21, the six notes are estimated to fetch as much as $80,000.

First Lady Jacqueline Kennedy’s sister, Lee Radziwill, was one of celebrated author Truman Capote’s “Swans,” developing a close, if complicated, friendship with the novelist. But Jackie had her own relationship with Capote, one that is less widely known. The pair corresponded with each other in the 1960s and Kennedy may have even gotten an early look at Capote’s 1966 novel, In Cold Blood.

“I have your precious book and am so overjoyed,” she wrote to Capote in an undated card, presumed to be from June 1963. “The minute Jack takes off for Europe I will retreat to the country + indulge myself by reading every page—not just the new ones.”

That letter, along with five others between Kennedy and Capote, will soon hit the auction block, ELLE Decor can reveal. The notes, three of which are written on White House letterhead, will be sold together in a single lot through Cornell Auctions, estimated to fetch between $40,000 and $80,000. Together, they reveal new details about the friendship between the former first lady and one of America’s great authors.

Available for the first time ever, these private letters are part of a larger auction of Capote-linked items accumulated over decades by his biographer, Gerald Clarke, author of 1988’s Capote. The sale, “The Magpie and the Swans: The Possessions of Truman Capote,” will take place on November 21.

Kennedy’s notes are all believed to have been written in 1963 and 1964, with four bearing postmark dates. During that period, Kennedy suffered personal tragedies, losing her infant son Patrick days after he was born as well as her husband, President John F. Kennedy, who was assassinated in November 1963.

The letters indicate a warm relationship between Kennedy and Capote. In several of the messages, the first lady makes reference to various presents and mementos she received from the writer.

One particularly meaningful gift came in the wake of President John F. Kennedy’s death, after Jackie and Capote saw each other at a gathering hosted by decorator Billy Baldwin. “The most beautiful porcelain rose that I ever saw or dreamed of—came to me the other day from you,” the former first lady wrote in a three-page December 1964 missive, handwritten on mourning stationery bearing a crest.

In another letter, Kennedy makes reference to a gift she sent to Capote, inspired by a visit to the novelist’s home. “In your Japanese-Victorian room I spotted two large apothecary jars of the same kind—I found this one in 1955 in Paris—so it has been with me for many years in many places,” she wrote in an undated letter, believed to be from December 1964.

Handwritten on black-bordered stationery, that note was addressed to Capote’s house at 70 Willow Street in Brooklyn Heights, where he wrote Breakfast at Tiffany’s and In Cold Blood.

Read Jackie Kennedy’s Letters to Truman Capote

Beyond the Kennedy notes, also up for auction is Capote’s four-piece formal dinner invitation from President Lyndon B. Johnson in 1966, which has an estimate of $600 to $2,000. Other highlights include a German pencil sharpener in a leather pouch; a chalkware bulldog; a monogrammed duffel bag; and jewel-like paperweights made by the author himself.

Taken together, these pieces paint a fuller picture of Capote’s life and work as a writer, as well as Kennedy’s enthusiastic support of his talents. “I keep thinking what power a great writer has. All the things you write move people,” she wrote. “I am glad you became a writer.”

“The Magpie and the Swans: The Possessions of Truman Capote” will take place November 21 starting at 11 a.m.; bidding can be done online via LiveAuctioneers or in person at Cornell Auctions in Bellport, New York.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6673. Jennifer Lawrence Swaps Her Pops of Lemon for a Polished Dark Monochrome Look

Complete with a glossy Hermès crocodile Kelly

Jennifer Lawrence can just as easily pull off a bright lemon-yellow Colleen Allen coat or a pair of funky sequined pink Siedrés pants as she can a polished, monochromatic business look.

Earlier this week, the actor looked so chic in an outfit composed of timeless navy and black staples. The base of her look was made up of an elongated, buttoned blazer, and matching trousers. To continue the color scheme, she wrapped a navy scarf around her neck.

Then came the luxurious black accents: square sunglasses and a rare Hermès Kelly Sellier in shiny crocodile leather and gold hardware. And because Lawrence cannot live without her pops of color, the actor slipped on a pair of burgundy velvet slippers.

Lawrence is often one to go minimalist with her jewelry and glam, and she did that here, opting for polish-free nails, minimal makeup, and a few gold rings (one of them being her engagement ring from husband Cooke Maroney).

As you may have guessed, the cherry on top of the look was Lawrence’s dirty-blonde hair, which was styled down in easy, breezy long waves. The Hunger Games alum recently introduced her haircare brand, Mes, and has since been promoting her products around town.

“I feel like if my hair looks good, no one will notice anything else about my face, my clothes, or my body,” the actor joked in a recent interview with The Cut.

Of course, we disagree. While we do love a good hair day, we’ll never miss a J.Law street-style moment.

#6672. Heeled Loafers Are the Shoe Trend Fashion People Are Loving Right Now

A little studious, a little unserious, and extremely easy to style.

There’s a new star shoe on the block, and it’s a little academia-core with a lot of fun. Yes, heeled loafers are the shoe fashion people are obsessing over, so you’re going to want to tap in early while you can.

We saw the shoe making its way onto several runways this past season, from Louis Vuitton and Mulberry to Diesel and Chanel. For a while now, dark academia and studious fashion have been super popular. Regular loafers are the go-to for many, whether it be wearing them to actual school or work.

The style has caught the eye of many fashion insiders, including our senior fashion editor, Jessica Neises. “The first heeled loafer I fell for this season is by Sofia Testino, an emerging NYC-based designer whose debut included the most luscious, candy-colored patent wedge loafers,” Neises says. “Other downtown brands like Vaquera have also followed suit with this shape. There’s something a little studious but still unserious about them, and the wedge is the perfect middle ground for anyone who isn’t ready to commit to a full-blown heel but doesn’t love a flat loafer either, comfortable yet still fab.”

Below, I’ve gathered some heeled loafer inspo so that you can figure out just how to style your pair.

Staying in the realm of academia, but upping the fashion, pairing your heeled loafers with a cinched blazer (and don’t forget the accessories) alongside a colorful patterned skirt is the surefire way into this trend.

As a Floridian who struggles with layering during the colder months, a good dress will always take you far. And this style works great with a more fashion-forward heeled loafer.

Similar to throwing on a dress, a good matching set will get the job done every time. Pairing a sleek leather pair of heeled loafers with this sweater set is not only stylish but super comfortable.

#6669. I Found the Most Comfortable Pointed-Toe Boots

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Buying pointed-toe boots has always been a challenge for me because I’m built like a ballerina—not, sadly, in terms of long legs or elegant arms or a swanlike neck, but in terms of feet. I have the bunions of a woman who has dedicated her life to the art of dance, which is ironic because I took ballet lessons for all of three months when I was eight. (I quit after they let me wear the tutu, which was really the only thing I was in it for.) I am confessing this not because I enjoy talking about my feet on the internet, but to establish my credibility when I say that I have finally found a pair of slim, sexy, pointed-toe boots that are comfortable enough to wear all day, even for those of us with less-than-ideal extremities.

Margaux’s Inga boots have a long, squared-off toe and a little kitten heel that measures 1.5 inches—tall enough to give you a bit of a lift, but low enough to be walkable. They come in black leather and a sleek espresso suede, both of which look great with jeans but would also be cute peeking out of long, drapey trousers. The best part is the sizing: Margaux offers its boots in narrow, medium, and wide, and somehow, through the magic of proportion play, the wide does not look wide. They accommodate the fullest part of my foot without looking bulky or breaking up the nice long line.

The first time I wore them, it was to a witches’ dinner that my friend Mo throws every Halloween. She invites 30 of her best female friends over, decorates her home in high goth style, and serves us a theatrically witchy meal. (This time, it was black squid-ink pasta.) Of course, one has to dress appropriately to dine with the coven, so I paired the boots with slim black jeans and a sheer black polka-dotted top. Not only did I get a bunch of compliments, but I didn’t find myself pining for my broomstick on the walk home—the boots were just as comfortable at the end of the evening as they’d been at the start of the night.

They’ve held up equally well on the commute to work. In addition to the extra width, they also come with five millimeters of cushioning on the inside, which is enough to protect you when you’re pounding the pavement. Ever since slimmer toes reentered the fashion conversation, I’ve been faced with the choice between sitting out the trend or hobbling around in boots that fundamentally do not fit. As much as I love a good pair of shoes, I also live for a long walk, and the Ingas, miraculously, let me have both. Really, they don’t feel like heels at all, which is a rare and wonderful thing for a pair of pointed-toe kitten-heel boots.

#6668. 6 Best Posture-Correcting Bras For All-Day Support and Comfort

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

We even found the one Taylor Swift wore for The Eras Tour rehearsals.

In a world where we’re hunched over our computers from nine to five, and then slouched and scrolling from five to nine, good posture is a rarity. But that doesn’t mean we should just accept a fate of poor spinal alignment. Especially when there’s actually a really easy solution (that doesn’t include deleting TikTok from your iPhone).

Allow me to introduce you to the posture-correcting bra. Along with delivering superior support, the shapewear category uses stretchy fabrics with varying levels of tension to reposition your shoulders and help you breathe better. Taylor Swift even wore one while training for the Eras Tour—and hers alone is low-key life-changing.

Now, even the best posture bras aren't intended to fix serious medical issues on their own, so it's not a substitute for, say, a scoliosis brace. But if you're looking for a little extra help retraining your body to sit up straight with your head held high, these types of bras encourage small corrections in the right direction!

A posture-correcting bra works best alongside an ergonomic chair, Pilates classes, or a new mattress, rather than as a replacement for any of them. It also happens to be the smallest investment of the bunch, and editors like me are big fans. Keep reading for my top picks—including the one and only Taylor Swift-approved option.

Why we love it: I noticed an immediate difference in my posture when I wore this bra for the first time. It's stretchy, sweat-wicking, and flat underneath clothing, plus it supports my shoulder that tends to act up.

We've unofficially dubbed this one the Taylor Swift bra because the singer has been spotted multiple times (ie. The End of an Era, her TIME profile, etc) rehearsing for the Eras Tour in this bad boy. I did a super in-depth review of this one here, by the way. This popular choice is a little tight at first, but give it a little stretch before your first wear to fix that. You’ll notice that it works pretty much as soon as you put it on. The Forme bra has nice clean lines (even under clothes) and although it's on the pricier side, it'll do an excellent job of ensuring your shoulders are upright whenever you're wearing it. I loved the fact that it helps with general alignment as well as relieving some upper back pain and tension. And if you prefer an option that has built-in padding, try Forme's Power+ Bra.

Why we love it: This under-$60 pick is on the more affordable side, while still offering both posture correction and relief from back aches, pain, and fatigue.

This high-coverage, cotton-blend bra uses a criss-cross style back to encourage proper posture. There are adjustable padded straps and a long front hook-and-eye closure for customizing your preferred tightness—it also makes the bra easier to put on and take off once you get the hang of the mechanism. According to reviewers, it's comfy enough for every day and night (yes, even for wearing to bed if you so choose). Note that some say it runs slightly small!

Why we love it: Between the front closure and the padded straps, this wireless pick gives you more than one way to fine-tune your fit.

This full-coverage bra has a wide elastic underbust band and a high back to support your shoulders and keep you in alignment. Its front hook-and-eye closure adjusts at two levels, so you can loosen or tighten the band as needed, and the padded adjustable straps help prevent shoulder strain. Reviewers love how smooth it looks under clothes, especially along the back and sides. Note that many say the cups run small, and the brand recommends sizing up for a more relaxed fit!

Why we love it: This lightweight brace is meant to be layered over a bra, so you get posture support without giving up your go-to everyday bra.

Instead of replacing your bra, this thin nylon-spandex brace slips on like a vest and wraps around your shoulders and upper back to gently pull them into alignment. A wide hook-and-loop belt fastens around your rib cage, so you can adjust how snug it feels, and it’s low-profile enough to wear under a blouse or sweater. Reviewers say it’s comfortable enough for long workdays at a desk, and some also wear it over a tee or cami at home. Note that a few say the shoulder straps can dig into their underarms after several hours, so you might want to ease into wearing it!

Why we love it: Sometimes posture-correcting bras, especially the more intense ones, can be hard to wear for extended periods of time. This one is more lightweight, if you're hoping for an option that can be worn all day, every day.

This posture corrector is less of an over-the-shoulder boulder holder and more of a bralette. Will it lock your shoulders into place like some of the more heavy-duty options on this list? No, probably not. But it’s lightweight and a lot more comfortable, so you can wear it for longer periods of time and reap the posture-correcting benefits while lounging. The thick straps have hook-and-eye closures that allow you to adjust the length!

Why we love it: This option comes in a wide range of sizes for a variety of bust types. It doesn't restrict your breathing or get uncomfortable throughout the day, either.

One of the best benefits of this posture-correcting bra? The bust and shoulder support combo that it offers will help you relieve boob-induced back pain while keeping your spine aligned. Glamorise is already such a tried-and-true brand for well-constructed bras made for anyone with a larger chest, so get ready to be impressed by this one—especially when you see that it's comfy enough that you're not itching to fling off your bra once you get home.

#6666. Bulgari Brings High Jewelry to a Palm Beach Stronghold

To celebrate its new boutique, the Italian house unveiled its Eclettica collection at a quintessential South Florida estate.

Above: To celebrate Bulgari’s new collection and boutique, guests roamed the grounds of a waterfront Palm Beach estate, designed by architect Addison Mizner.

Few people shaped the look of South Florida as much as architect Addison Mizner. A pioneer of Spanish Colonial Revival architecture, his Mediterranean style quickly took Palm Beach by storm in the 1920s, as he designed and built more than 50 Florida mansions and villas. And his impact still lingers, as, on Wednesday night, stars like Venus Williams and Chanel Iman spent their evening seated under the tennis pavilion at one of Mizner’s famed waterfront Palm Beach estates.

The crowd was there to celebrate a far newer building in the neighborhood: Bulgari’s Palm Beach boutique, which opened earlier this week. In honor of the store’s debut, the brand brought its newest high jewelry collection, Eclettica, to the United States for the first time.

“Palm Beach embodies a distinctive spirit of effortless sophistication,” said Bulgari CEO Laura Burdese.

Located in a heritage building in Palm Beach’s shopping district, the boutique is modeled after a traditional Italian piazza. Anchored by four travertine columns, the open space encourages clients to move through the maison’s most famous collections. Venini’s classic Serpenti chandelier, made of Murano glass and seen in Bvlgari boutiques worldwide, serves as a focal point in a shape reminiscent of the brand’s many symbolic snakes.

Elsewhere in the store, decorative panels by Milan-based Pictalab depict Floridian ocean and beach views, grounding the Italian maison in its surrounding landscape. Among the art is the Eclettica collection, which borrows its name from eclecticism and stays true to its moniker: mixing inspirations from painting, sculpture, and architecture, translating them into bold, colorful pieces.

It’s a fitting style for Palm Beach, where Mizner’s vibrant Mediterranean and Spanish-influenced designs borrow inspiration from entire eras and regions, giving the area its everlasting mix-master flair.

“We are delighted to bring Eclettica to this extraordinary destination,” Burdese said, “where its bold artistic vision and exceptional craftsmanship find a natural resonance with the city’s vibrant character.”

#6665. Drew Barrymore Winks to Her 2000s Style in a Black Newsboy Cap

Anyone else thinking of that scene from Charlie’s Angels: Full Throttle?

Last night, the Museum of Modern Art held its annual film benefit in tandem with Chanel, which raises money for “the acquisition and preservation of key film works” in the MoMA’s movie department, with director Steven Spielberg serving as this year’s special honoree. Typically, the New York City event unites some of the starriest names in Hollywood, and the 2026 benefit was no exception, with attendees including the likes of Cate Blanchett, Zoe Saldaña, Emily Blunt, and Michelle Pfeiffer. But even at such an A-list event, one person managed to steal the show: Drew Barrymore.

The talk show host turned heads in a striking, Mod-inspired look, the star of which was a shaggy black-and-white jacket. Meanwhile, her choice of headwear injected the evening with a welcome dose of nostalgia.

Like most of the attendees, Barrymore dressed in Chanel for the occasion, pulling a statement piece of outerwear from the house’s Pre-Fall 2026 collection by Matthieu Blazy. The jacket featured a black-and-white windowpane pattern in a distorted tweed, with fringe tufts running all along the trim. Meanwhile, the lining featured a pattern of repeating faces in red and white, which only peeked out in brief moments.

Stylist Lee Harris left the jacket open to reveal a black turtleneck top and matching slacks underneath, while a long pearl necklace adorned with the house’s signature double-C pendant dangled in front.

However, it was Barrymore’s black newsboy cap that made the greatest statement. Over the years, the Emmy Award-winner has been a champion of all kinds of hat styles, from fedoras and beanies to baseball caps and berets. But you might recall that she went through a strong newsboy-cap phase in the early 2000s, especially when she was promoting the Charlie’s Angels films.

In fact, she event wears one during one of the most-memorable scenes in the sequel, Charlie’s Angels: Full Throttle (2003), when she and co-stars Cameron Diaz and Lucy Liu have an impromptu dance break to MC Hammer’s “U Can’t Touch This.” (You can revisit that incredible moment here.)

In honor of Barrymore, and in honor of cinema, it might just be about time for a rewatch.

#6664. The Streamer’s Guide to a ‘Gilmore Girls’ Thanksgiving

The holiday-themed television episode is a lost art form in the streaming era. Back in the days of appointment television, these episodes became almost ritualistic, holding a distinct place in the annual rhythm of our pop culture consumption. Before we shared streaming passwords, we watched weekly (and traveled by horse and buggy, in the snow, uphill both ways). You actually cared about what your favorite characters dressed up as for Halloween. A few weeks later, your favorite show had a holiday episode— sometimes overt, like every Christmas-timed episode of Glee, and sometimes they were less so.

The holidays offered a time for our favorite shows to reflect on family dynamics and seasonal rituals. For Gilmore Girls, a show entirely based on family dynamics and seasonal rituals, the Thanksgiving holiday was a vessel through which to serve more Stars Hollow kitsch and crispier leaves.

That said…there are a mere two episodes of Gilmore Girls dedicated to Thanksgiving. In truth, the entire series feels like apple pie and stuffing, so any episode will do for Friendsgiving background noise. If you want to do the holiday right, these are the two best episodes to watch on turkey day.

Watch every episode of ‘Gilmore Girls’ on Hulu

In perhaps the most iconic Thanksgiving episode of Gilmore Girls, Rory and Lorelai have a Four Christmases holiday, attending four different celebrations in one day. At the elder Gilmores, it’s lavish, traditional fare. At Mrs. Kim’s house, it’s a strict, no-nonsense, Korean Thanksgiving. At Sookie and Jackson’s, it’s a farm-to-table feast, and at Luke’s diner, it’s a heartfelt bite.

Like any good Thanksgiving dinner, the main course is not the poultry, but the discourse happening at the table. In this episode, Rory reveals to Lorelai that she’s applied to multiple colleges beyond Harvard, opening the door for her to follow her grandfather’s footsteps, straight onto the campus of Yale. Other episode highlights include Lane’s first kiss and growing pains between Human Kirk and Cat Kirk. If you know you know, and if you don’t, thanks for reading this far.

It took three whole seasons to recover from the four-pronged holiday episode, but the girls came back to the table in season 6 with a much more weighted episode. The sixth season of Gilmore Girls is largely marked by the divide between Rory and Lorelai, but this holiday-timed episode marks their reunion after a months long feud. The drama this time is between Luke and Lorelai, with Luke hiding a major secret from his dream girl—he has a daughter named April, whom he’s just met.

In the spirit of journalism, we can’t definitively call any other episode of Gilmore Girls an official Thanksgiving episode. That said, there are more than a few autumnal picks that scratch the same itch.

Like this one, with its supreme autumnal vibes. “They Shoot Gilmores, Don’t They” aired in November, a few weeks before the big season 2 Thanksgiving episode. Here’s an idea: if you start streaming here and make it a mini-marathon, you’ll be feeling festive by Turkey Day.

For millions of Americans, Thanksgiving and football are synonymous whether you’re watching on television or playing in the backyard. So let’s go ahead and call “The One Where Emily and Richard Are Football Fans,” a.k.a. “Ted Koppel’s Big Night Out,” an honorary Thanksgiving episode. The Harvard-Yale game typically happens the Saturday before Thanksgiving. Plus, crisp air, big sweatshirts, and juicy family secrets exposed? Come on!

For cozy nostalgia of a different kind, switch from Hulu/Disney+ to Netflix and queue up the fourth and final episode of the revival series. Honestly, rewatching “A Year in the Life” might also be a good mini-binge idea. Milo Ventimiglia’s yearning face against the autumnal foliage of Stars Hollow is emotional terrorism for the members of Team Jess, but otherwise...

#6662. On the Couch: Tina Barney

In honor of her new book, the celebrated photographer shares some select sources of inspiration.

In 1974, Tina Barney moved from New York City to Sun Valley, Idaho, a quiet, picturesque ski hamlet set within the Rocky Mountains. It was there, while taking an art school class on great American photographers, that she began shooting photographs herself. In the five-plus decades that followed, Barney became a legendary American photographer in her own right, with works entering the permanent collections of museums like the Whitney, the Museum of Modern Art, and the Los Angeles County Museum of Art.

The daughter of 1940s fashion model Lillian Fox and granddaughter of the founder of Lehman Brothers, Barney is known for documenting high society with a sharp eye, photographing affluent Americans, European aristocrats, and lavish spaces. She also captured what they wore—and shot for fashion magazines and major designers like Gucci and Bottega Veneta. Barney’s relationship with style, stemming from her mother’s work with fashion photographers like Horst P. Horst and George Hoyningen-Huene, is the subject of her new book Tina Barney: Fashion, out now.

In honor of her book’s release, Barney took our On the Couch survey, sharing a few of her biggest influences and inspirations.

Greatest Influences: I made a film on Jan Groover. I made a film on Horst P. Horst. And you can see the photos he took of my mom in the new book.

Favorite Sofa: My couch has been in this house for decades, and I am probably getting a new one because this one's falling apart. But it is in the right place with the right light. I can watch TV; I can read; I can draw; I can do anything I want from this place. And it's comfortable.

Most beloved buildings: I did a whole book on the Noguchi Museum, a portrait of Isamu Noguchi’s building, in a way, and of his sculpture.

Destination Museum: The Musée d’Orsay. It’s the way the paintings are hung that really interests me. There are artworks placed within little cubicles, and then, of course, there are the most well-known paintings that are just so easy to keep looking at. So the Musée d'Orsay first, followed by almost any other museum in Paris.

Go-to galleries: It depends on the artist that is showing. In New York, I go out to look at galleries every month with a friend. We’ll see what the new shows are, and I always try to learn from what I see.

Favorite Artist: I don't like to name names because there are no favorites. But I just saw a Brice Marden show at Gagosian that was tops.

Favorite getaway: I travel as little as possible right now. I live in Rhode Island part-time, more so than in New York. Here it is quiet.

Best Shop for Gifts: Well, I love agnès b. She’s very supportive of photographers, so that’s how I came to know her, and I still visit her shop in New York.

Style Icons: I don't think of buzzwords like that. But, my mother for sure. Her style was led by her sense of humor, and by her own personality. In those days, you weren't thinking about the fashion designers and who was in and who was out. And so the clothes had much more character to them.

My mother brought me to an Yves Saint Laurent fashion show in Paris when I was about 16, which I remember very well. It was the first show that Yves Saint Laurent ever had on his own. We also saw the designer Guy Laroche; we went in after the show and my mother had a suit made for her, so they measured her. And that was a pretty fantastic experience.

Fashion Designers: I have a picture of me from about when I was age 14 and I'm wearing a suit that looks like a Chanel. And because they were starting to copy her at that point, you could get a viable suit for someone 14 or 16 years old that looked like a Chanel. I love that the style of Chanel has existed for so long, and has not changed that much.

Required Reading: Katie Kitamura. Also, Judith Freeman, my favorite author, who I know is working on a new novel.

Cocktail of Choice: I don’t drink; I never have. I’m like a monk.

Favorite Restaurant: The Olympia Tea Room in Watch Hill, Rhode Island.

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.
