#6661. Léa Seydoux Isn’t Trying to Act. She’s Trying to Live

The French actress continues her string of emotionally demanding roles in The Unknown and Gentle Monster

Léa Seydoux first stepped into the limelight in Christophe Honoré’s The Beautiful Person, where her role as Junie de Chartres earned her the Trophée Chopard award for rising actors at Cannes. From there, she moved seamlessly between highbrow French cinema and Hollywood, achieving a critical breakthrough with Blue Is the Warmest Color in 2013 and starring alongside Daniel Craig as a Bond girl in Spectre and No Time to Die. Most years, she’s starred in at least two films, and 2026 is no exception: She plays a woman with a man’s soul trapped in her body in Arthur Harari’s psychological fantasy The Unknown, and in Marie Kreutzer’s Gentle Monster, releasing on Netflix on November 18, she’s a pianist who unearths her husband’s hideous crimes. The words thorny and barbed read like understatements for both films, and it’s exactly what interests Seydoux about the stories. Like in life itself, she avoids easy answers, opting instead for exploration.

Seydoux got the chance to explore an especially ambitious idea in The Unknown. It tells the story of David Zimmerman (Niels Schneider), a photographer who wakes up in the body of a woman he sleeps with. Seydoux plays both that woman, Eva, and the man trapped within her body. “I felt the movie was something new to me, to play an impossible idea and make it believable, try to give it a truthful dimension,” Seydoux says. She’s no stranger to high-concept stories, previously starring in Bertrand Bonello’s The Beast and David Cronenberg’s Crimes of the Future. Each time, she imbues these characters with humanity, despite their far-fetched sci-fi dilemmas.

“I like movies that make you think, movies that bring up questions, when there’s a debate around the film,” she says. “What I love the most is to explore human nature, our human condition. This is what cinema allows us to do.”

Seydoux might vehemently love movies, yet acting remains a demanding process for her. She relies on the art form to dig into feelings and emotions. “I don’t feel necessarily very comfortable with acting. It’s not a place where I’m like, ‘I love it,’ but at the same time, I find it a bit painful. It’s more a way to experience things and ideas,” she says.

Gentle Monster provides another knotty premise, this time grounded in reality. It follows Lucy Weiss, a famed experimental pianist who gives up her career to raise her young son with her doting husband, Philip (Laurence Rupp). One day, the police knock on their door, and it’s revealed that Philip is accused of distributing child pornography.

“The subject matter is tough, but the fact that it’s through the woman’s perspective—and not through the victim or perpetrator—it’s through her emotions that the story unfolds [is what interested me],” she says. “Things are not too defined, but it’s also because of the subject matter; it was the only way to talk about it. [Marie] gave me a lot of freedom.”

Born in Paris in 1985, Seydoux spent her childhood wedged among seven siblings and half-siblings. She remembers feeling isolated, practically invisible, as a child. “I was always in the shadows in a way,” she says. “As a kid, I felt transparent. I was never at the center of [my] big family.” This plays a big part in her surprise at her acting career, never thinking of herself as someone deserving of center stage, of fame, of attention. In fact, acting never even crossed her mind until she befriended some actors when she turned 18. “I loved the way they lived their lives,” she says. “I admired their life—the fact that they were free.”

And yet, she rejects the label of “actress.” “I try to be truthful as much as possible,” she says. “I try not to act. This is what I try to be, and I’m more into reaction than action.” She adds, “It’s more reactive than acting.”

For Seydoux, “reacting” and really experiencing life is only possible if she “abandons” herself to her characters. One of the most intense examples of this is Abdellatif Kechiche’s controversial lesbian love story Blue Is the Warmest Color, in which she stars alongside Adèle Exarchopoulos. The film’s schedule stretched from two months to five months, and it has since become notorious for its grueling pace. (The famous sex scene took 10 days alone.) “The greatest and most intense experience I’ve had was with Adèle when we did Blue Is the Warmest Color,” she recalls. “It was crazy, and really the connection between us had to be very, very intense. I think, with Adèle, it’s something that will always be a part of me now. There was an exchange, and it was more than making a film. It was really a life experience.”

One scene from Gentle Monster lingered with me for weeks after I watched it: Lucy’s snotty, tearful confrontation with her husband at their family home as she pleads with him to tell the truth. This close-up of Seydoux not only accentuates her famous blue eyes but also shows her uncanny control of this character’s emotions. It feels, for lack of better words, real. Seydoux partly credits her empathy to being a mother of two children, but this scene goes beyond compassion; it shows how much of herself she gives to the character. Before filming this scene, Kreutzer offered little direction, letting her tap into how the character felt. “I prepare myself, and I have to just let myself go and be in the moment and just open up,” Seydoux says, suggesting that perhaps these emotions otherwise lie dormant within her.

I found this is what Seydoux means by “reaction.” Every role in her varied and extolled filmography functions as an avenue through which she can explore what it means to be human, what it means to feel. Her natural curiosity about the world and its inhabitants existed before she decided to act in her teens and will exist long into the future, even if acting jobs don’t remain on the table. “If it ended tomorrow, I’d be happy,” she says. “I will find a way. Now, I really feel that I’m acting because I still have the opportunity. But I have more things to explore, and I would love to have more experiences.”

#6659. Women’s Sports Teams Deserve Better Than What Their Homes Are Giving Them

Apparently, we’re going through this...again!

Gotham FC, the current reigning National Women’s Soccer Team champions, are on their way to winning another title. But their journey—which includes the chance to host their first semifinal game at home—has come with a major surprise: a change of venues. After their quarterfinal match against the Seattle Reign FC ended with a messed-up pitch and a fine against Gotham FC due to its conditions, the team was forced to find another place for their next match. The bigger issue? Their only option is a 5,000-person stadium located in Randall’s Island, New York.

Over 11,000 people made their way to Sports Illustrated Stadium—the home to Gotham FC as well as the MLS’s New York Red Bulls—to see them take down Seattle Reign FC. This means that less than half of that audience will be able to catch them at the match that can lead them to the finals.

While pitch conditions are absolutely important for the safety of the players and a big enough reason to change venues, the option to upgrade the pitch to a hybrid model was shut down by none other than New York Red Bulls due to a friendly game—AKA a game that does not count towards their season—they have on Saturday. Although the pitch would be ready for Gotham FC’s big game, RBNY shut down the plan as it wouldn’t be ready for their planned friendly. All of a sudden, a game-making match for a winning women’s team was being moved to a much smaller space due to a men’s game that does not count towards anything.

If you’re getting a sense of deja vu, that’s because this is not the first time a women’s team had to change their plans due to a men’s team. The WNBA’s Atlanta Dream were forced to move one of their WNBA Finals games from 8 p.m. to 12:30 p.m. on a Thursday. While the move was caused by them switching venues—going from their home at the Gateway Center, which fits 3,500 to the State Farm Arena which seats 17,000—it was also caused by a scheduled Atlanta Hawks game, a team that hasn’t won an NBA title since the 1950s.

It’s clear that no matter where they go, these women’s teams’ homes are not giving them what they need: a space where they can continue to host their ever-growing fanbases without fear of having to fall to the needs of a men’s team. The WNBA had a 21 percent increase in attendance to their games in 2026. The NWSL also gained some new faces in the crowd with a 3 percent increase, according to Sports Business Journal.

In 2028, Gotham FC will move to Etihad Park, a new stadium that will hold 25,000 fans in Queens, NYC. However, they’ll still have the problem of sharing the field with New York City FC, meaning this problem can always come up again.

A clear solution would be to give these incredible teams their own venues, but it’s evident that, financially, building a new stadium is not the easiest for some of these teams who are still growing. But, maybe next time, these men’s teams will just get the hint and let the real winners take over. It’s their home, too, after all.

#6658. A Menorca Farmhouse Finds Its Easy Elegance

Isabel López-Quesada honored the historical essence of a 19th-century Menorcan retreat while introducing vibrant colors and an elevated ease.

Above: In the living room, López-Quesada replicated the original ceiling beams and finished the walls in traditional Menorcan lime plaster. A sofa of her design is grouped with a 1960s Tito Agnoli rattan daybed, a Mexican palm leaf stool, and a custom cocktail table upholstered with a Moroccan rug.

For Spanish designer Isabel López-Quesada, summers tend to revolve around Biarritz, in southwestern France, where she has a home. Still, she always carves out time for Menorca, regarded as the most laid-back of Spain’s Balearic Islands. That vibe suits the ELLE Decor A-list designer, whose signature style is luxurious but unfussy, harmonizing old and new with breezy refinement.

Typically, López-Quesada goes there to relax and spend time with friends, but around five years ago she received a call from a Bilbao family who asked her to refresh their vacation home outside Mahón, Menorca’s capital. An old farm property, or finca, the five-bedroom residence required extensive updates. López-Quesada was steadfast in preserving such historical elements as a well and donkey stables, which she transformed into an indoor-outdoor bathroom with a patio and marble tub. “It was important to respect the style of Menorca,” she says. “We were very careful with the materials, so it all goes together and conveys the sentiment I want the house to have.”

López-Quesada founded her namesake studio as a precocious 20-year-old Madrileña 40 years ago. Initially inspired by her mother’s yen for decorating and the work of celebrated Spanish designers such as Paco Muñoz and Fernando Benjumea, López-Quesada garnered wide attention in the early 2000s, when she converted an old Madrid wax factory into an atelier and a luxurious home for her family, filled with antiques and contemporary pieces. Soon she had interest from clients all over the world. Today López-Quesada’s 12-person team includes one of her three adult children, daughter Isabel Llanza. Five years ago the pair co-founded the lifestyle brand Isita Home, selling products they make themselves or discover on their travels.

The Menorca project began by gutting the house’s interior and rebuilding it “in an old style,” López-Quesada says. Central to it all was adding custom windows and doors to each room so the island’s golden light could flood the spaces. Vaulted and beamed ceilings were left intact or were recreated. Reconfiguring the floor plan, López-Quesada eschewed a proper dining room in favor of an eat-in open kitchen. “Most of the time you are eating outside,” she says.

With Menorca’s unrelenting summer sun, bougainvillea-draped pergolas were a must for leisurely alfresco lunches and dinners. Swaths of white lantana and lily of the Nile border the terraces, while “the pool reflects the sky like a mirror and provides a refreshing feel,” López-Quesada says.

Her collaborator on the gardens was Spanish landscape architect Luis González-Camino. “My vision was to design a water-efficient Mediterranean garden, divided into distinct yet connected ‘garden rooms’ for different seasons and times of day,” he says. González-Camino preserved the property as a natural Mediterranean woodland, with wild olive trees providing cover.

Indoors, López-Quesada installed French terra-cotta floor tiles chosen for their aged look. The three fireplaces were all handcrafted in traditional Menorcan style—a little imperfect, painted white—and decorated with vintage tiles the designer keeps in her Madrid warehouse.

She offset the white ceilings and lime plaster walls with vibrant textiles and tilework in blues and greens inspired by Menorca’s native palette. An upstairs bedroom is anchored by an iron four-poster bed with a blue-and-white-striped canopy; its bath has a shower lined with lapis-colored zellige tiles. Doors, which across Menorca are customarily painted in bold shades, here are finished in verdant hues.

Custom cabinets are designed with whimsically patterned cutouts for airflow, since humidity is a major factor. “On the islands, if you close the cupboards completely, mushrooms will grow inside,” López-Quesada says.

Step Inside This Renovated Menorca Retreat

She arrayed the spaces with a lively mix of vintage and new furnishings, emphasizing organic materials: rattan chairs, bamboo shades, Moroccan rugs, lots of linen and cotton fabrics in spirited florals or stripes. “If I use a heavy piece, then the rest is lighter,” she says. The tableware maintains the blue and green theme, including pieces from Isita Home and hand-blown glassware from neighboring Mallorca.

In the end, the owners asked López-Quesada to redo their home in Spain’s Basque region as well. It’s one of several projects she currently has across the country, including reimagining a hotel in Barcelona, and she’s also designing homes in Paris, Shanghai, and the Dominican Republic. Many of her projects are with repeat clients. “That’s how it works,” the designer says. “It’s like a chain.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

Sarah Cristobal is a writer, editor, and creative consultant. She is currently editor-at-large for 10 Magazine USA, among other freelance projects.

#6657. Christy Turlington’s Ruby-Red Lipstick Is the Easy Makeup Secret for Women Over 40

Shop our favorite creamy, hydrating, and smoothing lipstick formulas

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The last few years have been marked by the “Lip Balm Wars.” That is, every brand—from Hailey Bieber’s Rhode to prestige labels like Chanel and Dior—has launched more sheer, glossy, and hydrating formulas than classic lipstick bullets. But even though we all own more than 10 emotional-support lip balms in various shades, flavors, and finishes, there's still nothing more powerful than a statement-making lip. This is especially true for women over 40.

Last night, supermodel Christy Turlington stepped out at the Museum of Modern Art’s 2026 Annual Film Benefit in New York, wearing a knit dress from Matthieu Blazy’s fall 2026 Chanel collection. A dress this graphic and eye-catching needs makeup to balance it, and Turlington paired it with black eyeliner and a glossy ruby-red lipstick.

While we don’t know the exact shade she wore last night, it’s clearly a formula that sits somewhere between a satin lipstick and a gloss-balm, with pigment that doesn’t sacrifice moisture or shine. Turlington is a Lancôme ambassador, so it could be the brand’s Lip Idole Butterglow Hydrating Lip Balm or L’Absolu Rouge Cream Lipstick. Otherwise, Chanel makes a gorgeous option in both the Rouge Coco Flash and Rouge Allure Extrait formulas. These formulas subtly plump and moisturize dry lips while blurring lip lines and imperfections.

Lancôme L'Absolu Rouge Moisturizing Cream Lipstick in 397 Berry Noir

Chanel Hydrating Vibrant Shine Lip Colour

Lancome Lip Idole Squalane-12 Butterglow Hydrating Lip Balm

The best part of this look is that it proves you don’t need to match your lipstick exactly to your outfit, as long as they’re in the same color family. While the Chanel dress is more of an orange-red knit, Turlington’s lipstick is a more neutral ruby that harmonizes with the warm tones and shades in the rest of her look. Plus, raspberry or ruby-red lipstick can brighten your face in seconds, even with little to no other makeup.

Bold lipstick is a bit outside Turlington’s day-to-day comfort zone; she told Bazaar recently that she’s “definitely an old-school Carmex or ChapStick kind of person, because I’m constantly reapplying it to my dry lips, especially in the winter.” The rest of her routine is equally as effortless: “I wash my face in the morning and moisturize a lot. Sometimes I even double-moisturize because, especially at this time of year, I get dry skin. And then, I’ll add a nice little layer of foundation, some blush, and mascara, and that’s kind of it, I’m on my way.”

#6653. Elias Matso Is the Twisted Corsetier Dressing Sydney Sweeney and Jenna Ortega

The young designer talked to Bazaar about craftsmanship, fittings, and developing his signature technique

These days, Elias Matso is known for twisted corsetry worn by the likes of Sydney Sweeney and Jenna Ortega. But the first time he noticed the impact of a good costume, he was a kid watching Julie Andrews in Victor/Victoria. He sat by the TV watching the Oscar-winning actor perform “Le Jazz Hot!” in Art Deco gowns, decadent headpieces, and dramatic feathered capes. “I know she was acting in that scene, but I just felt like she was so happy to be wearing those costumes,” he tells Harper’s Bazaar. “And I was obsessed with that.”

Matso is now 24, and it’s easy to see the impact of that moment on his creative approach. A recent graduate of Polimoda in Florence with Charles James, Bob Mackie, Mugler, and Mr. Pearl on his mood board, he crafts avant-garde, couture corsetry. (It’s perhaps no surprise that he has a history of making costumes for drag performers.) His approach is personal and intimate, rooted in hand craftsmanship and collaborative dialogue with clients.

In October 2025, Sweeney made waves when she wore a crystal naked dress, pulled from Christian Cowan’s Spring/Summer 2026 collection and made in collaboration with the young designer. The piece featured twisted corsetry and a semi-sheer body that hugged the Euphoria star’s body—and, naturally, the public had quite a lot to say about it. “I did not anticipate that it would be as big as it was,” the artist admits. “It was really cool that the conversation left fashion and became a bigger conversation about women’s bodies and what modesty should look like or shouldn’t look like. And how a lot of the world doesn’t know what boobs look like.”

While the discourse surprised Matso, it reflected one of his own core beliefs: that “fashion is at least 50% the person who’s wearing the thing.” He keeps this in mind during fittings, ensuring clients feel comfortable and beautiful every step of the way. Below, he talks to Bazaar about his early references, his focus on craftsmanship, and the colorful steel-boning dress recently worn by Jenna Ortega.

As a kid, I had a paper doll of myself, and whenever there were clothes that I wanted that I didn’t have, I would make them for the paper doll. It was pocket sized, and whenever I was bored—which was all the time, because I have attention deficit issues—I would pull it out and play.

It is. It was my third year and I wanted to explore the tension that children hold, when they feel like they’re growing faster than they can keep up with, and feeling at odds with themselves, because that’s how I felt when I was a kid. There was this feeling, especially in the queer world, of being pulled in different directions, gender-wise. And so I had developed that idea, and then through sketching and conversations with my teachers, we started getting more and more towards this very literal idea of a twist. And then the collaboration that I did with Christian Cowan pushed it further.

I was in New York over the summer before my senior year, and I was looking for jobs and not getting anything. I started looking into scooping ice cream at Van Leeuwen’s and stuff like that. And then, one day, I got a text from Alicia Keys’s stylist and a DM from Christian that was like, “Do you want to do some dresses for my show?” It was a good day.

It actually wasn’t originally supposed to be that fabric. We couldn’t find the fabric we wanted, and we couldn’t figure out how to put as many stones as we needed onto the fabric that we found. Then we just found this sheer fabric, and we were like, “Okay, it’s going to be the original dress, but it’s going to be completely see-through.”

I made it to fit this gorgeous six-foot-tall model from Finland. And this is why fit is so fun, because with that dress, there was this miracle where it fit her so perfectly one way, and then it fit Sydney, who has completely different proportions, in this totally other, also beautiful way. Often when things get pulled from a runway, they don’t fit like that. And it was just really cool to see.

I really like meeting people in person when I can because it just always creates a better outcome. I feel like the conversation is so much about your personality, but also your physiology, the way you’re built and the way you’re made up. People’s bodies respond differently. So it’s really important to do fittings and to check in with people before, during, and after the fitting, and ask, “How intense of an experience do you want this to be? What will make you feel the most beautiful, the most comfortable?”

Sometimes a little bit of discomfort is part of being comfortable, but you have to have signed up for that. That’s my thing that I’ve learned: Discomfort, if you’re prepared, can be part of it. But there has to be a conversation if discomfort is involved, or else we are forgetting that fashion is at least 50% the person who’s wearing the thing.

When I start a collection, I’m always looking through the archives of a museum, like the Whitney or often the Guggenheim, because you have less control over what you’re going to find. [For this piece,] I had found one painting that led me to Mark Ranger’s website. And we got in touch and then collaborated the whole year. It turns out we explore a lot of the same themes. Here, the dress was supposed to be about time and our body’s relationship to gravity and this idea of melting, not in a way that we’re trying to resist and slow down, but in a way that’s just natural and beautiful.

The goal was to make something unrecognizable, but using recognizable techniques. I already had a lot of steel boning because it’s the same stuff that’s inside all these corsets. And so I just got more, and then I started chopping it up into smaller and smaller pieces. I was able to create a relationship with Preciosa, who does crystals for a lot of big brands—I met them through a trip that they did to my school. I just came up with this weird technique that was, I guess, a test of my own physical and mental strength in a way.

I didn’t make it with Jenna in mind. I never thought it would have all these different lives and stages.

I guess I’m seeing it less and less valued in fashion. And even when something is hand-done and comes out gorgeous and successful, the people that wind up getting the praise for those things are not the people who do the thing, but the people who have curated those artisans—the artistic directors, the designers. My friends and I have just graduated, we’re looking for work, and I see my friends who have really singular skills getting so undervalued for their work and their time. That is something that is really frustrating to me. And in an age where so many things can be falsified via technology and the internet, I think things that you can’t fake are how something’s made, how something stands up over time, how something feels on the inside when you put it on. And designers care less and less about that, I guess.

I’m in a transitional stage where I’m not sure what I’m gonna do next. Every day, I get people asking me to make dresses on this scale for free or for bafflingly low amounts of money. You know, I’m told all the time that there’s no budget for things. People want couture for no budget. People in this industry have no shame when it comes to asking for that. For emerging designers, there are so many of us that they know that if I can’t supply what they’re looking for, they will find another independent designer who has the resources or the funds or the ability to provide that thing for less than what it’s worth. I do worry about that. But this press is making me think that maybe there is a niche for something that walks the line between art and fashion and not in the aesthetic sense, which people talk about all the time, but in the financial and logistical sense. So that’s what I’m trying to figure out.

Also, I found that if people are asking for things for free, it’s very rarely the client themselves. It’s often their team. I love the art of styling, but that is another reason that I find one-on-one relationships and conversations with the person who you’re going to be dressing really important. It makes them understand the level of attention or care that they are interacting with.

This is still in process, but I’m working on a piece with Laverne Cox. I always wanted to work with her. And then starting to work with her, I was like, “I was right.” Because she has such appreciation and respect for the process of everything and the details and how everything works. She’s a really respectful and engaging patron, and we’ve had a lot of interesting conversations about the designers that we both look up to.

For me, I want to take the momentum that I have and do something that feels exciting with it. And at the same time, I am not sure that I have the experience to start a company, you know? I am in conversations with lots of people who I’ve always wanted to collaborate with, and it’s so exciting. Really, really exciting and mind-blowing. And at the same time, I’m reaching out to brands that I admire to see what openings they have because we all need stability. I’m trying to remain as flexible as possible.

#6652. 45 Amazon Stocking Stuffers Under $50 That Make Surprisingly Great Gifts

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There's something for everyone on this list.

As you start buying big gifts, don’t forget about the little ones. Sometimes, these are the hardest items to get, especially if you’re working with a budget or trying to fit them into a smaller slot, like in an advent calendar or in a stocking. Stocking stuffers, in particular, can be tricky to shop for because you want the small item to feel unique to the recipient and useful, and if you’re getting the person more than one item, everything has to fit inside a 16 to 18 inches long stocking. The only place to turn for a task like this is Amazon.

The retailer offers a wide variety of stocking stuffers, so whether you want an item specific to your person, like headbands for runners, silky sleep masks for the traveler, or an item as seemingly random as an electric candle lighter, you can order all the products in one shopping trip. To save you a scroll, though, we found the best Amazon stocking stuffers below, all under $50. Surely something from this 45-item list will spark joy in your gift recipient’s life.

Check out more editor-curated gift ideas from Amazon

If they’re always showing off their record collection or asking you to pick out a vinyl to play during gatherings, you will light up their world when you gift them these coasters.

Support their newfound obsession with keeping their sourdough alive and baking bread by giving them this adorable ornament. You can even have it personalized for an extra special touch

For the friend or family member who loves collecting small items or building their own collectibles, gift them these mini LEGO plants. They rock back and forth!

Electric lighters come in handy if you’re not fond of lighters and open flames. When their candle wick burns too far down into the glass vessel, this will make lighting a shorter wick more seamless.

An AirTag makes for a nice stocking stuffer because it may be a device they don’t think about buying for themselves until they actually need it. Whether they decide to stick it in their luggage to track their suitcase or on their keys, which they’re always losing, it’s a tiny but mighty present to give.

How adorable are these magnetic bookmarks in the shape of a peering black cat? If they are bookworms and they own or like cats, this would be the perfect stocking stuffer that acknowledges both their loves.

This playful wine bottle pourer is in the shape of a bird, and it chirps every time liquid flows through its beak. Customers say that whenever they break this out, their guests are amused, turning an ordinary moment at the dinner table into a fun conversation starter.

#6650. Everything We Know About ‘The Body’ Season 2

Need more of The Body? The Netflix series is off to an excellent start, with fresh new characters and spooky, sexy situations that really take some turns. Here’s what we know about whether or not The Body has been renewed for season 2.

Set at a Catholic high school in the so-called innocence of 2004, the first season of The Body is about two girls who can’t express their feelings for each other, an ambitious dance team heading to regionals, and a mass hysteria that leads to prophetic visions and Satanic rituals. Totes normal, right? Once you’ve sped through season 1, you’ll be on the edge of your seat waiting to see what happens next.

As of the season 1 drop, The Body has not yet been renewed for season 2. It’s still early, however, and the buzz around the show has been good. Fingers crossed!

The Body is an original idea from Quinn Shepherd, not based on any pre-existing IP. So what happens next is entirely up to her and her team of writers and producers. Luckily, the first season ends with a cliff-hanger and a lot of unanswered questions.

“We always knew that that was where the show was going, Shepherd said in a post-mortem interview with Deadline about *that* thing with Grace. “From the beginning, that was the arc of the first season, and I think a lot of that has to do with things we would love to do next season and how to keep carrying the story.”

Executive producer Gina Gammell also mentioned Anya confessing to Father Franklin, Maddie’s prophecy, and the mystery over whether Tyler and Lisbeth actually slept together as potential avenues for season 2.

Given that the show doesn’t have a lot of movie stars with busy schedules in major roles, and filming on season 1 took place in 2025, it stands to reason that The Body could easily return in late 2027. But sometimes Netflix shows take a little longer. So again... cross your fingers and do everything short of ritual sacrifice to ensure The Body comes back for season 2 as soon as possible.

#6649. Denis Villeneuve Talks ‘Dune: Part Three’ and Rebooting James Bond: ‘We Cannot F*ck It Up.’

“I’m out of a job,” Denis Villeneuve said, jokingly. Hours earlier, Villeneuve had signed off on the IMAX 70-milimeter print of Dune: Part Three, officially wrapping post-production on that film—and in turn, a decade-long chapter of the Quebecois writer-director’s working life.

Fresh off this milestone, Villeneuve and GQ had come to the Lucas Museum of Narrative Art in Los Angeles for a conversation and a private screening of Dune: Part Two, presented by OMEGA, in honor of his anointment as GQ’s Director of the Year. Global Editorial Director Adam Baidawi introduced Villeneuve before the director and GQ’s Zach Baron dove into a conversation that covered his original Dune plans, where Dune: Part Three breaks from its predecessors, and Villeneuve’s feelings about taking over the James Bond franchise.

GQ’s Adam Baidawi and Zach Baron with Denis Villeneuve

“I feel light,” the director of the year told Baron, then added, “Numb.” He was still processing Dune’s end, he said—while also coming to grips with closing the book on “the most rewarding, inspiring, and best ten years of my life as a filmmaker.”

Villeneuve’s first two Dune films have raked in more than a billion dollars at the box office. Part Three appears likely to nearly double that when it hits theaters (and locks up most of the world’s IMAX screens) a week before Christmas. But at one point during his conversation with Baron, the director pushed back gently on the notion of these movies as a “franchise.”

“There was nothing industrial in the way they were made,” Villeneuve said. They were the work of “a bunch of friends”—a familial crew, out in the desert putting things together.

“In French,” the Quebecois director continued, “there is a word—artisanal. They are very personal. Made with a lot of care. They’re little big things—but in a way, they are delicate.”

After all, as sprawling and cosmically grand as they are, these movies are also a deeply personal project for the director, born of images that took root in his head when he first read Frank Herbert’s thick-as-a-brick, notoriously adaptation-resistant 1965 novel Dune at age 13. Later, as Villeneuve racked up credits and clout, people would ask him what his dream project was; the answer was always Dune.

“I kept saying to myself, Someone else will do it one day,” he told Baron. “Another filmmaker will adapt this book again. And I kept waiting, and I kept waiting, until I felt ready to do it myself.”

Herbert wrote five escalatingly bonkers Dune sequels before his death in 1986, but Villeneuve intended to walk away from the table after adapting the first book across two films.

“Trilogies are very dangerous,” he told Baron. “The first movie is a promise, and if you fulfill the promise with the second one, then there’s a feeling of security that comes with that, and you feel that you have a recipe…That’s the trap. Because then you repeat yourself.”

But after wrapping Part Two, Villeneuve found himself unable to stop thinking about Dune Messiah—the second Dune novel, which Herbert supposedly wrote in frustration, believing the audience had missed the point by embracing Paul Atreides as a hero.

Villeneuve said his take on Messiah is “a different beast” than the first two films, built it around the tension between Timothée Chalamet’s Paul and his Fremen consort Chani (Zendaya), who Villeneuve has re-imagined—in a pivotal departure from Herbert’s version—as a nonbeliever who takes a critical view of Paul’s rise to power.

Neel Shah, Timothy Simons, and Michael Lasker

Of course, the specter (S.P.E.C.T.R.E.?) of another franchise hung over the proceedings at the Lucas Museum. Villeneuve, after all, is only technically between jobs; he’s a self-described “monomaniac” who insists that he can only hyper-focus on one project at a time and has been locked in on Dune for a decade, but the as-yet-untitled 26th Bond film is in motion, with Villeneuve signed on to direct.

GQ’s Adam Baidawi, Denis Villeneuve, and OMEGA’s Lucien Jornod

He could say almost nothing in public about the project, or even when he’d begin shooting it—he joked about having “the target of the sniper” on his chest—but spoke of feeling the responsibility not to fail: “We cannot fuck it up. We do not have the choice.”

Over the past week, the casting rumors around Villeneuve’s Bond reboot have reached what our UK colleagues accurately termed the “fever-dream” stage. It might be Callum Turner or Slow Horses’ Jack Lowden, but it might be Robert Pattinson, who’s emerged as a surprise front-runner after months of supposition about the part being earmarked for a relative unknown. At some point, toward the end of Tuesday’s conversation, the subject of Bond 26’s lead could no longer be avoided.

“Has the role of James Bond been cast?” Baron asked the director.

“It’s—it’s in the process. In the process,” Villeneuve said with a smile. “I am pushing the gas.”

“Blink once for Robert Pattinson,” Baron suggested, “and twice for Callum Turner.”

Villeneuve, for the record, did not blink. ✦

GQ would like to thank Legendary Entertainment, Warner Bros., and Brianna Smith.

#6648. A Minimalist Fashion Editor Gets a Y2K Makeover

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Nicola Brognano, the creative director of 7 for All Mankind, gave me the ultimate Y2K style treatment

On a recent Tuesday afternoon, I stopped by 7 for All Mankind’s SoHo store to meet Nicola Brognano, the brand’s newest creative director, for a one-on-one styling session. I felt a bit nervous, knowing I’d need to step outside of my sartorial safe space. For reference, my personal style tends to lean more minimal, a “classics with a twist” vibe. When I entered the store, I was wearing a striped button-down shirt, black jeans, and a trench coat—my typical uniform. Immediately, Brognano, wearing a leather jacket layered over a hoodie, was warm and kind. I felt my anxiety lessen, knowing I was going to be in good hands.

Brognano’s aesthetic couldn’t be further from my own. At Blumarine, where he served as creative director from 2020 to 2023, he transformed the brand away from bohemian, romantic whimsy and injected it with a jolt of Y2K energy (butterfly crop tops, slinkier dresses, and low-rise pants). In 2025, the designer took the creative helm of 7 for All Mankind and staged the brand’s inaugural runway show in February during New York Fashion Week. The appointment was a fitting choice for the American-born brand, which, when it launched in the 2000s, had every It girl wearing its low-rise jeans, from Britney Spears and Christina Aguilera to Nicole Richie and Paris Hilton. It was a time when Gossip Girl was the hottest show and Mary-Kate and Ashley Olsen were regularly spotted around the downtown New York scene with skinny scarves wrapped around their necks.

Brognano brought an exciting new energy to 7 for All Mankind that built upon the label’s roots. His collection had a nostalgic Y2K-era undertone, but polished up for 2026. Models walked the runway in shrunken black peacoats with exaggerated shoulders, skinny scarves, cropped leather jackets, and sheer, silky blouses.

Ultra-skinny jeans, wax-coated maxi skirts, and babydoll dresses were key hero pieces. New York’s coolest It girl, Chloë Sevigny, stars in the fall campaign; images from the shoot appear on digital screens throughout the store.

When he joined the brand, Brognano tells me, “I really wanted to bring that sexiness back.” In designing the collection, “I imagined a super cool, strong woman with an eccentric attitude,” he says. Could it be me?

After we met and assessed my sizing, Brognano went straight to pulling looks for me. The first look I tried on was a semisheer blouse with an exaggerated pussy bow at the neck, ultra-low-rise dual-panel waxed skinny jeans (a leather-like finish on the front and rigid denim on the back), and, to top it off, a shrunken black peacoat. For the finishing touch, I slid on a pair of sky-high platform heels. I felt like I just stepped out of the early aughts and was ready to head to the club (in a good way).

I rarely wear heels. (I’m more of a flats-and-loafers kind of girl.) I don’t think I’ve ever worn platform heels in my life. While walking in them posed its own challenge, I did feel an overwhelming sense of confidence and sexiness. “The most important thing for the collection, for me, was the attitude,” Brognano tells me about the inspiration, adding, “I started thinking about Mary-Kate and Ashley Olsen at the time. And a mix of girls like that—a little bit rockish, a little bit indie sleaze.” I felt this spirit wearing his clothes, a sensibility that I hadn’t experienced since my early 20s and one I thought I’d moved far away from. I am currently nine months postpartum. The outfit gave me a jolt of energy that felt exhilarating.

7 for All Mankind Double-Breasted High Shoulder Jacket

7 for All Mankind Silk Bow Houndstooth Shirt

The next look was a nice medium between what I would normally wear—a sweater and jeans—and something more off-kilter. The look was amplified in small ways: instead of a crewneck, it was a cape sweater with an extra-high neck; the jeans weren’t dark and tailored but light, fitted, and straight; the sky-high heels remained. I felt a little bohemian in this look, in a good way.

7 for All Mankind High Neck Shawl Sweater

For the final look, Brognano styled me in a deep-plum cinched leather peacoat. Underneath, I wore a silky, sheer brown bow-tie blouse and dark-gray vintage-style coated jeans. I was surprised by how soft and comfortable this particular pair of jeans was.

This look felt more grown-up and polished than the other two, something I could wear out to dinner. While I’m not hitting the club these days, the clothes still felt like they were designed for someone like me too.

7 for All Mankind Double-Breasted High Neck Leather Jacket

7 for All Mankind Long-Sleeve Bow Silk Shirt

7 for All Mankind Calie Straight in Coated Denim

While the skinny jean is the centerpiece of the collection, Brognano explains, “Denim is very democratic, and you can choose your shape every season because you have to feel good with your denim, and it has to give you the best shape for your body.… I think it’s something very personal.” Despite the fact that I’ve been a non-skinny-jean wearer for over a decade, Brognano found a sweet spot in my comfort level.

It was fun to play dress-up and step outside my traditional style for once. Sometimes all it takes is a push. While I won’t be wearing head-to-toe leather, leather skinnies, or platform heels every day, I left the day feeling more inspired, more confident, and more eager to have fun with my outfits again—not for anyone else, but just for myself.

#6647. These Tinted SPFs Protect Your Under-Eyes and Conceal Dark Circles

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Sunscreen is always important, but when one product can also brighten your complexion and take the place of your favorite concealer, it becomes impossible to forget. Tinted SPF offers the best of both worlds, helping protect your skin from the sun while giving it the appearance of a more even complexion. And when it’s made specifically for the eye area, it can make one of the most delicate parts of your skin care routine feel a whole lot easier.

“Tinted sunscreens are one of those rare beauty products where the cosmetic benefit and the dermatological benefit really align,” says Dr. Pearl Ugwu-Dike, a board-certified dermatologist. “You’re protecting your skin from tomorrow’s sun damage while making it look good today.” As for why they’re especially useful under the eyes, the skin in that area is some of the most delicate that we have on our body. “That makes it particularly susceptible to visible signs of photoaging, like fine lines, crepiness, hyperpigmentation,” Dr. Ugwu-Dike says. “It’s also often a place that gets neglected when applying sunscreen, conventionally.” That’s why the best tinted SPFs for under-eyes are so special: They offer protection while providing immediate color correction and a brighter-looking appearance, helping to camouflage under-eye bags and dark circles.

Of course, the right formula depends on how much coverage you want, your skin type, and how your under-eye area tends to behave throughout the day. Some options are sheer and brightening, while others can pull double duty as a true concealer. Ahead, we’ve rounded up the best tinted SPFs for under-eyes so you can find the one that fits seamlessly into your morning routine.

This is what happens when an eye cream, sunscreen, and concealer all decide to overachieve. “I love that it addresses two of my biggest concerns with the under-eye area, which are pigmentation and photoaging,” says Dr. Pearl Ugwu-Dike, an NYC-based board-certified dermatologist. “I also really like that it combines peptides, hyaluronic acid, and a cooling applicator.”

Key ingredients: Titanium dioxide, zinc oxide, hyaluronic acid, peptides

Customer review: “This is a wonderful product! Skin care, SPF, and even color corrector in one. It goes on smoothly, does not crease, does not sting the eyes. The peachy color gives a natural concealing effect. I can't recommend this enough.”

This product is for anyone who wants their under-eye area to look like they just got eight hours of sleep followed by a facial. “The formulation is really smart,” says Dr. Ugwu-Dike. It contains both zinc oxide and titanium dioxide, along with snow mushroom, peptides, ceramides, and sodium PCA. “The snow mushroom is going to act like a humectant, which is particularly helpful in the delicate skin on the eye area where dehydration can exaggerate fine lines,” she adds.

Key ingredients: Titanium dioxide, zinc oxide, snow mushroom, peptides, ceramides, sodium PCA

Customer review: “This eye corrector feels great going on and covers dark circles very well. Love it and plan to keep purchasing it in the future.”

Some days you get eight hours of sleep, and other days you need a very convincing color corrector. This formula from RMS “relies on sheer, peachy undertones to neutralize the appearance of darkness while allowing skin to look like skin,” says Dr. Ugwu-Dike. “I also really like that it includes caffeine and coffee seed extract in the formula that might temporarily improve the appearance of puffiness.”

Key ingredients: Zinc oxide, caffeine, coffee seed extract

Customer review: “Love this eye cream. It makes the entire eye area look better. I use it every day, and it covers beautifully—no need for concealer over it. It lasts all day. My eyes are very sensitive and this does not bother them at all.”

“I think this formula is perfect for someone who doesn't necessarily have substantial pigmentation under the eyes, but wants to improve a tired or dull appearance,” says Dr. Ugwu-Dike. The lightweight formula approaches under-eye darkness through illumination rather than heavy coverage, delivering a bright appearance that’s never cakey. It also has caffeine for de-puffing and green tea extract for soothing.

Key ingredients: Titanium dioxide, zinc oxide, snow mushroom, peptides, ceramides, sodium PCA

Customer review: “I’ve been using this for nearly a year now and it is my holy grail product! It glides on well for me - no pilling. My skin leans towards dry and sensitive. I’ve never had any issues with this eye cream. I use it in the morning and it brightens beautifully. It acts great as a primer. On no-makeup days, it brightens me up!”

#6646. 10 Minimalist Thanksgiving Manicures That Are Perfect for the Holiday—How to Recreate

Celebrity nail artists share their favorite chic yet simple looks.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

First and foremost, Thanksgiving is about spending time with family, and then, eating all the food you can possibly ingest in the stretchiest pair of pants you own. (The two priorities can be slightly interchangeable.) And while you’re planning your outfit for the holiday, why not have a chic manicure to match? I mean, your fingers will be the ones at center stage holding a fork all night.

If you don’t want to go all out with adorned turkeys and food-themed designs, there are plenty of Thanksgiving manicure ideas that are equal parts chic and on-theme. “Minimalist doesn’t have to mean forgettable,” says celebrity nail artist Max Estrada. “Sometimes a beautiful neutral, a little sparkle, or the perfect French tip says everything.” Below, I chatted with manicurists for their favorite nail looks for the holiday feast.

Keep scrolling minimalist Thanksgiving nail ideas, from earthy polishes and elevated designs that will look gorgeous well into the end of November.

“For those who prefer a minimalist look, I’d recommend a classic French manicure with a seasonal twist, such as chocolate brown tips on a sheer nude base,” says celebrity nail artist Vikki Swan.

“Another beautiful option is using different shades from the same color family across the nails, transitioning from lighter to deeper tones,” Swan explains. “It’s a simple yet elegant way to capture the fall mood without intricate nail art.” This easy gradient requires no intricate art skills, and brings the fall foliage to your fingtertips.

“Cat-eye effects give those rich colors movement and dimension, so the manicure becomes interesting through the finish itself,” says Estrada. “The color and light are doing the work, and make a statement without adding any other nail art.” All it takes is your favorite shifting polish and a magnet to get the look.

Polka dots are yes, chic, but more importantly super easy to DIY if you still want to lean into a low-effort design. Choose your favorite contrasting autumnal hues and use a dotting tool (or even a toothpick) in the lighter polish to create the look. Or go for the reverse finish, by using the lighter color as the base.

Burgundy French tips are another favorite minimalist option of Swan’s, and adding a cat-eye effect gives some dimension to the classic yet seasonal manicure.

“Deep burgundy and wine red are also beautiful seasonal choices,” Swan says. “These colors complement fall wardrobes perfectly and create that cozy autumn feeling.” Some of our favorite quintessential fall reds include essie’s Bordeaux (how fitting!) and Sally Hansen’s Pomp & Plum.

“During this time of year, warm, rich shades are especially popular, including chocolate brown, espresso, and caramel,” says Swan. To get the coffee-esque hue, try OPI’s Espresso Your Inner Self.

If you want to lean into the theme of the feast, go for this subtle pumpkin pie-coded manicure. Choose two shades of orange and create a light-to dark gradient by blending the hues outwards with a small sponge.

These translucent jelly nails have the same decadent finish as a sweet Thanksgiving treat. To get the look, apply a sheer coat or two of your favorite polish, then seal it all in with a high-shine top coat for that signature glassy effect.

Pumpkin spice season goes well into November, and this reflective manicure is proof. Start with an orange base, then layer on a silver or orange chrome topper for a metallic finish that feels festive but still wearable well beyond the big feast.

Jasmine Hyman is the assistant beauty editor at Cosmo who covers all things nails—from the latest design trends to new polish launches. For this story, she consulted celebrity nail artists for their favorite minimalist Thanksgiving nail ideas.

#6645. I'm the Friend Everyone Texts for Tableware Recs. Here's Where I Send Them

Consider this your shortcut to a beautifully dressed table.

The art of tablescaping starts with some really beautiful tableware. No disrespect to placemats, but the glasses, plates, and cutlery that your guests use tend to take the spotlight. Everything tastes better coming out of a fun wine glass. And presentation is key. The right serving platter makes your weeknight leftovers look Michelin-starred. We know your design taste is as unique as your palate, so we wanted to make your shopping experience as seamless as possible. Below, we reveal over a dozen of the very best tableware brands on the market.

Whether you’re a frequent dinner party host or you’re romanticizing dinners for one, you deserve to find something truly unique for your dining table. We focused our search on dinnerware, glassware, cutlery, and serving essentials. (Table linens matter, but that’s a whole other article.) Of course, we started with the pieces filling our editors’ kitchens and dining rooms. That’s where we keep the cake plates for our favorite little treats, and high-quality wine glasses that aren’t too fragile for busy kitchens. But trust us, after going to the best design markets in the country and abroad, these are the brands that wow us year after year. They’re built to last and make every host’s life way easier.

East Fork was founded by Henri Matisse’s great-grandson, and the artistry shows in every piece. The hand-thrown pottery is crafted from regionally sourced clay in Asheville, North Carolina. The lightly speckled, colorful stoneware plates are a favorite of Associate Shopping Editor Janae McKenzie, especially the Matisse Cake Plate sets.

Fable is a tableware brand designed for the frequent family dinner host. The brand’s Canyon Clay collection brings a warm, sunbaked terracotta to their durable serveware. It’s dishwasher-safe and meant to hold up to your messiest meals, with a touch of elegance.

If you’re looking for the kind of beautiful plates and cups that can hold up to outdoor use, look no further than Thomas Fuchs’s melamine collection. These chip-proof, shatter-proof plates are designed for al fresco meals, and the half-and-half design adds some playful color.

We already loved Hommey for their viral striped cotton bedding, but the brand has brought that signature look to your tabletop. Even your cutlery deserves a little color-drenching, and certain colors are already selling out. It’s the kind of small upgrade that makes every meal feel a bit more special.

Let’s talk glassware: no one does clarity like Simon Pearce. It catches the light beautifully. Every single glassware piece from this brand is handmade by one of a few expert artisans, and the imperfection of handmade goods is celebrated here. (You can even get the extra quirky ones—called Seconds—for a discounted price.)

Chances are you’ve seen Serax tableware in a designer department store. The brand has decades of tabletop excellence under its belt, and its designer collaborations result in delightfully creative pieces. The Carte Blanche collection, designed to look like hand-drawn sketches, is one of our favorites.

Anthropologie is the home of little luxuries for everywhere in your home, but we’re especially partial to their small space-friendly tabletop pieces. Espresso cups and dessert plates just make breakfast feel way more glamorous. The gilded Bistro Tile collection is perfect for anyone wanting some Parisian flair in their kitchen.

Sabre is another iconic name in the world of tabletop, and one look at their stainless steel cutlery sets will tell you why. You can choose handles crafted from teak wood, tortoiseshell or a glossy lacquer, and you’ll love setting the table with them at dinnertime.

It really is the little things that make a difference, and at Mogutable, those are the irresistibly cute chopsticks rests. Imagine a few of these adorable food-shaped rests scattered across your dinner table, or letting each dinner guest select one of their own before noodles are served.

Arhaus is already one of our favorite furniture brands, so it’s no surprise the sophisticated home destination has incredible serveware. Their dinnerware sets get pretty pricey, so we suggest starting with their hosting essentials: napkin rings, coasters, and cocktail shakers that help you host like a pro.

Would you believe it if we told you those are paper plates? Table Two Studio is all about disposable plates for easy cleanup, without sacrificing style. The botanical patterns remind us of archival fabric prints from our favorite textile houses. Your guests will feel extra special, and you won’t spend hours doing dishes.

The Museum of Modern Art really knows how to curate an inspiring collection, and that extends to their home goods. It’s McKenzie’s first stop whenever she wants to find a cool hostess gift. Their glassware is unmatched, especially for any fans of colored glass or playful motifs.

Fishs Eddy dinnerware pays homage to a symbol of Americana and hospitality: the diner. Their green-band dishes, checkered plates, and simple white mugs are all evocative of a steaming diner breakfast after a long night. All of that cozy nostalgia warms up your family meals.

If you love all things spiraled and swirly, this is the glassware brand for you. The delicate pops of color and squiggled stems on the glass pitchers make even water look like a fun beverage. Perfect for cocktail hour, and sure to be treasured.

If you want to spot an Ichendorf Milano glass in the wild, look for colorful motifs from nature. A stem shaped like Lily of the Valley flowers or a fish at the bottom of a tumbler are telltale signs. It’s the kind of glassware you’d love to put on your registry and display.

Jono Pandolfi plates are a fine dining staple. They’re served at luxury restaurants across the country—you can even see them listed on the page of each glaze color. So if you like to get experimental in the kitchen and want your dishes to look Michelin star, you can literally use what the pros use.

The brand that ushered in a ceramic cookware takeover also has beautiful ceramic tableware. They have dinnerware bundles that are easy to build on over time, perfect for cozy brunches even in tiny apartment kitchens to feel a little fancier. It’s a starter set that doesn’t feel like it’s from the bargain bin.

#6644. Anna Sui on the Five Films That Inspired Her Aesthetic

Here at Bazaar, we are just as devoted to the well-dressed mind as we are to the well-dressed woman. In fact, we find that one often engenders the other. In our new series The Well-Dressed Mind, we examine the intersection point between style and intellect by asking industry professionals and tastemakers for their cultural recommendations.

Anna Sui’s namesake brand is rooted in a reverence for the past. By blending niche references across time periods, she makes her clothes feel distinctly now. Part of a defining generation of downtown New York designers in the ’80s and ’90s, Sui invokes her love of vintage fashion, design, photography, and art as the basis of her work. The feel of the resulting collections is at once girlish, grunge, ’70s, punk, and bohemian.

Last week, the Friends of the Costume Institute, an organization attached to the Met museum, asked Sui to dive into her vast collection of archival references and select a film that most influenced her as a designer for a screening. (Sui also funds a yearlong internship for the department.) She picked the 1970 movie Performance, directed by Donald Cammell and Nicolas Roeg, which depicts a London gangster’s identity crisis while in hiding at the home of a rock star (played by Mick Jagger) and his two girlfriends, one of whom is played by style icon Anita Pallenberg. You can see why this would appeal to her; the trippy, ’70s clash of glam and bohemia is very Sui.

Hungry for more, we asked Sui to outline the onus behind that selection and four additional films that had the greatest influence on her aesthetic.

Directed by Donald Cammell and Nicolas Roeg

“Anita Pallenberg has always been my style icon, especially since I saw her amazing wardrobe throughout Performance: the fringe kaftan, antique lace top, pink velvet pants, fox fur coat, silver platform sandals, texture zigzag tights, and the slinky black satin dresses.”

“This film is set in a brothel in 1930s fascist Italy. The lingerie, piano shawls with smoky eye makeup and wavy bobs looked straight out of a Sarah Moon photograph.”

“Jane Birkin is a fashion model with a Peeping Tom for a neighbor. He’s obsessed with what he perceives as her glamorous fashion lifestyle, and he comes to the rescue when she decides to do herself in. It’s got sets by the artistic collective the Fool, which designed the Beatles’ Apple Boutique, and a George Harrison soundtrack to transport you to the swinging ’60s in London.”

“The Who’s rock opera has a star-studded cast: Ann-Margret, Oliver Reed, Jack Nicholson, Elton John, Eric Clapton, Keith Moon, Tina Turner, and Roger Daltrey. There are amazing costumes by his wife, Shirley Russell, including Biba clothes on their daughter Victoria. This musical odyssey is filled with the amazing Who soundtrack.”

“Liza Minnelli's talent and effervescent personality brought to life the dynamo Sally Bowles. With an incredible cast—divinely decadent Joel Grey, gorgeous Michael York, and Marisa Berenson—we're transported to the Kit Kat Club and 1930s Weimar Republic Germany. The songs and music are just as sensational as Bob Fosse’s choreography and direction. And Liza’s costumes, with a helping hand from Halston, are legendary. She even wears an embellished Ossie Clark for one musical number. Her green nail polish, spiky false eyelashes, and signature haircut have stayed with me forever. One of the greatest musical dramas of all time.”

#6640. Inside the First Competition Between Historic Vintage Timepieces

Want more insider watch coverage? Get Box + Papers, GQ's newsletter devoted to the watch world, sent to your inbox every Friday. Sign up here to get it free.

In the automotive world, high-end cars have gone head-to-head since the early 1920s. Judges didn’t evaluate them based on their speed, though—in a Concours d’Élégance, the prize goes to the best-looking vehicles. The tradition continues today in picturesque sites around the world where collectors submit their often very high-end vintage, classic cars. Surprisingly, there has never been an event for vintage timepieces.

Aurel Bacs, the famous auctioneer whose longstanding association with Phillips has shaped the watch collecting world, endeavored to change that. “It began with a meeting where I said, ‘I have a dream,’” he said in a press release. For the very first iteration of this competition, Bacs and Phillips partnered with Swiss heavyweight Vacheron Constantin. They expected to receive maybe 100 to 200 submissions, but the jury was forced to whittle down 550 of them to 34 finalist watches, whose owners were flown out from across the world for the Concours held at the Hôtel Président in Geneva.

“It was the most beautiful evening in the field ever,” Bacs said. At the Hôtel Président, around 200 attendees gathered, along with a jury of 14 watch-industry experts including Bacs himself, Ben Clymer of Hodinkee, writer and historian Nick Foulkes, longtime watch journalist Roberta Naas, and others. Watches could only be submitted for consideration in one category (Chronographs, Métiers d’Art, Chronomètre Royal, Astronomical Complications, Multiple Complications, Design, and Chiming Mechanisms) and were judged based on nine criteria: authenticity, elegance, rarity, impact on the history of Vacheron Constantin and/or on watchmaking in general, provenance, technicality, métiers d’art (artistic crafts), state of preservation, and emotional dimension.

The jurors deliberated over several hours until the winners of the seven categories and a Best of Show were finally selected. Following a cocktail reception, everyone gathered on the lower floor of the Hôtel Président to see the results from the watch world’s first Concours d'Élégance.

The first category featured a smattering of some of the maison’s most important wristwatches, including a highly rare ref. 4072 in stainless steel from 1940. However, it was a yellow gold pocket-watch chronograph from 1929 that won the day. The result was such a surprise that the audience, intimately familiar with the historical importance of pocket watches, audibly gasped inside the room. It made me wonder if these old-fashioned timepieces could regain their lost position as the pinnacle of watch collecting. This piece certainly warranted the love it received: in excellent condition and featuring a white enamel dial with dual registers in an “up-down” configuration, it’s a beautiful timepiece.

This category celebrates handcrafts such as enameling and gem-setting, and it featured a smattering of truly spectacular pieces—mostly sub-40mm wristwatches in precious metals with wild cloisonné or Grand Feu champlevé enamel dials depicting landmasses or animals.

However, a stunning pocket watch set with blue sapphires throughout the bezel and Roman indices won out.

When an older American gentleman stepped on stage to collect his prize, he explained to a stunned crowd that not only had his great-grandfather, Dr. Otto von Frangeš, been given the watch by His Majesty Alexander I of Yugoslavia, its original owner, but that he wore it while sitting in the motorcade during the king’s assassination in France in 1934. Otto’s middle son, Nikola, eventually smuggled the watch out of Yugoslavia by sewing it into the lining of his wife’s jacket as they fled the Iron Curtain in the late 1940s.

In 1907, Vacheron launched the Chronomètre Royal sub-brand, specifically built to endure a life sailing the seas. The model’s parts were anti-magnetic and resisted rusting. The dials were made out of enamel, which could also stand up to the moist atmosphere without rusting. Because they were critical to maritime navigation, timing precision was of the utmost importance, and models from this line frequently won observatory trials (which tested for a watch’s accuracy) that ran from the 1800s to the mid-1900s. Given all that information, the winner is a bit of a shocker: a ref. 2215 from 1976 that exemplifies the qualities of the line but modernizes them in many ways. It doesn’t even feature a full set of numerals, trading them out for a few diamond-set indices. In his acceptance speech, the owner said he wears it on the weekends with jeans.

The Astronomical Complications category is dear to the heart of anyone who appreciates a good moonphase display and also includes timepieces equipped with a triple-calendar, a complete calendar, or a perpetual calendar. Though more modern pieces, such as a skeletonized QP wristwatch from 1987, made it to the final round, it was a wildly neat (and unique) platinum-cased “Cioccolatone” from 1957 that won out. Housed in a 34mm x 43mm rectangular case and equipped with a complete calendar, it’s owned by a dapper Italian collector, who beamed as he collected the prize with his grown son. This would not be this pair’s first time at the podium…

This is a loaded category for Vacheron, which famously makes the most complicated pocket watch and wristwatch in the world, with 57 and 41 complications, respectively. To be eligible in this category, watches were required to have at least two complications—child’s play for VC.

Spanning everything from an enamel-dialed, minute-repeating, split-seconds chronograph pocket watch from 1901 to several Patrimony watches from the 1990s, the winner was the “Don Pancho.” Delivered in 1940, this diminutive yellow-gold timepiece with minute repeater, day, and retrograde date indications was once the world’s most complicated wristwatch. Double-signed by Brooking in Madrid, it sold for roughly $740,000 at auction back in 2019.

A more subjective category, perhaps, than the others, Design included watches “featuring Vacheron Constantin’s signature design, either in the case or through the time display.” One entrant, for example—a wild time-only ref. 4695 dress watch—featured incredibly intricate lugs and a Maltese Cross-type dial. But the winner was nothing short of revelatory. Once again owned by the aforementioned tall Italian gentleman, the possibly unique ref. 6213 is an automatic world timer from 1957 in yellow gold. Based upon Louis Cottier’s famous system, it is so rare that even Clymer admitted he had never heard of it before the competition. The owner’s son described his father returning home with it one day when he was a boy, excited beyond belief that he had finally tracked it down after years of searching.

This category included pocket watches or wristwatches fitted with a minute repeater, quarter repeater, half-quarter repeater, or grand sonnerie—a mechanism that automatically strikes the hours and quarter-hours every 15 minutes. Incredibly, among the entrants was a red gold quarter-repeater pocket watch from the early 1820s with a stunning guilloché dial. However, the winner was another pocket watch dating to 1926 and cased in platinum. Its ultra-thin design, black Breguet numerals, Breguet blued steel handset, and simple silver dial belied the complex mechanism within. The visibly emotional American gentleman who owns it recalled how, as a boy, he would open a chest of drawers, remove his grandfather’s pocket watch, and listen to its minute repeater.

A bonus category, Best of Show went to a truly spectacular wristwatch: a ref. 6448 in platinum with diamond-set indices. Seemingly a simple time-only dress watch, this tremendous piece—which was produced in just three examples in the early 1960s—boasts a hand-wound, 13-ligne minute-repeater movement. Housed in a brushed-finish case with incredible teardrop lugs, it’s the only known example in this metal, and it was notably the last minute-repeating reference produced by the brand for nearly 30 years.

Nominally, the crowd at the awards ceremony was there to judge the merits of a watch, but I left emotionally moved by the stories each watch owner shared about their timepieces. As the very first concours for watches, the format will no doubt be repeated in the future—either by Vacheron, adopted by other brands, or done as a more traditional multi-brand event like the Concours d’Élégance at Pebble Beach.

#6638. Beyoncé Just Revealed Where 9-Year-Old Rumi Carter Loves to Shop

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Beyoncé gave fans an early holiday gift this year: a rare new interview, this time with i-D magazine. While she kept the majority of her comments focused on her hair care line Cécred, she did offer a little insight into her youngest daughter Rumi Carter’s sense of style at age 9, as well as her two favorite places to shop.

The outlet asked about Beyoncé’s House of Deréon archive. After reflecting on revisiting the pieces for the 20th anniversary rerelease of her album B’Day, the singer revealed that her daughters Blue Ivy and Rumi would be inheriting all her clothes.

“I’m so excited to pass all my archives to my kids, especially now that they’re all discovering their own personal style,” she said. “I’m going to enjoy seeing how they pull from their parents’ carpets and tours if they’re ever interested. Rumi’s favorite place to shop right now is Ross and TJ Maxx these days.”

Beyoncé also looked back at her wedding day when discussing her favorite piece that her mother, Tina Knowles, ever made for her. “My favorite dress my mother designed was my wedding dress—it was gorgeously simple and made with her love,” Beyoncé explained. “We had a very small wedding in our home [in April 2008]. We told the guests we were having a party, and no one knew they were coming to our wedding but our parents and a few friends. Wearing a dress she designed was very important to me, and more important than any designer dress.”

Knowles has long been impressed with her granddaughter Rumi’s style, too. She praised it during an interview with Oprah Daily in July 2022.

“Rumi loves fashion. She picks out her own clothes,” Knowles said. “We recently went to Disneyland, and she put on her Mickey Mouse shorts. Then she wanted to put on a sparkly printed top which had the same colors. It actually looked really cute.” The moment impressed Knowles, who noted Rumi was just 5 at the time.

#6637. This Historic Napa Valley Estate Features 18th-Century Charm, Fig Trees, and a Luxe Spa

Preserving the past while readying the property for a new chapter was the goal.

At Vine & Fig, each moment takes on a bit of everyday grandeur, spurred on by the sun-drenched warmth and crisp air of Napa County. The aptly named estate, set among vineyards, olive groves, and fig trees in St. Helena, California, has always had the makings of a particularly good gathering place; it just needed a little help catching up with the times.

Enter designer Jaime Rummerfield, who spent four years transforming the 19th-century property into a 10,500-square-foot retreat that feels both comfortable and reverential to its past. The undertaking was hardly a straightforward process. Between fires, floods, and the delicate logistics of preserving the estate’s historic architecture and geothermal streams, Rummerfield had to tackle the renovation with equal parts imagination and restraint. “You cannot approach a place like this as a blank canvas,” she explains. “Understanding what is already there becomes part of the design narrative.”

Fortunately, she had a willing creative partner in homeowner Olivia Marciano, whose background as artistic director of the Marciano Art Foundation brought an especially discerning perspective to the process. The two spent hours considering materials, palettes, and custom details, often allowing a single piece to inform the direction of an entire room. The resulting interiors are richly layered yet approachably livable: antique-style cabinetry with exposed hinges, hand-crafted furnishings, expressive stone, and artwork that lends the rooms a sense of collected history. “Everything shouldn’t feel as though it arrived on the same truck,” Rummerfield says.

Given that this is Napa Valley, it goes without saying that the perks of the property extend well beyond its walls. A chef’s kitchen opens onto the surrounding gardens, while the historic pool house was reimagined as an immersive hammam, complete with Moroccan mosaics, Taj Mahal stone, and copper hot and cold plunge tubs. But for Rummerfield, the property’s greatest luxury isn’t any single amenity, but the new chapter unfolding around it. “There are vineyards, olive groves, gardens, food being prepared, and people coming together,” she says. "The interiors are the stage and participate in all of that.” And at Vine & Fig, there's always room for one more act.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Jaime RummerfieldBuilder: JMA Uncommon BuildersArchitect: 4C Design GroupLandscape Architect: Surfacedesign, Inc Location: St. Helena, CaliforniaThe Space: A six-bedroom, ten-bathroom home across 10,500 square feet

Designed to accommodate everything from quiet family dinners to spirited parties, the kitchen embraces the property’s farm-to-table ethos. Antique-style cabinetry with exposed butt hinges lends the space a sense of history, while a white oak island, stone backsplash, and French trestle table keep things grounded. Beyond the windows, views of the mountains and gardens reinforce the connection between the home and its surroundings.

“We wanted the practical decisions and the decorative decisions to support one another,” adds Rummerfield. “The cabinetry details, hidden technology, ample storage, textiles, and connection to the outdoors keep it feeling like a room in a home. It is a chef’s delight, but it is also a very welcoming heart of the house.”

“We wanted this multi-use kitchen to have a personality appropriate to its setting while still feeling connected to the wider property, hammam, and pool,” says Rummerfield of this bonus cook space. “The proportions and material relationships become especially important. It ended up being one of our favorite spaces—the rich chocolate zellige tile back splash really made it unique and divine.”

Few amenities feel more appropriate to a Napa Valley estate than a dedicated wine cave. Here, Rummerfield let the architecture and natural materials establish the atmosphere, incorporating honed black soapstone countertops to create a space that feels equally suited to storing prized bottles and sharing a favorite vintage with friends.

Rummerfield bypassed a rigid furniture arrangement in favor of several intimate seating areas, creating a room that feels equally suited to lively conversation and afternoon lounging. “I wanted the living room to have a sense of occasion while remaining completely comfortable,” she says.

To that end, a generously proportioned, low-slung sectional offers a particularly inviting place to land, while custom cabinetry conceals modern technology, including a drop-down projector screen, without interrupting the room’s collected aesthetic.

“Passageways are wonderful places to create a moment of discovery,” Rummerfield says of the dramatic entryway and curved staircase, which features the client’s art colleciton. “I want these spaces to feel as personal, stylish and welcoming as the rooms they connect to.”

An arched ceiling and soft lighting sets the tone for the journey into the property’s reimagined pool house, where Rummerfield wanted to create “a completely different experience” within the property. “The hammam is about slowing down and enjoying the beauty of the materials,” she adds. “The barrel ceiling, Moroccan mosaic, and Taj Mahal stone give the space a wonderful sense of intimacy and ritual.”

“Powder rooms offer a lovely opportunity for personalization; the relationship between the vanity, sink, mirror, and light can give the room a distinct personality,” says Rummerfield. In here, she sources a Turkish sink from Olive Ateliers, incorporated a 1940’s glass sconce, inlaid an antique mirror, and brought the terra cotta paver floors from outside in. “Each element is deliberately chosen and work beautifully together,” she adds.

Affectionately dubbed the “Strawberry Room” after the pattern on the BDDW chair, this bedroom offers comfort and relaxation in space. Rummerfield leaned into the room’s cozy proportions with a tactile Phillip Jeffries wallcovering, layered fabrics, and sculptural lighting by Ken Fulk for Urban Electric.

“I like a bathroom to feel considered as a room in its own right,” says Rummerfield. “The vanity, mirror, lighting, and tub should have a pleasing relationship, with the materials doing much of the decorative work. The atmosphere should feel quiet and indulgent.”

“I enjoy giving utility spaces some personality,” Rummerfield says of the laundry room, which she coated in Benjamin Moore’s Spring Valley, a fresh green that nods to the surrounding landscape. “Many people spend time in them, so the light, finishes, and everyday experience deserve attention.”

Jaime Rummerfield is a Los Angeles–based interior designer known for her richly layered spaces that balance timeless elegance with a distinct sense of personality. Her work celebrates craftsmanship, architectural character, and the art of collecting, combining vintage finds, custom furnishings, and expressive materials to create interiors that feel both sophisticated and lived-in.
